&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\mathrm{exp}\left(-\pi r\frac{\sqrt{V_{0}}}{\omega^{2}}\frac{\left(\sin^{2}t+\gamma^{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\cos(2 t)}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\pi^{3}}{36}\frac{\omega^{2}}{\sqrt{V_{0}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\cos(2 t)}}+\cdots\right)\,,\end{split}

(1.3.32)

where we have introduced Keldysh’s adiabaticity parameter
[
198
]
:

\displaystyle\gamma:=\frac{m\omega}{\sqrt{V_{0}}}.

(1.3.33)

Note that the first correction in Eq. (
1.3.32
) is positive and also reflects the large-potential character of our expansion, which is not in powers of
\gamma
, but in terms of the dimensionless, small parameter
\omega^{2}/\sqrt{V_{0}}
. For extreme fields, one could work out the time integral using Laplace’s theory, which effectively leads to a parabolic approximation. Indeed, physically speaking, the expression (
1.3.32
) can be seen as an improvement over simply approximating the harmonic function with a periodic array of quadratic potentials centered around its minima.
Additionally, in the small
\omega
limit the quadratic result is reobtained; this can be seen either by using Laplace’s theory in the previous equation or by rescaling in
\omega
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\Gamma_{\rm M}}{L_{0}\operatorname{Vol_{\rm D-1}}}&\overset{\phantom{\omega\to 0}}{=}\frac{\pi\omega}{4}\,\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{r+1}\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int^{\pi/4\omega}_{-\pi/4\omega}{\rm d}t\left(\frac{\sqrt{V_{0}\cos(2\omega t)}}{4\pi^{2}r}\right)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{-\pi r\left(\frac{V_{0}}{\omega^{2}}\sin^{2}(\omega t)+m^{2}\right)}{\sqrt{V_{0}\cos(2\omega t)}}\right)\\
&\overset{\omega\to 0}{=}\frac{\pi\omega}{4}\,\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{r+1}\left(\frac{v}{4\pi^{2}r}\right)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{rv}}\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{-\pi rm^{2}}{v}\right),\end{split}

(1.3.34)

where we have identified
\sqrt{V_{0}}\to v
.
Although we have not managed to obtain a closed analytic formula for Eq. (
1.3.32
), one can truncate the series at any desired order and readily numerically perform the integrations, at least for massive fields, for which a decreasing exponential behaviour is guaranteed.

As a last comment, we would like to emphasize once more that we expect Eq. (
1.3.32
) to be a good approximation for small frequency
\omega
. If an all-scalar version of the worldline instantons technique could be developed, it could be used to check all these (and the following section’s) results and complement them with expressions encompassing all the derivative contributions (but just the first large mass one).

1.3.1.3
Spatial particle creation

The framework is slightly more involved when considering the spatial case. For simplicity, we will analyze the case depending on just one variable, say
x_{3}
, so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma_{\rm M}&=-\frac{{\rm i}\operatorname{Vol_{\rm D-1}}}{2\sqrt{2}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{{\rm d}T}{T}\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\rm d}x_{3}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-T\left(V(x_{3})+m^{2}\right)}}{\left(4\pi T\right)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}}\,\frac{\sqrt{V^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})}T}{\sinh\left(\sqrt{V^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})}T/\sqrt{2}\right)}.\ \end{split}

(1.3.35)

In this setup, for weak fields instabilities arise when
\partial^{2}_{3}V
becomes negative; the difficulty resides in the fact that the instabilities are already present at the level of the Euclidean effective action and, thus, the imaginary proper time prescription described in the previous section is required in order to obtain a sensible answer.

To simplify the discussion further, let us consider the quadratic case

\displaystyle V(x_{3})=v^{2}\,x_{3}^{2}+m^{2},

(1.3.36)

leaving the sign of
v^{2}
for the moment undetermined; note that the situation with the inverted quadratic potential mimics what happens in the electromagnetic homogeneous field in the spatial gauge.
Then, following the lines of the previous section we arrive at

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{M}}[V]}{\operatorname{Vol}_{\rm D-1}}=-\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}T}{T}\,\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}{\rm d}x_{3}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-T(v^{2}{x}_{3}^{2}+m^{2})}}{\left(4\pi T\right)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}}\,\frac{vT}{\sinh(v T)}\ .

(1.3.37)

At this point, we have to consider the two possible alternatives separately. If
v^{2}>0
, all the integrals in Eq. (
1.3.37
) are well defined and are real, i.e. there is no creation of pairs. This is of course logical: since the potential is static, no energetic source is available to create the pairs. If instead
v^{2}<0
, the sinh in the denominator becomes a sine and singularities appear.
However, the integral in
x_{3}
becomes ill-defined; this can be seen as a consequence of the fact that the effective mass
V+m^{2}
is negative in an infinite region.
A way to circumvent this problem in this simple case is to perform first the integral in
x_{3}
(with
v^{2}>0
) and afterwards appeal to an analytical continuation in
10
10

10

One should employ the rule of thumb that the rotation should not interfere with the
{\rm i}\epsilon
Feynman rule.

v
. The result obtained with this prescription agrees with the one using the imaginary proper time, and gives the same pair creation probability as for the quadratic time dependence. This agreement resembles the situation in the
EM
homogeneous case.

As a last example, consider another spatial, now oscillating background,

\displaystyle V(x_{3})=\frac{V_{0}}{\omega^{2}}\sin^{2}(\omega x_{3}).

(1.3.38)

If
V_{0}>0
, then the only change with respect to the time-dependent field is in the integration region over
x_{3}
, which is effectively shifted to the complementary interval in the period of
\cos(2\omega x_3)
;
11
11

11

In this case,
\rm Vol_{D}=\mathit{L}_{3}Vol_{D-1}
, where
L_{3}
denotes a spatial cutoff.
after shifting the integral one gets:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\Gamma_{\rm M}}{\operatorname{Vol_{\rm D}}}=&\,\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{r+1}\pi}{4}\\
&\times\int^{\pi/4}_{-\pi/4}{\rm d}x_{3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{V_{0}\cos(2x_3)}}{4\pi^{2}r}\right)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{-\pi r\left(\frac{\sqrt{V_{0}}}{\omega^{2}}\cos^{2}(x_{3})+\frac{m^{2}}{\sqrt{V_{0}}}\right)}{\sqrt{\cos(2x_3)}}\right)\,.\end{split}

(1.3.39)

Since our expansion is valid for strong potentials, the integral in the coordinate
x_{3}
can be done using Laplace’s theory; contrary to the time-dependent scenario, one notices that
\cos^{2}x_{3}\geq 1/2
in the integration region, and thus the integral is exponentially suppressed in the strong field expansion. This is consistent with the fact that for a positive potential one does not expect particle creation to take place. This can actually be discussed in more general terms: whenever the potential is strictly positive and its second derivative is negative, an exponential suppression is present in the imaginary part of the effective action in the
LQFA
.

Taking into account our discussion for the quadratic case, one would expect that the harmonic potential (
1.3.38
) with
V_{0}<0
should resemble the most an electromagnetic space-dependent field. However, in this scenario there is a caveat: for strong (negative) fields the integral in Eq. (
1.3.35
) develops infrared (
IR
) singularities. These are related to the divergent behaviour for large
T
and could imply a further source of imaginary contributions in the effective action; they are of course not present for
QED
, since gauge invariance precludes the presence of the gauge potential in the effective action. One could still appeal for example to analytical continuations to analyze the
IR
singularities, as done for the quadratic field; however, for the oscillatory potential the integrals can not all be done explicitly and the same can be said for the most frequently analyzed pulses in the literature.

For the sake of completeness, let us briefly discuss an analogue expansion in
QED
; a thorough analysis will be left for the future. In such a case, the first derivative contribution, i.e. the equivalent of the
\omega^{2}/\sqrt{V_{0}}
term in Eq. (
1.3.32
), is given by the
c_{3}^{\rm QED}
coefficient. For a background field which is exclusively electric (
F_{0i}=E_{i}(x)
and
F_{ij}=0
), it can be shown that

\displaystyle c_{3}^{\rm QED}=\frac{2}{5}\left[\partial_{j}E_{i}\partial^{j}E^{i}-\partial_{0}E_{i}\partial^{0}E^{i}\right],

(1.3.40)

cf. the
\bar{o}_{3}
coefficient in Ref.
[
212
]
.
This expression clearly displays a sign flip when shuffling from purely spatial to time-dependent potentials, meaning that the pair production probability is enhanced in the latter case, while it decreases for the former. This seems to be in agreement with the threshold which has been observed using worldline instantons for potentials depending on spatial coordinates
[
132
]
.

1.3.2
First and higher orders in
\mathcal{V}

Before analyzing the first-order contribution in
\epsilon
, we will sketch how the computation to any order can be done.
At
n
th order in the assisting potential, the contribution to the generating functional is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Z_{n}[\eta](\bar{x};T)=(-1)^{n}\oint\mathcal{D}s\,\int_{t_{1}t_{2}\cdots}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{t}\frac{\dot{s}^{2}}{4}+s^{\mu}s^{\nu}\Omega_{\mu\nu}+s^{\mu}\eta_{\mu}}\,\mathcal{V}(\bar{x}+s(t_{1}))\mathcal{V}(\bar{x}+s(t_{2}))\cdots\\
&=(-1)^{n}\int\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\hat{\rm d}q_{j}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(q_{1})\right]\int_{t_{1}\cdots}\,\oint\mathcal{D}s\,\mathrm{e}^{-i\sum_{l=1}^{n}q_{l}\cdot\bar{x}-\int_{t}\frac{\dot{s}^{2}}{4}+s^{\mu}s^{\nu}\Omega_{\mu\nu}+s^{\mu}\eta_{\mu}+is_{\mu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}^{\mu}\delta(\tau-t_{i})}\,\ ,\end{split}

(1.3.41)

where we have Fourier transformed
\mathcal{V}
as

\mathcal{V}(x)=\int\hat{\rm d}q\,\mathrm{e}^{-iq\cdot x}\,\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(q)\

(1.3.42)

and used the short-hand notation
\hat{\rm d}q:=\frac{{\rm d}^{D}q}{(2\pi)^{D}}
.
The expression (
1.3.41
) is still manageable, since introducing explicitly the classical solution to the equations of motion of the worldline,

s^{\mu}\to s^{\mu}_{\mathrm{cl}}+\hat{s}^{\mu}\ ,

(1.3.43)

the exponent becomes quadratic in the quantum fluctuations.
A direct calculation shows that we need to find the solution of

(\Delta_{\rm SI})_{\mu\nu}\,s_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\nu}(\tau)=-\eta_{\mu}(\tau)-\mathfrak{d}_{\mu}(\tau)\ ,

(1.3.44)

where
\mathfrak{d}
contains all the momentum inhomogeneities:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{\mu}(\tau):=i\sum_{i=1}^{n}q^{\mu}_{i}\delta(\tau-t_{i})\ .

(1.3.45)

This can be simply solved by considering the Green function of the operator,
\mathcal{G}:=\Delta_{\rm SI}^{-1}
, which is computed in App.
1.A
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\mu\nu}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left[-\frac{1}{2T\Omega^{2}}+\frac{\cosh{(\Omega(T+2t-2t'))}}{2\Omega\sinh{(\Omega T)}}-\Theta(t-t^{\prime})\,\frac{\sinh{(2\Omega(t-t'))}}{\Omega}\right]^{\mu\nu}\!\!,

(1.3.46)

so that

\displaystyle s^{\mu}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=-i\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{G}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(\tau,t_{i})q^{\nu}_{i}-\int_{t^{\prime}}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mu\nu}(\tau,t^{\prime})\eta_{\nu}(t^{\prime})\ .

(1.3.47)

After replacing Eq. (
1.3.47
) in the expression for
Z_{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle Z_{n}[\eta](\bar{x};T)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\int\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\hat{\rm d}q_{j}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(q_{1})\right]\,\mathrm{e}^{-i\sum_{l=1}^{n}q_{l}\cdot\bar{x}}\int_{t_{1}t_{2}\cdots}\oint\mathcal{D}s\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{tt^{\prime}}\hat{s}^{\mu}\Delta_{\mu\nu}\hat{s}^{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{tt^{\prime}}\left(\eta+\mathfrak{d}\right)_{\mu}\mathcal{G}^{\mu\nu}\left(\eta+\mathfrak{d}\right)_{\nu}}\,

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-TV}}{\left(4\pi T\right)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}}\,\mathrm{det}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\left(\frac{\Omega^{2}T^{2}}{\sinh^{2}(\Omega T)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\int\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\hat{\rm d}q_{j}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(q_{1})\right]\,\mathrm{e}^{-i\sum_{l=1}^{n}q_{l}\cdot\bar{x}}\int_{t_{1}t_{2}\cdots}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{tt^{\prime}}\left(\eta+\mathfrak{d}\right)_{\mu}\mathcal{G}^{\mu\nu}\left(\eta+\mathfrak{d}\right)_{\nu}}\ .

(1.3.48)

If we are just interested in the contribution without worldline interactions, this formula can be further simplified to

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{n}[\eta](\bar{x};T)&=(-1)^{n}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-TV}}{\left(4\pi T\right)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}}\,\mathrm{det}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\left(\frac{\Omega^{2}T^{2}}{\sinh^{2}(\Omega T)}\right)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\times\int\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\hat{\rm d}q_{j}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(q_{1})\right]\,\mathrm{e}^{-i\sum_{l=1}^{n}q_{l}\cdot\bar{x}}\int_{t_{1}t_{2}\cdots}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{tt^{\prime}}\mathfrak{d}_{\mu}\mathcal{G}^{\mu\nu}\mathfrak{d}_{\nu}}\ ,\end{split}

(1.3.49)

given that the
SI
Green function satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{t^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}^{\mu\nu}(t,t^{\prime})\partial_{\mu}V(\bar{x})=0\,\ .

(1.3.50)

It is important to note that, in Eq. (
1.3.49
), an integral in
\bar{x}
is not simply going to give a Dirac delta, which would imply momentum conservation, because of our nonperturbative approach. Indeed, there are also implicit dependences on
\bar{x}
through
V
and
\Omega
; the former will be a crucial difference with respect to the Abelian gauge field case.

Let us now focus on the first order contribution in the assisting potential.
The relevant computation reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{1}[\eta](\bar{x};T)=&-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-TV}}{\left(4\pi T\right)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}}\,\mathrm{det}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\left(\frac{\Omega^{2}T^{2}}{\sinh^{2}(\Omega T)}\right)\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt\times\int\hat{\rm d}q\,\mathrm{e}^{-iq\cdot\bar{x}-\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu}\mathcal{E}^{\mu\nu}q_{\nu}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(q)\int_{t_{1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}S_{\mathrm{bos}}[\eta]+iq\int_{t}\mathcal{G}(t,t_{1})\,\eta(t)}\ ,\end{split}

(1.3.51)

with

\mathcal{E}^{\mu\nu}:=\mathcal{G}^{\mu\nu}(t,t)=\left[\frac{\Omega T\coth{(\Omega T)}-1}{2\Omega^{2}T}\right]^{\mu\nu}\ .

(1.3.52)

Once these results are replaced in Eq. (
1.3.8
), we can perturbatively recast the trace of the heat kernel as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{K}(T)=-\frac{1}{\left(4\pi T\right)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}}&\int{\rm d}^{D}\bar{x}\;\mathrm{e}^{-TV}\,\mathrm{det}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\left(\frac{\Omega^{2}T^{2}}{\sinh^{2}(\Omega T)}\right)\\
\times&\int\hat{\rm d}q\,\mathrm{e}^{-iq\cdot\bar{x}-\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu}\mathcal{E}^{\mu\nu}q_{\nu}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(q)\int_{t_{1}}\,\big(1+\Sigma^{(1)}(\bar{x};T,t_{1})\big)\ ,\end{split}

(1.3.53)

where higher-derivatives contributions in the Yukawa potential are encoded in
\Sigma^{(1)}(\bar{x};T,t_{1})
.
If we split them according to the number of momenta they contain,

\Sigma^{(1)}(\bar{x};T,t_{1})=\Sigma^{(1)}_{0}(\bar{x};T)+\Sigma^{(1)}_{1}(\bar{x};T,t_{1})+\cdots\ ,

(1.3.54)

we get the first terms

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(1)}_{0}(\bar{x};T)

\displaystyle=-\frac{T}{8}\partial_{\mu\nu\rho\lambda}V\,\mathcal{E}^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{E}^{\rho\lambda}-\frac{T}{48}\partial_{\mu\nu\rho\lambda\tau\theta}V\,\mathcal{E}^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{E}^{\rho\lambda}\mathcal{E}^{\tau\theta}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{T}{384}\partial_{\mu\nu\rho\lambda\tau\theta\alpha\beta}V\,\mathcal{E}^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{E}^{\rho\lambda}\mathcal{E}^{\tau\theta}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{1}{12}\partial_{\mu\nu\rho}V\partial_{\alpha\beta\gamma}V\,\int_{ab}\mathcal{G}^{\mu\alpha}(a,b)\mathcal{G}^{\nu\beta}(a,b)\mathcal{G}^{\rho\gamma}(a,b)+\dots\ ,

(1.3.55)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Sigma^{(1)}_{1}(\bar{x};T,t_{1})&=\frac{i}{2}\,\partial_{\mu\nu\rho}V\,q_{\alpha}\int_{t}\mathcal{G}^{\mu\nu}(t,t)\mathcal{G}^{\rho\alpha}(t,t_{1})\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{i}{6}\,\partial_{\mu\nu\rho\lambda\tau}V\,q_{\alpha}\int_{t}\mathcal{G}^{\mu\nu}(t,t)G^{\rho\lambda}(t,t)\mathcal{G}^{\tau\alpha}(t,t_{1})+\dots\ ,\end{split}

(1.3.56)

where we reported only the terms contributing to order
T^{5}
once expanded in powers of
T
.
These results can be compared with the known expansion for integrated heat kernels, the difference with the local results in Sec.
1.2
consisting just in boundary contributions.

1.3.2.1
Gauss assisted pair creation at first order

The results from the previous section can be readily used to analyze the effect of assisted pair creation.
For simplicity, let us consider a minimal model in which the background, assisted field is quadratic in the coordinates, so that the integrated heat kernel simplifies to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{K}(T)&=-\frac{T}{\left(4\pi T\right)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}}\int{\rm d}^{D}\bar{x}\;\mathrm{e}^{-TV}\,\mathrm{det}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\left(\frac{\Omega^{2}T^{2}}{\sinh^{2}(\Omega T)}\right)\int\hat{\rm d}q\,\mathrm{e}^{-iq\cdot\bar{x}-\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu}\mathcal{E}^{\mu\nu}q_{\nu}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(q)\ .\end{split}

(1.3.57)

Without losing generality, we can further restrict the potential to be only time-dependent, as we have done in Eq. (
1.3.20
):

V_{0}(x)=v^{2}\,x_{0}^{2}+m^{2}\ ,\quad v=\text{const}\ .

(1.3.58)

As assisting field we propose instead a Gaussian (infinitely wide) pulse, which is frequently employed in the description of experimental setups:

\mathcal{V}(x)=\frac{\omega^{2}}{\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}x_{0}^{2}}\ .

(1.3.59)

Replacing these profiles for the fields into Eq. (
1.3.57
) and performing the integral in
\bar{x}_{0}
and in the energy
q_{0}
, we get a compact expression for the trace of the heat kernel:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{K}(T)&=-\frac{\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathrm{(D-1)}}}{(4\pi T)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}}\frac{\omega T^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}{\sinh{\left( vT \right)}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}T}\,\left(\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}+\frac{\coth{(vT)}}{v}\right)^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\ .\end{split}

(1.3.60)

Using this expression, the Euclidean effective action is immediately obtained. As we have already performed at zeroth order in the assisting field, in the present case we can analyze the Minkowskian scenario by performing a Wick rotation, the only difference being that the frequency
\omega
shall be also included among the quantities that have to be Wick-rotated.
Keeping track once more of the necessary regularizations resulting from the rotation, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma_{\mathrm{M}}^{(1)}&=-\frac{\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathrm{(D-1)}}\epsilon\omega_{\mathrm{M}}}{2(4\pi)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}T}{T^{\nicefrac{{(D-1)}}{{2}}}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}T}}{\sin{\left( v_\mathrm{M} T + i 0\right)}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}+\frac{\cot{( v_\mathrm{M}T)}}{v_{\mathrm{M}}}-i0\right)^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}.\end{split}

(1.3.61)

The analytic structure of the integrand in this expression is severely influenced by the square root factor. On the one hand, notice that the zeros of the sine are not poles of the integrand, given that the cotangent inside the square root partially compensates the divergence, rendering it integrable. On the other hand, its argument vanishes periodically in
T
, generating an infinite number of branch cuts in the complex
T
plane, the
-i0
resulting from the Wick rotation telling us on which side of the cut the integral shall be performed.
To simplify the discussion, let us use
v_{\mathrm{M}}
as scale and define the following complete set of dimensionless parameters:

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}:

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{\rm M}}{v_{\mathrm{M}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}\ ,

(1.3.62)

\displaystyle\bar{m}:

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{v_{\mathrm{M}}}\ ,

(1.3.63)

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{*}:

\displaystyle=v_{\mathrm{M}}T^{*}\ ,

(1.3.64)

where
T^{*}
is the first positive root of
\bar{\omega}^{-2}+\cot{( \bar{T}^*)}=0
. Then, the cuts from the cotangent give rise to an imaginary part in the effective action, whose analytic expression can be straightforwardly found to be

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{\mathrm{M}}^{(1)}&=\frac{\pi\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathrm{(D-1)}}}{2(2\pi)^{{D}{}}}{v_{\mathrm{M}}}^{\nicefrac{{(D-1)}}{{2}}}\epsilon\mathrm{e}^{-\bar{m}^{2}\bar{T}^{*}}\bar{\omega}\int_{0}^{\pi-\bar{T}^{*}}{\rm d}T\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\bar{m}^{2}T}}{\sin{\left( T + \bar{T}^* \right)}}\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\,\left|\bar{\omega}^{-2}+\cot(T+\bar{T}^*)\right|^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Phi\left(-\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\bar{m}^{2}},\frac{D-1}{2},\frac{T+\bar{T}^{*}}{\pi}\right),\end{split}

(1.3.65)

where
\Phi(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
is the Lerch transcendent function,

\displaystyle\Phi(z,s,a):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{(a+n)^{s}}.

(1.3.66)

Importantly,
T^{*}
always belongs to the interval
T^{*}\in[\pi/2,\pi)
, since we consider
v_{\mathrm{M}},\omega_{\mathrm{M}}>0
; this implies that the exponential decay of the pair creation effect can be greatly softened by the assisted field.
Such a softening can be readily observed by inspecting the ratio of the pair creation probability at first assisted order,
P^{(1)}
, with respect to the non-assisted case,
P^{(0)}
. In the left panel of Fig.
1.1
, one can observe a density plot of
\log\left(\frac{P^{(1)}}{P^{(0)}}\right)
as a function of
\bar{m}
and
\bar{\omega}
; the integral in the proper time
T
has been performed numerically, while the value
\epsilon=10^{-3}
has been chosen so that in the depicted region we roughly satisfy the small potential criterium
\epsilon\bar{\omega}^{2}\ll 1
.

As expected, the ratio of probabilities rapidly increases as a function of the rescaled frequency
\bar{\omega}
.
Indeed, the higher the frequency
\omega
the larger the available energy to catalyze the production of pair creation. One can also see that, for the depicted values of the parameters, the ratio also increases with the mass; the reason is that the transseries structure in Eq. (
1.3.65
) is shifted with respect to that in Eq. (
1.3.25
), thanks to the exponential prefactor
{\mathrm{e}}^{-\bar{m}^{2}\bar{T}^{*}}
.

Figure 1.1:
The left panel is a density plot of
\log\left(\frac{P^{(1)}}{P^{(0)}}\right)
as a function of
\bar{m}
and
\bar{\omega}
, taking
\epsilon=10^{-3}
and
D=4
. The right panel shows
\frac{P^{(1)}}{P^{(0)}}
as a function of
\bar{\omega}
, for three different values of the dimensionless mass:
\bar{m}=2
(yellow, dotted line),
\bar{m}=1
(blue, dashed line) and
\bar{m}=0.5
(red, continuous line).

We have also depicted the double logarithmic plot of the ratio of pair creation assisted and non-assisted probabilities as a function of
\bar{\omega}
in the right panel of Fig.
1.1
, for a few values of the rescaled mass. For large values of
\bar{\omega}
, the behaviour is linear, as dictated by the prefactor in Eq. (
1.3.65
). Going to smaller values, there is a transition to a
\bar{\omega}^{2}
behaviour. For small
\bar{\omega}
the hierarchy of the curves with respect to the mass is kept, thanks to the following effect: in this region the softening
{\mathrm{e}}^{-\bar{m}^{2}\bar{T}^{*}}
plays no role, the Lerch function becomes essentially a polylogarithm and the relevant ratio is governed by the dimensionality of the two polylogarithms.

1.4
Final remarks

In this chapter, we have shown how the Worldline Formalism can be used to perform nonperturbative computations for a scalar quantum field coupled to a Yukawa background. In particular, using Dirichlet boundary conditions on the worldline, we have seen that a local, resummed expression for the heat kernel is obtainable, cf. Sec.
1.2
. In this way one can straightforwardly compute the first generalized heat kernel coefficients, which agree with those recently found in the literature. Instead, in Sec.
1.3
we have developed what would be combined Parker–Toms and Barvinsky–Vilkovisky resummations, the former being used for the strong field background and the latter for the assisting field. Since the ultimate goal was to obtain the pair production probability, the so-called string-inspired boundary conditions proved to be more efficient. From the master formula (
1.3.8
), an expansion of the integrated heat kernel to include higher derivative terms for arbitrary fields can be obtained by direct replacement.
This enables one to compute the imaginary part of the Minkowskian effective action and, ergo, the instabilities of the vacuum in terms of the probability of pair creation. The advantage of our method over other available techniques (such as the worldline instantons
[
132
]
) is that our method provides a formula that can be computed for arbitrary, inhomogeneous fields to any desired level of precision. Using these results, we have seen that the assistance occurs already at the first order in the assisting field. To illustrate this effect, we have worked out in detail the calculations for a Gaussian assisting field; compared to the non-assisted effect, the transseries structure of the pair creation probability is translated, leaving room for a large exponential enhancement. On the contrary, in
sQED
one has to resort to at least second order in order to observe the assisted effect
[
241
]
, because gauge invariance precludes a term like
\mathrm{e}^{-TV}
in Eq. (
1.3.57
).

Our analysis can be generalized in several directions. First, contributing to the discussion on a possible new effect of pair creation in the presence of gravitational fields

[
150
,
188
,
15
,
78
]
. Using the curved spacetime version of the Wordline Formalism, such a situation is tractable.
In this scenario, we would also expect the possibility to have a first order assisted pair creation, if a mixed of strong/fastly-varying potentials is used. In this sense, our computations are of great help, since at least in a perturbative approach, the computation in a curved spacetime is totally analogous to a scalar computation
[
245
]
.
Second, the interaction of fields with higher spins is also accessible in the worldline; the corresponding resummed expressions could already be inferred from our present results.
Finally, understanding whether further resummations of derivatives are possible would be a further step in the analysis of nonperturbative phenomena, in particular related to the transseries structure of the corresponding physical effects.

Appendices

Appendix 1.A
Boundary conditions and Green functions in the worldline

In this appendix, we will discuss some of the technicalities mentioned throughout the chapter regarding boundary conditions; first we will consider the case of the free operator, i.e. the pure kinetic one, and will compute the corresponding Green functions. Afterwards, we will extend the results to the Yukawa case.

1.A.1
Kinetic operator and zero mode

The kinetic term of the worldline theory that we study corresponds to the free worldline action

S_{\mathrm{kin}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}\,\delta_{\mu\nu}\,\dot{x}^{\mu}(t)\dot{x}^{\nu}(t)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{1}t_{2}}\,x^{\mu}(t_{1})\left[\delta_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}_{t_{1}}\delta(t_{1}-t_{2})\right]x^{\nu}(t_{2})\ ,

(1.A.1)

where the boundary terms arising from the integration by parts vanish both for Dirichlet and periodic
BC
.
In particular, we are interested in the case where the trajectories are periodic, since we will see that the vanishing
DBC
can be reobtained as a special case.

The associated differential operator,

K_{\mu\nu}(t,t^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}_{t}\delta_{\mathrm{P}}(t-t^{\prime}),

(1.A.2)

is defined on the interval
t,t^{\prime}\in[0,T]
and has a zero mode on the circle: periodic boundary conditions allow for the presence of constant paths, preventing thus
K_{\mu\nu}
to be invertible. Note that
\delta_{\mathrm{P}}(t-t^{\prime})
is the Dirac delta function on the space of periodic functions on
[0,T]

[
49
]
; the subscript has been dropped throughout the work for notational convenience.

One way to solve the invertibility issue consists of factoring out the zero mode, i.e. to decompose the generic periodic path into the zero mode
\bar{x}^{\mu}
and a quantum fluctuation
y^{\mu}(t)
, so that

x^{\mu}(t)=\bar{x}^{\mu}+y^{\mu}(t)\ .

(1.A.3)

This amounts to introducing a redundant (pure gauge) field
\bar{x}^{\mu}
in the worldline theory; indeed, there is now a shift symmetry, which is actually a gauge symmetry:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta\bar{x}^{\mu}&=\xi^{\mu}\ ,\\
\delta y^{\mu}(t)&=-\xi^{\mu}\ .\end{split}

(1.A.4)

Differently to what happens in the background field method
[
3
]
, here both fields are considered dynamical.
12
12

12

That is, they are integrated over in the path integral and the overcounting of physically distinct configurations must be avoided by a gauge-fixing procedure.
This explains why the shift symmetry (
1.A.4
) is promoted to a
gauge symmetry
, and therefore must be gauge-fixed. The gauge-fixing procedure can be performed by means of BRST methods, as analyzed in Refs.
[
50
,
24
,
104
,
147
]
within the Worldline Formalism. The outcome of the discussion is that one can introduce a gauge condition of the form

\int_{t}\,\rho(t)\,y(t)=0\ ,

(1.A.5)

where
\rho(t)
is usually called the
background charge
(in analogy with electrostatics) and is normalized to

\int_{t}\,\rho(t)=1\ .

(1.A.6)

The gauge-fixing (
1.A.5
) allows to invert the free kinetic operator of the fluctuations
y^{\mu}
. It is clear that different choices of background charge will result, in general, in different boundary conditions for the quantum fluctuations and propagators. Nevertheless, the
\rho
-independence of the path integral is still guaranteed by the BRST symmetry.
13
13

13

To be more precise, the worldline partition function can be expressed as an integral over the zero mode

Z=\int{\rm d}^{D}\bar{x}\,z^{(\rho)}(\bar{x},\rho)\ ,

where the partition function density
z^{(\rho)}
may depend on the gauge-fixing choice
\rho(t)
, but this can only happen through total derivatives, which must then integrate to zero
[
50
]
.

More precisely, the Green function
\mathfrak{G}(t,t^{\prime})
of the operator
-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{t}
acting on fields constrained by the equation (
1.A.5
) depends on
\rho(t)
and satisfies

{-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\,\mathfrak{G}(t,t^{\prime})=\delta(t-t^{\prime})-\rho(t)}\ .

(1.A.7)

Let us discuss the most prominent choices for the background charge for the free theory.

•

DBC
- The choice

\rho(t)=\delta(t)

(1.A.8)

is tantamount to factorizing out the zero mode as in (
1.A.3
) where
\bar{x}^{\mu}
is identified with a base-point in the target space along the loop. Indeed, such a background charge produces, from (
1.A.5
), vanishing Dirichlet boundary conditions for the fluctuations:

y(0)=y(T)=0\ .

(1.A.9)

In this scenario, i.e. when working in the space of functions with vanishing
DBC
on the interval
[0,T]
, the Dirac delta function is actually
\delta(t-t^{\prime})
and vanishes at the boundaries
t,t^{\prime}=\{0,T\}

[
49
]
. The delta function will still be denoted as usual, since no confusion should arise. The free propagator is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}G^{(f)}(t,t^{\prime})&=-|t-t^{\prime}|+\left(t+t^{\prime}\right)-\frac{2tt^{\prime}}{T}\ ,\end{split}

(1.A.10)

which can be obtained by solving

-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\tau}^{2}G^{(f)}(t,t^{\prime})=\delta(t-t^{\prime})\ ,

(1.A.11)

with boundary conditions

G^{(f)}(0,t^{\prime})=G^{(f)}(T,t^{\prime})=0\ .

(1.A.12)

•

SI
- The choice

\rho(\tau)=\frac{1}{T}

(1.A.13)

gives rise to the so-called “string-inspired” boundary conditions, as the factorization (
1.A.3
) of the zero mode is akin to the customary practice in string theory, namely to identify
\bar{x}^{\mu}
with the “center of mass” of the worldline (
1.3.3
) and separate it from the quantum fluctuations. The resulting boundary conditions for the latter are

\int_{t}\,y(t)=0\ ,

(1.A.14)

while the free propagator is given by

\mathcal{G}^{(f)}(t,t^{\prime})=-|t-t^{\prime}|+\frac{(t-t^{\prime})^{2}}{T}+\frac{T}{6}\ .

(1.A.15)

It satisfies the equation

-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{G}^{(f)}(t,t^{\prime})=\delta(t-t^{\prime})-\frac{1}{T}\ ,

(1.A.16)

with the conditions

\displaystyle\int_{t}\,\mathcal{G}^{(f)}(t,t^{\prime})=0\ ,\quad\mathcal{G}^{(f)}(0,t^{\prime})=\mathcal{G}^{(f)}(T,t^{\prime})\ .

(1.A.17)

Note that
\mathcal{G}^{(f)}(t,t^{\prime})
actually depends only on
t-t^{\prime}
, while
G^{(f)}(t,t^{\prime})
is a true function of two variables.

1.A.2
Green function with a Yukawa coupling

Let us now consider quadratic interactions, namely the linear, one-dimensional differential operator

\Delta_{\mu\nu}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}_{t_{1}}\delta(t_{1}-t_{2})+2\,\Omega^{2}_{\mu\nu}(y)\delta(t_{1}-t_{2})\ .

(1.A.18)

The corresponding Green functions are described in the following (we omit Lorentz indices throughout the calculations).

•

DBC
- We start with the computation of Sec.
1.2
, namely with the case of vanishing
DBC
for the fluctuations
\hat{s}
, cf. Eq. (
1.2.13
). The Green function
G^{\mu\nu}(t,t^{\prime}):=\Delta^{-1}_{\mu\nu}
is defined by the following differential equation,

\Delta(t,t^{\prime})\,G(t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})=\delta(t-t^{\prime\prime})\ ,

(1.A.19)

together with the boundary conditions

G(0,t^{\prime})=G(T,t^{\prime})=0\ .

(1.A.20)

We can build an Ansatz starting from the solution of the homogeneous equation associated with (
1.A.19
), namely

\displaystyle G(t,t^{\prime})=\begin{cases}A(t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{2\Omega t}+B(t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\Omega t}\quad\text{if}\quad t<t^{\prime}\\
C(t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{2\Omega t}+D(t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\Omega t}\quad\text{if}\quad t^{\prime}\leq t\end{cases}\ .

(1.A.21)

Imposing the usual conditions on the (dis)continuity of the (first derivative of) the Green function at
t=t^{\prime}
, we can relate the four unknown functions
A,B,C,D
to get

G(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{\sinh(2\Omega t)\sinh\big(2\Omega(T-t')\big)-\Theta(t-t^{\prime})\sinh(2\Omega T)\sinh\big(2\Omega(t-t')\big)}{\Omega\sinh(2\Omega T)}\ ,

(1.A.22)

where one should not forget that the Green function carries Lorentz indices
G^{\mu\nu}(t,t^{\prime})
.
Note that, in the case of vanishing strong field (
\Omega\to 0
), the Green function reduces to the free propagator (
1.A.10
).

•

SI
- Let us move on to the computation of Sec.
1.3
, where string-inspired boundary conditions are enforced for the fluctuations
\hat{s}
. The Green function
\mathcal{G}^{\mu\nu}(t,t^{\prime})
associated with the linear, one-dimensional differential operator
\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\rm SI}(t)
is defined by the differential equation

\Delta_{\rm SI}(t,t^{\prime})\,\mathcal{G}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})=\delta(t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime})-\frac{1}{T}\ ,

(1.A.23)

and must satisfy the following conditions:

\displaystyle\int_{t}\,\mathcal{G}(t,t^{\prime})=0\ ,\quad\mathcal{G}(0,t^{\prime})=\mathcal{G}(T,t^{\prime})\ .

(1.A.24)

The final result for the Green function reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{G}(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2\Omega}\bigg[&\frac{1}{\sinh({\Omega T})}\bigg(-\frac{\sinh(\Omega T)}{\Omega T}+\cosh\Big( \Omega(T+2t-2t' ) \Big)\bigg)\\
&-\Theta(t-t^{\prime})\,2\,\sinh\Big({2\Omega(t-t')} \Big)\bigg]\ .\end{split}

(1.A.25)

As already mentioned, the
SI
propagator depends only on the difference of its two arguments: for instance, note that the coincidence limit reads

\mathcal{G}(t,t)=\frac{\Omega T\coth{(\Omega T)}-1}{2\Omega^{2}T}\ .

(1.A.26)

In the case of vanishing assisted field (
\Omega\to 0
) the Green function reduces to the free propagator (
1.A.15
), as expected.

Appendix 1.B
Computation of the functional determinants and generalized Gel’fand–Yaglom theorem

The Gel’fand–Yaglom theorem
[
171
]
and its extensions
[
199
,
200
,
201
]
have been extensively applied in the context of the Worldline Formalism
[
132
,
118
,
119
]
. Let us recall the main statement of the theorem: given a one-dimensional operator defined on an interval
z\in[0,T]
with vanishing Dirichlet boundary conditions

\left[-\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}z^{2}}+V(z)\right]\psi(z)=\lambda\,\psi(z)\ ,\quad\text{with}\quad\psi(0)=\psi(T)=0\ ,

(1.B.1)

there is no need to explicitly know its eigenvalues (not even one of them) if one desires to compute its functional determinant
[
134
]
. The only required information is the boundary value of the unique solution to the initial value problem

\left[-\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}z^{2}}+V(z)\right]\Phi(z)=0\ ,\quad\text{with}\quad\Phi(0)=0\ ,\quad\dot{\Phi}(0)=1\ ,

(1.B.2)

which satisfies

\mathrm{Det}\left[-\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}z^{2}}+V(z)\right]\propto\Phi(T)\ .

(1.B.3)

The result (
1.B.3
) can be extended to more general boundary conditions and for higher-dimensional differential operators. Indeed, consider a family of Sturm–Liouville type operators of the form

L_{i}=-\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}z}\left(P_{i}(z)\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}z}\right)\,\mathbb{1}_{r\times r}+V_{i}(z)

(1.B.4)

where
\mathbb{1}_{r\times r}
is the
r\times r
identity matrix and
V_{i}
can also be matrix-valued. Then, the ratio of the functional determinants of the operator
L_{1}
relative to that of another operator
L_{2}
of the same type can be expressed in terms of four
2r\times 2r
matrices
M
,
N
and
Y_{i}
, which are built as follows
[
200
]
.
The matrices
Y_{i}
contain the
2r
solutions
\mathbf{u}^{(j)}(z)
of the eigenvalue problem
14
14

14

We omit a subscript
i
in the eigenfunctions for readability reasons.

L_{i}\mathbf{u}^{(j)}=\lambda\mathbf{u}^{(j)}
with
\lambda\to 0
and their weighted first derivatives

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{(j)}(z):=P_{i}(z)\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}z}\mathbf{u}^{(j)}(z)\ ;

(1.B.5)

using these vectors as (semi)-column entries, we have

Y_{i}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbf{u}^{(1)}&\dots&\mathbf{u}^{(2r)}\\
\mathbf{v}^{(1)}&\dots&\mathbf{v}^{(2r)}\\
\end{array}\right)\ .

(1.B.6)

For simplicity, one generally chooses the initial conditions as
15
15

15

For instance, in the case
r=1
we have two one-dimensional solutions
u^{1,2}(z)
with first derivatives
v^{1,2}(z)
, whose initial conditions read
u^{(1)}(0)=1\,,v^{(1)}(0)=0,u^{(2)}(0)=0
and
v^{(2)}(0)=1
.

Y(0)=\mathbb{1}_{2r\times 2r}\ .

(1.B.7)

On the other hand, the matrices
M
and
N
are fixed by the choice of the boundary conditions; in fact, the latter can be recast in full generality as

M\,Y(0)+N\,Y(T)=0\ .

(1.B.8)

As an example, in the case of vanishing
DBC
these matrices reduce to

M_{\rm D}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{1}_{r\times r}&\mathbb{0}_{r\times r}\\
\mathbb{0}_{r\times r}&\mathbb{0}_{r\times r}\\
\end{array}\right)\ ,\quad N_{\rm D}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{0}_{r\times r}&\mathbb{0}_{r\times r}\\
\mathbb{1}_{r\times r}&\mathbb{0}_{r\times r}\\
\end{array}\right)\ ,

(1.B.9)

while in the case of periodic boundary conditions they read

M_{\rm P}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{1}_{r\times r}&\mathbb{0}_{r\times r}\\
\mathbb{0}_{r\times r}&\mathbb{1}_{r\times r}\\
\end{array}\right)\ ,\quad N_{\rm P}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\mathbb{1}_{r\times r}&\mathbb{0}_{r\times r}\\
\mathbb{0}_{r\times r}&-\mathbb{1}_{r\times r}\\
\end{array}\right)\ .

(1.B.10)

Employing this notation, the generalized
GY
theorem states that the ratio of the functional determinants satisfies

\frac{\mathrm{Det}(L_{1})}{\mathrm{Det}(L_{2})}=\frac{\mathrm{det}(M+N\,Y_{1}(T))}{\mathrm{det}(M+N\,Y_{2}(T))}\ .

(1.B.11)

Let us now show how to use this formalism for the interacting differential operator
\Delta_{\mu\nu}
defined in Eq. (
1.2.8
).

1.B.1
Dirichlet boundary conditions

For
DBC
, relevant to Sec.
1.2
, the formula (
1.B.11
) reduces to the evaluation of the determinant of an
r\times r
solutions-submatrix:

\mathrm{Det}(\Delta)=\mathrm{det}(\mathbf{u}^{(r+1)}\,\dots\,\mathbf{u}^{(2r)})\ .

(1.B.12)

Thus, we don’t need to know the whole set of
2r
solutions of the homogeneous equation; instead, we only need the
r=D
solutions
\varphi_{\mu}^{(\rho)}
which have initial conditions

\varphi_{\mu}^{(\rho)}(0)=\mathbb{0}\ ,\quad\dot{\varphi}_{\mu}^{(\rho)}(0)=\delta^{\rho}_{\mu}\ .

(1.B.13)

Building from the one-dimensional solution, an Ansatz of the form

\varphi_{\mu}^{(\rho)}(z)=\left(A\,\mathrm{e}^{2\Omega z}\right)^{\rho}{}_{\mu}+\left(B\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\Omega z}\right)^{\rho}{}_{\mu}\ ,

(1.B.14)

one obtains

\varphi_{\mu}^{(\rho)}(z)=\left[\left.\frac{1}{2\Omega}\sinh(2\Omega z)\right]^{\rho}{}\right._{\mu}\ .

(1.B.15)

Using as a reference operator the free kinetic operator
L_{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}_{z}
, whose
Y
-matrix can be computed effortlessly, the final result for the functional determinant reads

{\overline{\mathrm{Det}}(\Delta)=\mathrm{det}\left[\frac{\sinh(2\Omega T)}{2\Omega T}\right]}\ .

(1.B.16)

As a double check of this result, we can calculate the determinant as a
\zeta
-regularized infinite product of the eigenvalues of the operator
\Delta
divided by those of the free operator. Using a simple Fourier expansion to solve the eigenvalue equation, we get

\overline{\mathrm{Det}}(\Delta)=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\prod\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[1+\left(\frac{2\Omega^{(j)}T}{\pi n}\right)^{2}\right]=\mathrm{det}\left[\frac{\sinh{(2\Omega T)}}{2\Omega T}\right]\ ,

(1.B.17)

where
\Omega^{(j)}
are the eigenvalues of the matrix
\Omega^{\mu}{}_{\nu}
and we have used the well-known result

\prod\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1+\frac{x^{2}}{\pi^{2}n^{2}}\right)=\frac{\sinh{(x)}}{x}\ .

(1.B.18)

Note in particular that the free limit is regular:

\lim_{\Omega\to 0}\,\overline{\mathrm{Det}}(\Delta)=1\ .

(1.B.19)

1.B.2
Periodic boundary conditions

The
PBC
, relevant to Sec.
1.3
, require more carefulness; let us first show the exemplificative non-matricial case, i.e.
r=1
.

Proposition 1
.

Assuming Eq. (
1.B.10
) for the matrices
M
and
N
, and considering an arbitrary operator of the form given in Eq. (
1.B.4
), the relevant determinant for the
GY
formula in the one-dimensional (
r=1
) case reduces to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{det}\Big(M+N\,Y_{1}(T)\Big)&=2-\mathrm{tr}\left(Y_{r=1}(T)\right)=2-2\cosh(2\Omega T)\ .\end{split}

(1.B.20)

Proof.

In general, a direct calculation actually gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{det}\Big(M+N\,Y_{1}(T)\Big)&=1-\left.\left(u^{(1)}+v^{(2)}\right)\right|_{z=T}+\left.(u^{(1)}v^{(2)}-v^{(1)}u^{(2)})\right|_{z=T}\\
&=1-\mathrm{tr}\left(Y_{r=1}(T)\right)+\mathrm{det}\left(Y_{r=1}(T)\right)\ ,\end{split}

(1.B.21)

with the matrix of solutions given by

Y_{r=1}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u^{(1)}(z)&u^{(2)}(z)\\
v^{(1)}(z)&v^{(2)}(z)\\
\end{array}\right)\ .

(1.B.22)

One can recognize in the
RHS
of (
1.B.21
) the Wronskian

\mathcal{W}(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})(z)=u^{(1)}(z)v^{(2)}(z)-v^{(1)}(z)u^{(2)}(z)\ ,

(1.B.23)

evaluated at
z=T
, which is associated with the two independent solutions
u^{1,2}(z)
of the eigenvalue equation for a Sturm–Liouville operator.
It is a stablished result that this type of Wronskian is constant;
therefore, extracting from the initial conditions its value at
z=0
,

\mathcal{W}(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})(0)=1\ ,

(1.B.24)

we also have that
\mathcal{W}(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})(T)=1
; hence, using the explicit solutions
u^{(1)}
and
v^{(2)}
, Eq. (
1.B.20
) follows.
∎

It is not hard to convince oneself that the previous one-dimensional result can be directly generalized to higher dimensions.

Theorem 1
.

Consider an operator of the form given in Eq. (
1.3.11
), defined on the domain of periodic functions. Be
u_{a}^{(b)}
the
a
th component of the
b
th solution of the homogeneous equation
L_{1}\mathbf{u}^{(b)}=0
and
\mathbf{v}^{(b)}
its corresponding weighted derivative, as in Eq. (
1.B.5
).
Then, the relevant determinant for the
GY
theorem satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{det}\Big(M+N\,Y_{\Delta,\mathrm{PBC}}(T)\Big)=\det\big(2-2\cosh(2\Omega T)\big)\ .

(1.B.25)

Proof.

Note first that, in contrast to the
DBC
, to compute the functional determinant using the generalized
GY
theorem we do need the whole set of
2r
solutions of the homogeneous equation

\Delta_{\rm SI}^{\mu\nu}\,\Phi_{\nu}^{(\Pi)}=0\ ,\quad\Phi^{(\Pi)}_{\mu}=\begin{cases}\phi_{\mu}^{(\rho)}\quad\text{if}\quad\Pi=1,\dots,r\\
\psi_{\mu}^{(\rho)}\quad\text{if}\quad\Pi=r+1,\dots,2r\end{cases}\ .

(1.B.26)

Considering Eq. (
1.B.7
) as initial conditions, namely

\displaystyle\phi_{\mu}^{(\rho)}(0)

\displaystyle=\delta^{\rho}_{\mu}\ ,\quad\frac{{\rm d}\phi}{{\rm d}z}_{\mu}^{(\rho)}(0)=\mathbb{0}\ ,

(1.B.27)

\displaystyle\psi_{\mu}^{(\rho)}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{0}\ ,\quad\frac{{\rm d}\psi}{{\rm d}z}_{\mu}^{(\rho)}(0)=\delta^{\rho}_{\mu}\ ,

(1.B.28)

an explicit computation gives

\phi_{\mu}^{(\rho)}(z)=\dot{\psi}_{\mu}^{(\rho)}(z)=\Big[\cosh(2\Omega z)\Big]^{\rho}{}\Big._{\mu}\ .

(1.B.29)

Note then that in arbitrary dimensions we can straightforwardly prove that the
n
th power of the matrix
Y_{\Delta}
is

\displaystyle Y^{n}_{\Delta,\mathrm{PBC}}(T)=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh(2n\Omega T)&\sinh(2n\Omega T)\\
\sinh(2n\Omega T)&\cosh(2n\Omega T)\end{pmatrix},

(1.B.30)

and consequently, from Eq. (
1.B.11
), we have the equalities

\displaystyle\begin{split}\det(\mathbb{1}_{2r} - Y_{\Delta, \mathrm{PBC}})&=\mathrm{e}^{{\textrm{tr}}\log[2-2\cosh(2\Omega T)]}=\det\big(2-2\cosh(2\Omega T)\big)\ ,\end{split}

(1.B.31)

which indeed agree with the
RHS
of Eq. (
1.B.25
).
∎

As a corollary of Th.
1
, recall that the difference between
P
and
SI

BC
resides just in the omission of the constant mode in the latter; in the free case, this mode corresponds to a zero mode. The quotient of
\Delta_{\rm SI}
with the the free operator, after extracting the zero mode of the latter (denoted with a prime in the Det), is thus given by

{\overline{\mathrm{Det}}^{\prime}(\Delta_{\rm SI})=\mathrm{det}\left[\frac{\sinh^{2}(\Omega T)}{\Omega^{2}T^{2}}\right]}\ .

(1.B.32)

This is consistent with the fact that we have omitted the constant mode, so that the free limit
\Omega\to 0
is non-vanishing. As an alternative check of the
GY
result, we can directly evaluate the determinant of
\Delta_{\rm SI}
as a product of its eigenvalues; using a Fourier expansion and dividing by the free eigenvalues (we exclude the vanishing one), we get the following
\zeta
-regularized infinite product

\overline{\mathrm{Det}}^{\prime}(\Delta_{\rm SI})=\mathrm{det}\left[\prod\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1+\frac{\Omega^{2}T^{2}}{\pi^{2}n^{2}}\right)^{2}\,\right]=\mathrm{det}\left[\frac{\sinh^{2}(\Omega T)}{\Omega^{2}T^{2}}\right]\ ,

(1.B.33)

which confirms Eq. (
1.B.32
).

Appendix 1.C
Generalized Schwinger–DeWitt coefficients in the string-inspired approach

The first generalized Schwinger–DeWitt coefficients computed using the
SI

BC
, defined in the expansion

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(1)}_{0}({x};T)=:\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c^{\mathrm{SI}}_{j}(x)\,T^{j}\ ,

(1.C.1)

are given by

\displaystyle c^{\mathrm{SI}}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(1.C.2)

\displaystyle c^{\mathrm{SI}}_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(1.C.3)

\displaystyle c^{\mathrm{SI}}_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(1.C.4)

\displaystyle c^{\mathrm{SI}}_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{288}\partial_{\mu}{}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}{}^{\nu}V\ ,

(1.C.5)

\displaystyle c^{\mathrm{SI}}_{4}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{10368}\partial_{\mu}{}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}{}^{\nu}{}_{\rho}{}^{\rho}V\ ,

(1.C.6)

\displaystyle c^{\mathrm{SI}}_{5}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{45360}\partial_{\mu\nu\rho}V\partial^{\mu\nu\rho}V+\frac{1}{4320}\partial_{\mu}{}^{\mu}{}_{\rho\lambda}V\partial^{\rho\lambda}V-\frac{1}{497664}\partial_{\mu}{}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}{}^{\nu}{}_{\rho}{}^{\rho}{}_{\sigma}{}^{\sigma}V\ .

(1.C.7)

Recall that these coefficients are only valid at the integrated level, i.e. they are related via integration by parts to the
c_{i}
coefficients in Eq. (
1.2.29
).

Spin 1

Chapter 2
Pair production of massive charged vector bosons

In this chapter, we continue our investigation of non-perturbative physics within the Worldline Formalism by considering a massive spin-1 particle interacting with an electromagnetic background. Unlike the previous chapter, here we adopt a “bottom-up” strategy and introduce a worldline model augmented by bosonic oscillators to encode the spin degrees of freedom, a framework that can accommodate particles of arbitrary integer spin. We first review how quantization in the spin-1 sector – carried out both through Dirac’s method and via BRST quantization – reproduces the free Proca field theory. We then couple the model to an external
EM
field and show that Maxwell’s equations for the background arise as the consistency condition ensuring the nilpotency of the BRST charge in the spin-1 sector.
Encouraged by this result, which establishes the viability of the particle model, we proceed to construct the path integral quantization of the worldline action for a charged spin-1 particle on the circle. This leads to the one-loop effective Lagrangian for a constant electromagnetic background induced by a massive charged vector boson. As expected, the result exhibits a vacuum instability, which we quantify by deriving the pair-production rate for the vector bosons, thereby recovering known results from quantum field theory.

Conventions

Whether we work in Minkowski or Euclidean signature, and in which spacetime dimension, will be specified as needed throughout the text for convenience.

2.1
The free bosonic spinning particle

The worldline model known as the bosonic spinning particle has already been introduced in the
introductory section on the Worldline Formalism
. In this section, we shall provide a more detailed presentation and establish our notation. It is defined by the usual set of phase-space variables
(x^{\mu},p_{\mu})
, augmented by an additional pair of complex bosonic variables
(\alpha^{\mu},\bar{\alpha}^{\mu})
. The former represent Cartesian coordinates and conjugate momenta of a relativistic particle, while the latter are needed to account for the spin degrees of freedom. Both pairs are functions of the proper time, which we take to range as
\tau\in[0,1]
. The kinetic term

S_{\mathrm{kin}}=\int\mathrm{d}\tau\left(p_{\mu}\dot{x}^{\mu}-i\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}\dot{\alpha}^{\mu}\right)

(2.1.1)

defines the phase space symplectic structure and fixes the Poisson brackets to

\{x^{\mu},p_{\nu}\}_{\mathrm{PB}}=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\;,\quad\{\alpha^{\mu},\bar{\alpha}^{\nu}\}_{\mathrm{PB}}=i\eta^{\mu\nu}\;.

(2.1.2)

As it stands, the model is not unitary, as upon quantization negative norm states will be generated by the
(x^{0},p^{0},\alpha^{0},\bar{\alpha}^{0})
variables. Moreover, the model, as we shall discuss, contains particle excitations of any integer spin, and one needs to eliminate some further degrees of freedom to describe a single particle with fixed spin. Both problems can be addressed by gauging suitable constraints: the gauged worldline action we are interested in is given by

S=\int\mathrm{d}\tau\;\left[p_{\mu}\dot{x}^{\mu}-i\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}\dot{\alpha}^{\mu}-eH-\bar{u}L-u\bar{L}-aJ\right]\;,

(2.1.3)

where we introduced the worldline gauge multiplet
(e,\bar{u},u,a)
acting as a set of Lagrange multipliers
that enforce the constraints that, in the massless case, read

\displaystyle H=\frac{1}{2}p^{\mu}p_{\mu}\;,\quad L=\alpha^{\mu}p_{\mu}\;,\quad\bar{L}=\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}p_{\mu}\;,\quad J=\alpha^{\mu}\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}\;.

(2.1.4)

The latter satisfy a first-class Poisson-bracket algebra:

\{L,\bar{L}\}_{\mathrm{PB}}=2iH\;,\quad\{J,L\}_{\mathrm{PB}}=-iL\;,\quad\{J,\bar{L}\}_{\mathrm{PB}}=i\bar{L}\;.

(2.1.5)

The phase-space functions (
2.1.4
) play rather different roles.

•

The role of
(H,L,\bar{L})
constraints is to remove the negative-norm states and
must
be gauged to make the model consistent with unitarity. The Hamiltonian constraint
H
corresponds to the mass-shell condition for massless particles, and generates
\tau
-reparametrization in phase space, while the remaining pair,
L
and
\bar{L}
, generates “bosonic” supersymmetries.
1
1

1

We deliberately use this terminology since (
2.1.5
) is formally similar to a
SUSY
algebra, except for the fact that
L
and
\bar{L}
are bosonic rather than fermionic.

•

The
J
constraint is a
U(1)
generator which rotates the bosonic oscillators by a phase; its gauging is optional as far as unitarity is concerned. However, upon quantization, it projects the Hilbert space onto the physical
subspace with a specific occupation number, describing the degrees of freedom of a particle with maximal spin
s
. For this to happen, one must add a Chern–Simons term on the worldline
with the Chern–Simons (
CS
) coupling fine-tuned according to the value of the spin
s
one wants to achieve.
This approach has been discussed extensively
in Ref.
[
35
,
33
]
.

To make explicit the gauge symmetries enjoyed by (
2.1.3
), one has to compute the action of the constraints on generic phase-space functions
F
via the Poisson brackets:
\delta F=\{F,V\}_{\mathrm{PB}}
. Considering the linear combination of the constraints
V=\epsilon H+\bar{\xi}L+\xi\bar{L}+\phi J
with gauge parameters
(\epsilon,\bar{\xi},\xi,\phi)
, the corresponding transformations are

\displaystyle\delta x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\epsilon p^{\mu}+\xi\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}+\bar{\xi}\alpha^{\mu}\;,

(2.1.6a)

\displaystyle\delta p_{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\;,

(2.1.6b)

\displaystyle\delta\alpha^{\mu}

\displaystyle=i\xi p^{\mu}+i\phi\alpha^{\mu}\;,

(2.1.6c)

\displaystyle\delta\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=-i\bar{\xi}p^{\mu}-i\phi\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}\;.

(2.1.6d)

For the action (
2.1.3
) to be invariant, the gauge fields must transform as follows

\displaystyle\delta e

\displaystyle=\dot{\epsilon}+2iu\bar{\xi}-2i\bar{u}\xi\;,

(2.1.7a)

\displaystyle\delta u

\displaystyle=\dot{\xi}-ia\xi+i\phi u\;,

(2.1.7b)

\displaystyle\delta\bar{u}

\displaystyle=\dot{\bar{\xi}}+ia\bar{\xi}-i\phi\bar{u}\;,

(2.1.7c)

\displaystyle\delta a

\displaystyle=\dot{\phi}\;.

(2.1.7d)

The need for the worldline constraints to enforce unitarity remains somewhat obscure in this setup.
To review and clarify this point, it may be beneficial to perform a brief lightcone analysis.

2.1.1
Lightcone analysis

Despite the loss of manifest covariance, a lightcone analysis allows for a direct calculation of the number of propagating physical degrees of freedom. It is a well-known method, employed in many worldline models, see e.g.
[
234
,
36
,
38
]
. We define lightcone coordinates
x^{\pm}
in
D
spacetime dimensions by

x^{\mu}=(x^{+},x^{-},x^{a})\;,\quad\text{with}\quad x^{\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(x^{0}\pm x^{D-1})\;,

(2.1.8)

where
x^{a=1,\dots,D-2}
are the transverse directions. The line element reads
\mathrm{d}s^{2}=-2\mathrm{d}x^{+}\mathrm{d}x^{-}+\mathrm{d}x^{a}\mathrm{d}x^{a}
; for any vector
V^{\mu}
,
V^{+}=-V_{-}
and
V^{-}=-V_{+}
.

The guiding idea behind the lightcone analysis is to remove negative-norm states by implementing a gauge-fixing that isolates the physical degrees of freedom, which in turn leads to a well-defined Hilbert space upon quantization. To do that, let us first assume motion with
p^{+}\neq 0
and consider the Hamiltonian constraint

H=\frac{1}{2}p^{\mu}p_{\mu}=-p^{+}p^{-}+\frac{1}{2}p^{a}p^{a}=0\;.

(2.1.9)

It generates time translations, see (
2.1.6a
). These symmetries
are gauge-fixed by imposing the lightcone gauge

x^{+}=\tau\;.

(2.1.10)

Correspondingly, the Hamiltonian constraint is solved for the momentum
p^{-}
, conjugate to
x^{+}
,

p^{-}=\frac{1}{2p^{+}}p^{a}p^{a}\;.

(2.1.11)

At this point, the remaining independent phase-space variables are
(x^{-},p^{+})
and
(x^{a},p^{a})
. The lightcone gauge has the flavor of nonrelativistic mechanics, but the model is fully relativistic. A Hilbert space can be constructed by quantizing these independent variables.
On top of these variables, there are also the relativistic oscillators, which may also lead to negative norms.
That this does not happen (the so-called no-ghost theorem) is again made explicit by completing the lightcone gauge
fixing.
The gauge symmetries generated by
L
and
\bar{L}
, see Eqs.
(
2.1.6c
) and (
2.1.6d
), are fixed by setting

\alpha^{+}=0\;,\quad\bar{\alpha}^{+}=0\;,

(2.1.12)

while the constraints
L=\bar{L}=0
are solved explicitly by expressing the
variables conjugate to (
2.1.12
) in terms of the remaining independent variables

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}^{-}=\frac{1}{p^{+}}\bar{\alpha}^{a}p_{a}\;,\quad\alpha^{-}=\frac{1}{p^{+}}\alpha^{a}p_{a}\;.

(2.1.13)

The conjugated pairs
(\bar{\alpha}^{-},\alpha^{+})
and
(\bar{\alpha}^{+},\alpha^{-})
are thus eliminated as independent phase-space coordinates, highlighting the fact that the only independent physical oscillators are the transverse ones
(\bar{\alpha}^{a},\alpha^{a})
. They produce states with positive norm upon quantization, as can be inferred by promoting their Poisson brackets to commutation relations

[\bar{\alpha}^{a},\alpha^{b}]=\delta^{ab}\;,

(2.1.14)

which are realized on a Fock space, where
\alpha^{a}
act as creation operators while
\bar{\alpha}^{a}
as destruction operators,
thus yielding a unitary spectrum of massless particles that decompose into irreps of the little group
SO(D-2)
.

To conclude this section, we report the (partially) gauge-fixed worldline Lagrangian

\mathcal{L}=p_{-}\dot{x}^{-}+p_{a}\dot{x}^{a}-\frac{1}{2p^{+}}p^{a}p^{a}-i\bar{\alpha}_{a}\dot{\alpha}^{a}-a\,\bar{\alpha}_{a}\alpha^{a}\;.

(2.1.15)

At this stage, it only remains to address the further constraint related to the worldline gauge field
a(\tau)
, but this has no relevance to the no-ghost theorem.

2.1.2
Mass from dimensional reduction

In this chapter, our main interest is to describe
massive
spinning particles. One way to introduce the mass consists
of the dimensional reduction of a higher-dimensional massless theory. We take the theory (
2.1.3
) to live in
(D+1)
-dimensions and gauge the direction
x^{D}
by imposing the first-class constraint

p_{D}=m\;,

(2.1.16)

with
m
the mass of the particle. We further define
(\beta,\bar{\beta})\coloneqq(\alpha^{D},\bar{\alpha}^{D})
, which inherit the following Poisson brackets

\{\beta,\bar{\beta}\}_{\mathrm{PB}}=i\;.

(2.1.17)

The constraints (
2.1.4
) get modified by the presence of the mass:
2
2

2

From now on, we take spacetime indices to run as
\mu=0,\dots,D-1
where
D
denotes the number of spacetime dimensions.

\displaystyle H=\frac{1}{2}(p^{\mu}p_{\mu}+m^{2})\;,\quad L=\alpha^{\mu}p_{\mu}+\beta m\;,\quad\bar{L}=\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}p_{\mu}+\bar{\beta}m\;,\quad J_{c}=\alpha^{\mu}\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}+\beta\bar{\beta}-c\;,

(2.1.18)

where we redefined the
U(1)
constraint as
J_{c}=J-c
for future convenience. The constant
c

is sometimes called Chern–Simons coupling. Note that, importantly, they still satisfy the first-class algebra (
2.1.5
) despite the mass improvement. The gauge transformations (
2.1.6
) are enriched by

\displaystyle\delta\beta

\displaystyle=i\xi m+i\phi\beta\;,

(2.1.19a)

\displaystyle\delta\bar{\beta}

\displaystyle=-i\bar{\xi}m-i\phi\bar{\beta}\;.

(2.1.19b)

Lightcone analysis

The lightcone gauge is implemented just as in the massless case:
in particular, the bosonic supersymmetries are used to fix
\alpha^{+}=\bar{\alpha}^{+}=0
once again, and the constraints
L=\bar{L}=0
are solved by

\displaystyle\alpha^{-}=\frac{1}{p^{+}}\left(\alpha^{a}p_{a}+m\beta\right)\;,\quad\bar{\alpha}^{-}=\frac{1}{p^{+}}\left(\bar{\alpha}^{a}p_{a}+m\bar{\beta}\right)\;,

(2.1.20)

thus eliminating the longitudinal oscillators.
Differently from the massless case, the presence of the extra pair of
\beta
-oscillators produces a sum of irreps
3
3

3

This can be explicitly seen by implementing the
J_{c}
constraint
à la
Dirac
[
36
]
.
of the
SO(D-2)
group that fill irreps of the
SO(D-1)
rotation group, corresponding to the polarizations of massive spin particles in
D
spacetime dimensions.

2.1.3
Dirac quantization

Upon covariant quantization, the worldline coordinates obey the following commutation relations fixed by their classical Poisson brackets

\displaystyle[x^{\mu},p_{\nu}]=i\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\;,\quad[\bar{\alpha}^{\mu},\alpha^{\nu}]=\eta^{\mu\nu}\;,\quad[\bar{\beta},\beta]=1\;,

(2.1.21)

and the first-class algebra becomes

[\bar{L},L]=2H\;,\quad[J_{c},L]=L\;,\quad[J_{c},\bar{L}]=-\bar{L}\;.

(2.1.22)

Note that ordering ambiguities emerge only for the constraint
J_{c}
.
We have defined the quantum
J_{c}
operator by a symmetric quantization prescription, so that

\displaystyle J_{c}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{\mu}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}+\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}\alpha_{\mu}+\beta\bar{\beta}+\bar{\beta}\beta)-c\;,

(2.1.23)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mu}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}+\beta\bar{\beta}+\frac{D+1}{2}-c\;,

(2.1.24)

\displaystyle=N_{\alpha}+N_{\beta}-s\;,

(2.1.25)

where we have used the commutation relations and introduced the usual number operators

N_{\alpha}=\alpha_{\mu}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}\;,\quad N_{\beta}=\beta\bar{\beta}\;,

(2.1.26)

and related the
CS
coupling
c
to the real number
s
by setting

c=\frac{D+1}{2}+s\;.

(2.1.27)

The algebra (
2.1.22
) is now easily obtained.
At this point, it is worth mentioning that the relation between the
CS
coupling
c
and the physical value of the spin
s
generally depends on the quantization scheme adopted.

The Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{matter}}
of the “matter” sector, i.e. the one associated with the
(x,p)
coordinates and
(\alpha,\bar{\alpha},\beta,\bar{\beta})
oscillators, is realized as a tensor product of the representations of the algebras (
2.1.21
)

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{matter}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{M}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{(\alpha,\beta)}\;.

(2.1.28)

Specifically, we represent it by identifying the states in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{M}}
as the smooth functions of
x^{\mu}
, while we construct
\mathcal{H}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
as the Fock space with vacuum defined by

(\bar{\alpha}^{\mu},\bar{\beta})\,\ket{0}=0\;.

(2.1.29)

The decomposition of a generic state
\ket{\varphi}
is thus written in terms of coefficients corresponding to rank-
s
symmetric tensors:

\ket{\varphi}=\sum_{r,p=0}^{\infty}\ket{\varphi^{(r,p)}}=\sum_{r,p=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r!p!}\,\varphi^{(r,p)}_{\mu_{1}...\mu_{r}}(x)\otimes\alpha^{\mu_{1}}\dots\alpha^{\mu_{r}}\beta^{p}\ket{0}\;.

(2.1.30)

The quantization may proceed either following a procedure
à la
Dirac or by using BRST techniques; either way, we can deal with the gauge symmetries without abandoning manifest covariance, obtaining at last a positive-definite physical Hilbert space. We start with the former method, leaving the BRST analysis for the dedicated section.

The physical Hilbert space in the Dirac (also known as Dirac–Gupta–Bleuler) scheme is determined by asking the constraints to have null matrix elements for arbitrary physical states

|\varphi\rangle
and
|\chi\rangle

\braket{\chi|(H,L,\bar{L},J_{c})|\varphi}=0\;.

(2.1.31)

This can be satisfied by requiring

H\ket{\varphi}=\bar{L}\ket{\varphi}=J_{c}\ket{\varphi}=0

(2.1.32)

for any physical state
|\varphi\rangle
,
since then also
\langle\varphi|\bar{L}=0
,
as
\bar{L}
is the hermitian conjugate of
L
. Recalling now that at the quantum level

J_{c}=N_{\alpha}+N_{\beta}-s

(2.1.33)

where the number operators
N_{\alpha}
and
N_{\beta}

count the occupation number of the
\alpha
and
\beta
oscillators
in the Fock space, we see
that the quantum
J_{c}
constraint selects precisely states with occupation number
s
, which must be
a nonnegative integer.
Incidentally, we notice that the
CS
coupling must be quantized to get a nontrivial solution of the constraint, and therefore a nontrivial quantum theory.
The upshot is that the quantum
J_{c}
constraint
reduces the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{matter}}
to the subspace with occupation number
s
for the oscillators.
The condition (
2.1.32
) defines physical states which are seen to form equivalence classes

\ket{\varphi}\sim\ket{\varphi}+\ket{\varphi_{\rm null}}\;,

(2.1.34)

where
\ket{\varphi_{\rm null}}
is a null state of the form

\displaystyle\ket{\varphi_{\rm null}}=L\ket{\xi}\;,\qquad\text{with}\quad H\ket{\xi}=\bar{L}\ket{\xi}=(J_{c}+1)\ket{\xi}=0\;.

(2.1.35)

These null states are physical, but have zero norm and vanishing overlap with any other physical state.
They give rise to redundancies or residual “gauge symmetries” of the state
\ket{\varphi}
.

Let us make the conditions for the case
s=1
, which is of interest to us, explicit.
A generic state at occupation number
s=1
is given by

\ket{\psi}=W_{\mu}(x)\alpha^{\mu}\ket{0}-i\varphi(x)\beta\ket{0}

(2.1.36)

and the physicality conditions (
2.1.32
) translate into the following set of equations, denoting
\Box:=\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
,

\displaystyle(\Box-m^{2})W_{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\;,

(2.1.37)

\displaystyle(\Box-m^{2})\varphi

\displaystyle=0\;,

(2.1.38)

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}W_{\mu}+m\varphi

\displaystyle=0\;,

(2.1.39)

with gauge symmetries related to null states given by

\displaystyle\delta W_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\xi\;,\quad\delta\varphi=-m\xi\;.

(2.1.40)

Using the gauge symmetry to set
\varphi(x)=0
, we recover the standard Fierz–Pauli equations for a massive spin-1 field
W_{\mu}(x)
.

2.1.4
Counting degrees of freedom

In this section, we aim to use the path integral to count the number of degrees of freedom propagated by the massive model for different values of the
CS
coupling.
As a byproduct, this will provide the overall normalization of the effective action we intend to study in later sections.

To count the number of degrees of freedom, we consider the one-loop effective action obtained by
path integrating the free action on worldlines with the topology of a circle
S^{1}
(the loop). After fixing
the overall normalization to match the scalar case, we will get the number of degrees of freedom in the other
sectors of the worldline theory.

Thus, we consider the following path integral

\displaystyle\Gamma=\int_{S^{1}}\frac{DGDX}{\rm Vol(Gauge)}\,\mathrm{e}^{iS[X,G]}\;,

(2.1.41)

where
G=(e,\bar{u},u,a)
denotes the gauge fields, whereas
X=(x^{\mu},p_{\mu},\alpha^{\mu},\bar{\alpha}_{\mu},\beta,\bar{\beta})
collectively denotes all dynamical variables parametrizing the phase space.
The action is similar to the one in (
2.1.3
) with
the additional
(\beta,\bar{\beta})
oscillator and with
the constraints in (
2.1.18
), namely

\displaystyle S=\int\mathrm{d}\tau\biggl[

\displaystyle p_{\mu}\dot{x}^{\mu}-i\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}\dot{\alpha}^{\mu}-i\bar{\beta}\dot{\beta}-\frac{e}{2}(p^{\mu}p_{\mu}+m^{2})-\bar{u}(\alpha^{\mu}p_{\mu}+\beta m)-u(\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}p_{\mu}+\bar{\beta}m)

(2.1.42)

\displaystyle-a(\alpha^{\mu}\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}+\beta\bar{\beta}-c)\biggr]\;.

Periodic boundary conditions are understood to implement the path integral on the circle.

The overcounting from summing over gauge equivalent configurations is formally taken into account by dividing by the volume of the gauge group. We use the Faddeev–Popov (
ΦΠ
) method to extract the latter, and gauge-fix the worldline gauge fields to constant moduli

G=(e,\bar{u},u,a)\ \ \to\ \ \hat{G}=(2T,0,0,\theta)\;.

(2.1.43)

Here
T
is once again the Schwinger proper time, playing the role of the modulus related to the einbein
e(\tau)
, and corresponding to the gauge-invariant worldline length
\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\tau\,e
.
The modulus
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
is associated with the worldline
U(1)
gauge field
a(\tau)
and parametrizes the gauge invariant Wilson loop
\mathrm{e}^{-i\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\tau\,a}
.
It is responsible for the reduction of the Hilbert space to a given spin sector. On the other hand, the gauge fields
(u,\bar{u})
can be gauge-fixed to zero:
this value can always be reached by inverting the differential operator that relates these fields to their respective gauge parameters
(\xi,\bar{\xi})
, as shown in Eqs. (
2.1.7b
) and (
2.1.7c
).
This inversion fails only at the point
\theta=0
, which, however, can be handled through a limiting procedure, as in the standard

\mathcal{N}=2
particle case
[
30
,
29
]
. Therefore,
(u,\bar{u})
carry no moduli.
As a reminder, moduli generically parametrize gauge-invariant field configurations that must be integrated over in the path integral.

We prefer to work in the Euclidean version of the theory, so that we first pass to configuration space by eliminating the momenta
p_{\mu}
,
Wick rotate the action with
\tau\to-i\tau
, taking into account also the rotation of the gauge field
a\to ia
, and we get the Euclidean worldline action

S_{\mathrm{E}}[X,\hat{G}]=\int\mathrm{d}\tau\left[\frac{1}{4T}\dot{x}^{2}+\alpha_{\mu}(\partial_{\tau}+i\theta)\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}+\beta(\partial_{\tau}+i\theta)\bar{\beta}+m^{2}T-ic\theta\right]\;.

(2.1.44)

The final expression of the path integral on worldlines with the topology of a circle – dubbed “worldloop” – can thus be recast as

\displaystyle\Gamma=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}T}{T}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}T}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}\bar{x}}{(4\pi T)^{\nicefrac{{D}}{{2}}}}\ {\rm DoF}(c,D)\;,

(2.1.45)

where we extracted the dependence on the zero modes
\bar{x}^{\mu}
of the coordinates by setting

x^{\mu}(\tau)=\bar{x}^{\mu}+t^{\mu}(\tau)\;,\quad\text{with}\quad t^{\mu}(0)=t^{\mu}(1)=0\;,

(2.1.46)

with the quantum fluctuations
t^{\mu}(\tau)
satisfying Dirichlet boundary conditions, evaluated the free path integral that produces functional determinants, and denoted by
DoF(
c,D
) the number of (complex) degrees of freedom, that acquires the expression

{\rm DoF}(c,D)=k\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{ic\theta}\;{\rm Det}\left(\partial_{\tau}-i\theta\right){\rm Det}\left(\partial_{\tau}+i\theta\right)\left[{\rm Det}(\partial_{\tau}+i\theta)\right]^{-D-1}\;,

(2.1.47)

with
k
an overall normalization to be fixed later on.
The value DoF
=1
corresponds to a complex scalar, as seen by comparing with
QFT
expressions.
In this formula, the first two functional determinants are the
ΦΠ
ones,
whereas the third one is due to the path integration over the bosonic oscillators. All determinants are evaluated with periodic boundary conditions:
using

{\rm Det}\left(\partial_{\tau}+i\theta\right)=2i\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\;,

(2.1.48)

see for example
[
47
,
108
]
,
setting the
CS
coupling to
4
4

4

The shift from the value given in (
2.1.27
) is due to the contribution of the ghost fields. For convenience, we now indicate the degrees of freedom by

{\rm DoF}(s,D)
, which highlights the dependence on the value of the spin
s
, rather than on the
CS
coupling
c
.
This should not cause any confusion.

c=\frac{D-1}{2}+s
, and fixing
k=-1
as overall normalization, we find the following expression for the number of degrees of freedom

\displaystyle{\rm DoF}(s,D)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}\;\mathrm{e}^{i\left(\frac{D-1}{2}+s\right)\theta}\;\left(2i\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{1-D}\;.

(2.1.49)

To evaluate it, we find it more convenient to recast it in terms of the Wilson loop variable

w:=\mathrm{e}^{-i\theta}
, so that

{\rm DoF}(s,D)=\oint\frac{\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi i}\,\frac{1}{w^{s+1}}\,\frac{1}{(1-w)^{D-1}}\;.

(2.1.50)

Deforming the contour to exclude the singular point
w=1
, while taking care of the pole in
w=0
, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\rm DoF}(0,D)=1\;,\\
&{\rm DoF}(1,D)=(D-1)\;,\\
&{\rm DoF}(2,D)=\frac{D(D-1)}{2}\;,\\
&\cdots\\
&{\rm DoF}(s,D)=\frac{(D-1)D\cdots(D+s-2)}{s!}\;,\end{split}

(2.1.51)

which indeed describes the degrees of freedom of a reducible (for
s\geq 1
) representation of the little group
SO(D-1)

as carried by a symmetric tensor with
s
indices. It corresponds to the propagation of a
multiplet of massive particles of decreasing
spin
s,s-2,s-4,\cdots,0
for even
s
, and
s,s-2,\cdots,1
for odd
s
.
This matches the results seen in the lightcone gauge.

2.2
Coupling to electromagnetism

The BRST formalism is especially well-suited for analyzing the constraints required for consistent background interactions. For this reason, we briefly review the free particle in this framework and then examine its interaction with an electromagnetic background.

2.2.1
Free BRST analysis

We proceed with the BRST quantization focusing only on the subalgebra of (
2.1.22
) generated by
(H,L,\bar{L})
. The constraint
J_{c}
is treated on different footings: it is imposed as a constraint on the
BRST Hilbert space, defining a restricted Hilbert space where the cohomology of the BRST operator will be analyzed.

The Hilbert space is enlarged to realize the fermionic ghost-antighost pairs of operators

\displaystyle\{b,c\}=1\;,\quad\{\mathcal{B},\bar{\mathcal{C}}\}=1\;,\quad\{\bar{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{C}\}=1\;,

(2.2.1)

associated with the
(H,L,\bar{L})
constraints, respectively. We assign them the following ghost numbers:
\mathrm{gh}(c,\bar{\mathcal{C}},\mathcal{C})=+1\,,\;\mathrm{gh}(b,\bar{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{B})=-1
. The BRST charge associated with a first-class system is readily constructed. In the present case, it takes the form

\mathcal{Q}=cH+\bar{\mathcal{C}}L+\mathcal{C}\bar{L}-2\mathcal{C}\bar{\mathcal{C}}b\;.

(2.2.2)

It is an anticommuting, ghost number
+1
, nilpotent operator by construction. It is hermitian provided that

\displaystyle c^{\dagger}\ =c\;,\quad b^{\dagger}=b\;,\quad\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}\;,\quad\mathcal{B}^{\dagger}=\bar{\mathcal{B}}\;.

(2.2.3)

The matter sector Hilbert space (
2.1.28
) is extended to the BRST Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{BRST}}
by a tensor product with the ghost sector, associated with the
(c,b,\mathcal{B},\bar{\mathcal{C}},\mathcal{C},\bar{\mathcal{B}})
operators. The latter is constructed
as a Fock space on the ghost vacuum defined by

(b,\bar{\mathcal{C}},\bar{\mathcal{B}})\,\ket{0}_{\mathrm{gh}}=0\;.

(2.2.4)

Since all ghosts are Grassmann odd,
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{gh}}
is finite dimensional.
5
5

5

In particular, the Fock vacuum
\ket{0}_{\mathrm{gh}}
can be mapped into the “physical vacuum”
\ket{1}_{\mathrm{gh}}
, with the concept of “physicality” to be defined shortly,
by

\ket{1}_{\mathrm{gh}}:=\mathcal{B}\ket{0}_{\mathrm{gh}}\ ,

see e.g. the discussion in Ref.
[
59
,
89
]
. The vacuum
\ket{1}_{\mathrm{gh}}
turns out to be the correct one to consider in order to create external states by inserting vertex operators in the worldline path integral, as illustrated in the upcoming Chapter
3
.
A generic state
\ket{\Phi}
in the BRST-extended Hilbert
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{BRST}}
space reads

\displaystyle\ket{\Phi}=\sum_{s,p=0}^{\infty}\sum_{q,r,t=0}^{1}c^{q}\mathcal{C}^{r}\mathcal{B}^{t}\ket{\Phi^{(s,p)(q,r,t)}}

(2.2.5)

where

\displaystyle\ket{\Phi^{(s,p)(q,r,t)}}=\frac{1}{s!p!}\,\Phi^{(s,p)(q,r,t)}_{\mu_{1}...\mu_{s}}(x)\,\alpha^{\mu_{1}}\dots\alpha^{\mu_{s}}\beta^{p}\ket{0}\;,

(2.2.6)

with
\ket{0}
now denoting the full BRST vacuum. With this choice, the conjugate momenta act as derivatives:

p_{\mu}=-i\partial_{\mu}\;,\quad\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}=\partial_{\alpha^{\mu}}\;,\quad\bar{\beta}=\partial_{\beta}\;,\quad b=\partial_{c}\;,\quad\bar{\mathcal{C}}=\partial_{\mathcal{B}}\;,\quad\bar{\mathcal{B}}=\partial_{\mathcal{C}}\;.

(2.2.7)

We now introduce a couple of operators,
G
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{s}
, to further restrict the full BRST Hilbert space. These are the ghost number operator

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=cb+\mathcal{C}\bar{\mathcal{B}}-\mathcal{B}\bar{\mathcal{C}}\;,\qquad[G,\mathcal{Q}]=\mathcal{Q}\;,

(2.2.8)

and the (shifted) occupation number operator
{\mathcal{J}}_{s}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mu}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}+\beta\bar{\beta}+\mathcal{C}\bar{\mathcal{B}}+\mathcal{B}\bar{\mathcal{C}}-s\;,\quad[\mathcal{Q},{\mathcal{J}}_{s}]=0\;.

(2.2.9)

They commute between themselves,
[G,{\mathcal{J}}_{s}]=0
.
The ghost number operator grades the BRST Hilbert space according to the ghost number, and the commutator
[G,\mathcal{Q}]=\mathcal{Q}
manifests that the BRST charge has ghost number 1.
The occupation number operator
{\mathcal{J}}_{s}
also grades the Hilbert space according to its eigenvalues and
can be used as a constraint to project the Hilbert space onto the subspace with fixed occupation number
s
.
6
6

6

We choose an antisymmetric quantization’s prescriptions for fermionic operators. In combination with the Weyl ordering for the bosonic operators previously discussed, the effect is to shift the
CS
coupling as
c=\frac{D-1}{2}+s
.
This relation has already been used in the path integral construction (see footnote 4), which evidently involves a regularization consistent with this ordering prescription.

We can exploit the operators above simultaneously – since
[G,{\mathcal{J}}_{s}]=0
– to select states in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{BRST}}
with a precise ghost and occupation number. The physical states are identified as elements of the BRST cohomology

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}\ket{\Phi}=0\;,\quad\ket{\Phi}\sim\ket{\Phi}+\mathcal{Q}\ket{\Lambda}

(2.2.10)

restricted to the subspace with vanishing eigenvalues of the ghost number and shifted occupation number operators, i.e.

\displaystyle G\ket{\Phi}={\mathcal{J}}_{s}\ket{\Phi}=0\;.

(2.2.11)

Our interest lies in the first-quantized description of a massive spin-1 particle: this is achieved by choosing
s=1
.
An arbitrary wavefunction at zero ghost number and with
s=1
is then given by

\ket{\psi}=W_{\mu}(x)\alpha^{\mu}\ket{0}-i\varphi(x)\beta\ket{0}+f(x)c\mathcal{B}\ket{0}\;,

(2.2.12)

where the complex fields
W_{\mu}(x)
,
\varphi(x)
, and
f(x)
must be further constrained by Eq. (
2.2.10
) to represent the physical states of the theory.
From the closure equation, i.e., the first one in (
2.2.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\Box-m^{2}\right)W_{\mu}-2i\partial_{\mu}f

\displaystyle=0\;,

(2.2.13a)

\displaystyle\left(\Box-m^{2}\right)\varphi+2imf

\displaystyle=0\;,

(2.2.13b)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}W^{\mu}+m\varphi-2if

\displaystyle=0\;,

(2.2.13c)

which, upon eliminating the auxiliary field
f(x)
, represent the field equations of the Proca field in the Stückelberg formulation

\displaystyle\left(\Box-m^{2}\right)W_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}\partial\cdot W-m\partial_{\mu}\varphi

\displaystyle=0\;,

(2.2.14a)

\displaystyle\Box\varphi+m\partial_{\mu}W^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\;,

(2.2.14b)

where the dot “
\cdot
” indicates contraction over spacetime indices. The latter equations enjoy a gauge symmetry, which, from (
2.2.10
), reads

\delta\ket{\psi}=Q\ket{\Lambda}\;,\quad\text{with}\quad\ket{\Lambda}=i\xi(x)\mathcal{B}\ket{0}\;,

(2.2.15)

i.e.

\delta W_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\xi\;,\quad\delta\varphi=-m\xi\;,

(2.2.16)

which is the well-known Stückelberg gauge symmetry.

A few comments are in order: (
i
) the wavefunction (
2.2.12
) can be interpreted as a spacetime Batalin–Vilkovisky (
BV
) “string field” displaying only
the classical fields out of the minimal
BV
spectrum of the Proca theory, along with an auxiliary field.
7
7

7

The complete minimal
BV
spectrum is obtained by relaxing the condition
G\ket{\psi}=0
, see for instance
[
99
,
147
]
.
The Grassmann parities and ghost numbers of the field components are all equal to zero.
Note the presence of the Stückelberg scalar
\varphi
, which restores the
U(1)
gauge symmetry
[
239
]
, originally broken due to the introduction of the mass.
(
ii
) In the so-called
unitary gauge
, namely setting the Stückelberg field to zero, one reduces the field equations to the standard Fierz–Pauli system for the massive spin-1 field
W_{\mu}(x)
. (
iii
) Taking the massless limit produces from (
2.2.14
) a pair of decoupled equations: one for a free-propagating massless vector field
W_{\mu}(x)
and one for a massless scalar field
\varphi(x)
. This is tantamount to the fact that the theory of massive spin-1 does not suffer from the so-called “van Dam–Veltman–Zakharov (
vDVZ
) discontinuity”,
8
8

8

Roughly speaking, the
vDVZ
discontinuity implies that that the massive theory, however small the mass of the particles may be, leads to different physical predictions that do not smoothly recover the results of the massless theory.
differently from the massive spin 2 case
[
243
,
255
]
, which is covered in Chapter
4
.

2.2.2
Consistent electromagnetic coupling

The coupling of the worldline to an abelian background field
A_{\mu}(x)
in spacetime (with coupling constant
q
) is achieved by covariantizing the
(L,\bar{L})
constraints as follows
