color-adjacent
, which means that, in computing color-ordered amplitudes,
V_{ij}
is needed only if gluons
i
and
j
are adjacent in the cyclic ordering
12\ldots n
.

Finally, as we shall discuss, additional nonlinear vertex operators are required for the construction of amplitudes. While the linear and quadratic vertex operators are already encoded in the action – or equivalently, in the BRST charge – further composite vertex operators, which attach subtrees to a main worldline, arise from the requirement of BRST-invariant amplitudes, thereby ensuring the consistency of the theory. Although a derivation of these operators from purely worldline considerations remains perhaps incomplete, the analysis presented in Ref.
[
87
]
for the scalar theory contains the essential ingredients. We will return to this issue in the next sections.

3.2
Tree-level gluon amplitudes

We now apply the formalism developed in the previous sections to compute gluon scattering amplitudes at tree level. To this end, following the analysis of Ref.
[
87
]
, we take the worldline to have infinite length, so that the proper time
T
serves purely as an infrared regulator and drops out of all computations. The amplitudes are obtained by evaluating suitable correlation functions with insertions of the vertex operators, previously derived by coupling the worldline to a Yang–Mills background field.
Before delving into the explicit calculations, we briefly outline how correlators are defined and evaluated on open worldlines in our formalism. In what follows, all external gluons are taken to be on-shell, though not necessarily transverse.

3.2.1
Correlation functions on open lines

To project on states of zero momentum, the coordinate trajectories are split as

x^{\mu}(\tau)=x^{\mu}_{0}+z^{\mu}(\tau)\;,\quad\dot{z}^{\mu}(\pm T/2)=0\;,

(3.2.1)

where
x_{0}^{\mu}
is the zero mode, which enforces momentum conservation. Coming to the oscillator vacuum, recall that the boundary conditions corresponding to
\ket{0}
at both
\tau=\pm T/2
are

\bra{0}\ldots\ket{0}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad\bar{\alpha}_{M}(-T/2)=0\;,\quad\alpha^{M}(T/2)=0\;,\quad b(\pm T/2)=0\;.

(3.2.2)

On the other hand, the physical vacuum
\ket{1}={\cal B}\ket{0}
is characterized by
({\cal B},b,\bar{\cal B})\ket{1}=0
. Upon denoting all ghosts and antighosts as
C^{i}:=({\cal C},c,\bar{\cal C})
and
B_{i}:=(\bar{\cal B},b,{\cal B})
, the boundary conditions corresponding to the vacuum
\ket{1}
are then

\bra{1}\ldots\ket{1}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}(-T/2)=0\;,\quad\alpha^{\mu}(T/2)=0\;,\quad B_{i}(\pm T/2)=0\;.

(3.2.3)

Since all ghosts and antighosts have the same boundary conditions, we can rewrite the free Lagrangian action (
3.1.36
) as

S_{\text{free}}=\int_{-T/2}^{T/2}\!\!\!d\tau\Big[\tfrac{1}{4}\,\dot{x}^{2}-i\,\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}\dot{\alpha}^{\mu}+iB_{i}\dot{C}^{i}\Big]\;.

(3.2.4)

Note that the basic overlap for the physical vacuum needs three ghost insertions:

\bra{1}{\cal C}\,c\,\bar{\cal C}\ket{1}=1\;.

(3.2.5)

In the path integral, this is reflected by the fact that all
C^{i}(\tau)
admit a zero mode, which needs to be saturated. The basic expectation value for the ghosts is thus given by

\langle{\cal C}(\tau_{1})\,c(\tau_{2})\,\bar{\cal C}(\tau_{3})\rangle=1\;.

(3.2.6)

From the quadratic action (
3.2.4
) with boundary conditions (
3.2.1
) and (
3.2.3
), we derive the following two-point functions (the ghost zero mode saturation is left implicit)

\begin{split}\langle\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)\alpha^{\nu}(\sigma)\rangle&=\eta^{\mu\nu}\theta(\tau-\sigma)\;,\\
\langle z^{\mu}(\tau)z^{\nu}(\sigma)\rangle&=-i\eta^{\mu\nu}G(\tau,\sigma)\;,\\
\langle\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau)z^{\nu}(\sigma)\rangle&=-i\eta^{\mu\nu}\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G(\tau,\sigma)\;,\\
\langle\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau)\dot{z}^{\nu}(\sigma)\rangle&=-i\eta^{\mu\nu}\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G\dot{\phantom{a}}\!(\tau,\sigma)\;,\end{split}

(3.2.7)

where we denote derivatives with respect to the left and right variables by left and right dots, respectively. The
z
-propagator and its derivatives read

\begin{split}G(\tau,\sigma)&=|\tau-\sigma|-\frac{1}{T}\,(\tau-\sigma)^{2}-\frac{2}{T}\,\tau\sigma-\frac{T}{6}\;,\\
\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G(\tau,\sigma)&=\text{sgn}(\tau-\sigma)-\frac{2}{T}\,\tau\;,\\
\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G\dot{\phantom{a}}\!(\tau,\sigma)&=-2\,\delta(\tau-\sigma)\;.\end{split}

(3.2.8)

In particular, notice that the Neumann boundary condition
\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G(\pm T/2,\sigma)=0
holds only at separate points. At coincident points one rather has
\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G(\pm T/2,\pm T/2)=\mp 1
. This reflects the Weyl ordering of operators, that is implicit in the path integral.
5
5

5

For instance, the quantum operator corresponding to
x^{\mu}p^{\nu}
is
\frac{1}{2}\,(\hat{x}^{\mu}\hat{p}^{\nu}+\hat{p}^{\nu}\hat{x}^{\mu})
.
This fact will be relevant when computing the three-gluon amplitude.

3.2.2
Color-ordered amplitudes

We now illustrate the construction above with explicit computations, starting from the simplest test of the model: the three-gluon amplitude. Then, we move on to the calculation of the color-ordered four-point amplitude, which we perform in two distinct ways. First, we select the worldline connecting two of the least color-adjacent particles, which we regard as a “smart” choice for computational reasons, as should be clear shortly. Then, we reproduce the same calculation by choosing the “not-so-smart” worldline, namely the one that connects two color-adjacent particles. Throughout this section, we implicitly factor out the momentum-conserving delta function, which arises from the integral over the zero-mode
x_{0}^{\mu}
. Every
x^{\mu}(\tau)
in the vertex operators can thus be substituted with the fluctuation
z^{\mu}(\tau)
.

3.2.2.1
The three-gluon amplitude

Although the three-gluon amplitude vanishes for real momenta due to kinematical reasons, by taking the external gluons to be on-shell but not necessarily transverse (
k_{i}^{2}=0
,
k_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{i}\neq 0
), we obtain a nontrivial expression that can be directly compared with the standard field theory result.

To saturate the ghost zero modes, we need all three vertex operators to be unintegrated. This has a clear geometric interpretation: two vertex operators are fixed at asymptotic times
\tau=\pm\infty
to create the incoming and outgoing worldline states, while the third one is fixed at an intermediate time using the rigid translation invariance on the infinite line. To compute the color-ordered amplitude, we need to insert the three vertex operators with a fixed (cyclic) order in time (see figure
3.1
). The remaining expectation value is taken with respect to
z^{\mu}
and the other variables and gives the color-ordered amplitude as

{\cal A}_{123}=\big\langle{\cal V}_{1}(+\infty){\cal V}_{2}(0){\cal V}_{3}(-\infty)\big\rangle\;,

(3.2.9)

where we used the shorthand
i=\{\epsilon_{i},k_{i}\}
for particle labels.

{\cal A}_{123}=
x
1
2
0
x
3

Figure 3.1:
Diagrammatic representation of the color-ordered three-gluon amplitude
{\cal A}_{123}
. The straight line denotes the insertion of an unintegrated vertex operator at
\tau=0
. The asymptotic endpoints of the worldline are depicted by a cross.

Using the split
{\cal V}_{i}(\tau_{i})=W_{i}(\tau_{i})+c(\tau_{i})\,V_{i}(\tau_{i})
, the above expression gives three contributions
(as one needs only one
c
-ghost insertion for obtaining a non-vanishing result)

\begin{split}{\cal A}_{123}&=\big\langle W_{1}(+\infty)\,c(0)V_{2}(0)W_{3}(-\infty)\big\rangle+\big\langle W_{1}(+\infty)W_{2}(0)\,c(-\infty)V_{3}(-\infty)\big\rangle\\
&\phantom{=}+\big\langle c(+\infty)V_{1}(+\infty)W_{2}(0)W_{3}(-\infty)\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(3.2.10)

The expression (
3.1.59
) for
V_{i}(\tau_{i})
suggests that
V_{i}(\pm\infty)=0
, thanks to the boundary conditions. As we have already mentioned, however, this is not true due to the self-contraction

\epsilon\cdot\dot{z}(\tau)\,e^{ik\cdot z(\tau)}\sim k\cdot\epsilon\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G(\tau,\tau)\,e^{ik\cdot z(\tau)}+\cdots\;,

(3.2.11)

and
\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G(\tau,\tau)
does not vanish when
\tau\rightarrow\pm\infty
. This term does vanish for transverse gluons, which motivates us to consider non-transverse polarizations in order to study its effect.

Let us compute separately the three contributions in (
3.2.10
). All correlators have a common “Koba-Nielsen”
factor, given by the contraction of the plane waves
e^{ik_{i}\cdot z(\tau_{i})}
, which is trivial, due to momentum conservation and the on-shell condition:

\begin{split}\exp\{\tfrac{i}{2}\sum_{ij}k_{i}\cdot k_{j}\,G(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})\Big\}&=\exp\{i\sum_{i<j}k_{i}\cdot k_{j}\,|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}|\Big\}\\
&=\exp\left\{-\frac{iT(k_{1}^{2}+k_{3}^{2})}{2}\right\}\to 1\;,\end{split}

(3.2.12)

where we used
T/2\rightarrow\infty
as an infrared regulator. We further use (
3.2.6
) for saturating the ghost zero modes. This only gives relative signs between the three contributions. The first term in (
3.2.10
) yields

\begin{split}&\big\langle W_{1}(+\infty)\,c(0)V_{2}(0)W_{3}(-\infty)\big\rangle\\
&=\Big\langle\epsilon_{1}\cdot\bar{\alpha}(+\infty)\,\big[i\dot{z}(0)\cdot\epsilon_{2}+4i\,k_{2}^{[\mu}\epsilon_{2}^{\nu]}\alpha_{\mu}(0)\bar{\alpha}_{\nu}(0)\big]\epsilon_{3}\cdot\alpha(-\infty)e^{i\sum_{i}k_{i}\cdot z(\tau_{i})}\Big\rangle\\
&=\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,i\epsilon_{2}\cdot\sum_{i}k_{i}\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G(0,\tau_{i})+4i\,k_{2}^{[\mu}\epsilon_{2}^{\nu]}\epsilon_{1\mu}\epsilon_{3\nu}\\
&=i\Big(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot(k_{3}-k_{1})+2\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{2}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{2}-2\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot k_{2}\Big)\;,\end{split}

(3.2.13)

where in the second line we removed the ghost factor. The above result is already the correct amplitude for transverse gluons. The remaining two terms add the contributions from the longitudinal modes:

\begin{split}&\big\langle W_{1}(+\infty)W_{2}(0)\,c(-\infty)V_{3}(-\infty)\big\rangle\\
&=-\Big\langle\epsilon_{1}\cdot\bar{\alpha}(+\infty)\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\alpha(0)\,i\epsilon_{3}\cdot\dot{z}(-\infty)\,e^{i\sum_{i}k_{i}\cdot z(\tau_{i})}\Big\rangle\\
&=-\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,ik_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G(-\infty,-\infty)=-i\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,k_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\;,\end{split}

(3.2.14)

where we used the fact that only the term with
\dot{z}^{\mu}
in
V_{i}(\pm\infty)
contributes. The last term in (
3.2.10
) similarly gives

\begin{split}&\big\langle c(+\infty)V_{1}(+\infty)W_{2}(0)W_{3}(-\infty)\big\rangle\\
&=-\Big\langle i\epsilon_{1}\cdot\dot{z}(+\infty)\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\bar{\alpha}(0)\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot\alpha(-\infty)\,\,e^{i\sum_{i}k_{i}\cdot z(\tau_{i})}\Big\rangle\\
&=-\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,ik_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{1}\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G(+\infty,+\infty)=i\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,k_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{1}\;.\end{split}

(3.2.15)

By using momentum conservation, we can write the full result in the manifest cyclic form

\begin{split}{\cal A}_{123}&=i\,\Big[\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot(k_{1}-k_{2})+\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot(k_{2}-k_{3})+\epsilon_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{1}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot(k_{3}-k_{1})\Big]\;.\end{split}

(3.2.16)

This is the correct three-point amplitude even for non-transverse gluons, as it coincides with the color-ordered cubic vertex seen in the second quantized picture.

3.2.2.2
The four-gluon amplitude

For the four-point color-ordered amplitude, we start by selecting the “smart” worldline, i.e. the one connecting particles 4 in the past with particle 2 in the future (or, equivalently, the one connecting particles 1 and 3, see figure
3.2
).
6
6

6

It seems that the most convenient choice is a worldline that connects two of the least color-adjacent particles.
The advantage in this choice of worldline comes from the fact that we are allowed to integrate
V_{1}(\tau)
over the entire line, without altering the cyclic order of the particles. We are thus led to evaluate

{\cal A}_{1234}=\Big\langle{\cal V}_{2}(+\infty)\,{\cal V}_{3}(0)\,\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\hskip-17.07164ptd\tau}\,V_{1}(\tau)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\;,

(3.2.17)

which, for transverse gluons (
V_{\epsilon,k}(\pm\infty)=0
), reduces to

{\cal A}_{1234}=\Big\langle W_{2}(+\infty)\,c(0)\,V_{3}(0)\,\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\hskip-17.07164ptd\tau}\,V_{1}(\tau)\,W_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\;.

(3.2.18)

Considering the overlap
\langle{\cal C}\,c\ \bar{\cal C}\rangle=1
, it simplifies to

{\cal A}_{1234}=\Big\langle\epsilon_{2}\cdot\bar{\alpha}(+\infty)e^{ik_{2}\cdot z(+\infty)}\,V_{3}(0)\,\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\hskip-17.07164ptd\tau}\,V_{1}(\tau)\,\epsilon_{4}\cdot\alpha(-\infty)e^{ik_{4}\cdot z(-\infty)}\Big\rangle\;.

(3.2.19)

The overall exponentials of the vertex operators will lead by Wick contractions to

\begin{split}\big\langle e^{i\sum_{i}k_{i}\cdot z(\tau_{i})}\big\rangle\ &=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij}k_{i}\cdot\langle z(\tau_{i})z(\tau_{j})\rangle\cdot k_{j}}=e^{\frac{i}{2}\sum_{ij}k_{i}\cdot k_{j}G(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})}\\
&=e^{i\sum_{i<j}k_{i}\cdot k_{j}|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}|}=e^{\frac{i}{2}(-s_{12}\tau+s_{23}\tau+s_{31}|\tau|)}\;,\end{split}

(3.2.20)

where we have used the basic two-point functions in (
3.2.7
) and (
3.2.8
),
noted that only the absolute value in
G(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})
contributes,
and used the Mandelstam variables

s_{ij}=(k_{i}+k_{j})^{2}=2k_{i}\cdot k_{j}\;.

(3.2.21)

To display the separation in two channels, the final answer is better written using the on-shell value of
s_{31}=-s_{12}-s_{23}
, leading to

\big\langle e^{i\sum_{i}k_{i}\cdot z(\tau_{i})}\big\rangle\ =e^{\theta(\tau)(-i\tau s_{12})+\theta(-\tau)(i\tau s_{23})}=\theta(\tau)\,e^{-i\tau s_{12}}+\theta(-\tau)\,e^{i\tau s_{23}}\;.

(3.2.22)

The integral in
\tau
will split into the two regions
\tau>0
and
\tau<0
, corresponding to the
s
- and
t
-channels, respectively (recall that there is no
u
-channel in the color-ordered amplitude).

{\cal A}_{1234}=
x
2
1
3
\tau
0
x
4

Figure 3.2:
Diagrammatic representation of the color-ordered four-gluon amplitude
{\cal A}_{1234}
with the smart choice for the worldline. The wavy line denotes the insertion of an integrated vertex operator, while, as before, the straight line denotes the one fixed by translation invariance.

The remainder of the correlation function yields the numerator contributions. To compute these, we can further split the vertex operators
V_{i}(\tau)
into their “scalar” and “spin” parts as

V_{i}(\tau)=V^{\rm scal}_{i}(\tau)+V^{\rm spin}_{i}(\tau)\;

(3.2.23)

with

\begin{split}V^{\rm scal}_{i}(\tau)&=i\,\epsilon_{i}\cdot\dot{z}(\tau)\,e^{ik_{i}\cdot z(\tau)}\;,\\
V^{\rm spin}_{i}(\tau)&=4i\,k_{i}^{[\mu}\epsilon_{i}^{\nu]}\alpha_{\mu}(\tau)\,\bar{\alpha}_{\nu}(\tau)\,e^{ik_{i}\cdot z(\tau)}\;.\end{split}

(3.2.24)

Omitting the contraction (
3.2.22
) of the plane waves, these terms contribute to the correlator as follows:

\begin{split}&\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm scal}_{3}(0)\,V^{\rm scal}_{1}(\tau)\cdots\big\rangle=4\,\theta(\tau)\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{2}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot k_{4}+4\,\theta(-\tau)\,(1\leftrightarrow 3)-2i\,\delta(\tau)\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\;,\\[8.53581pt]
&\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm scal}_{3}(0)\,V^{\rm spin}_{1}(\tau)\cdots\big\rangle+\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm spin}_{3}(0)\,V^{\rm scal}_{1}(\tau)\cdots\big\rangle=\\
&4\,\theta(\tau)\,\Big[\big(\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{3}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,k_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\big)\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{2}-\big(\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{1}\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{1}\,k_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\big)\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot k_{4}\Big]+4\,\theta(-\tau)\,(1\leftrightarrow 3)\;,\\[8.53581pt]
&\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm spin}_{3}(0)\,V^{\rm spin}_{1}(\tau)\cdots\big\rangle=\\
&4\,\theta(\tau)\,\Big[\epsilon_{4}\cdot k_{3}\big(\epsilon_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{1}\,k_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}\cdot k_{1}\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\big)-\epsilon_{4}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\big(k_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{1}\,k_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}-k_{3}\cdot k_{1}\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\big)\Big]+4\,\theta(-\tau)\,(1\leftrightarrow 3)\;,\end{split}

(3.2.25)

where
+\,(1\leftrightarrow 3)
means to exchange simultaneously
(\epsilon_{1},k_{1})
with
(\epsilon_{3},k_{3})
. Summing the three contributions above, multiplied by the common scalar factor (
3.2.22
), and performing the
\tau
integral gives the final result. This can be organized upon introducing the following products:

\begin{split}m_{2}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{j})&:=i\,\Big(2\,\epsilon_{i}\cdot k_{j}\,\epsilon_{j}^{\mu}-2\,\epsilon_{j}\cdot k_{i}\,\epsilon_{i}^{\mu}+(k^{\mu}_{i}-k^{\mu}_{j})\,\epsilon_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{j}\Big)\;,\\
m_{3}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{j},\epsilon_{k})&:=\epsilon_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{j}\,\epsilon_{k}^{\mu}+\epsilon_{k}\cdot\epsilon_{j}\,\epsilon_{i}^{\mu}-2\,\epsilon_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{k}\,\epsilon_{j}^{\mu}\;.\end{split}

(3.2.26)

These products encode (for transverse gluons) the color-ordered Feynman rules for the cubic and quartic vertices, respectively
[
256
,
86
,
83
]
, and play a central role in the Berends-Giele recursion relations
[
62
,
230
,
209
,
204
]
. In terms of the products above, one can rewrite the result coming from the path integral as

{\cal A}_{1234}=\frac{i}{s_{12}}\,m_{2}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cdot m_{2}(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4})+\frac{i}{s_{23}}\,m_{2}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\cdot m_{2}(\epsilon_{4},\epsilon_{1})+i\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot m_{3}(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4},\epsilon_{1})\;,

(3.2.27)

which highlights the two exchange diagrams and the contact quartic term. This is the correct color-ordered four-gluon amplitude. It is customary to blow up the quartic vertex and define kinematic numerators as

\begin{split}n_{12}&:=m_{2}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cdot m_{2}(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4})+\frac{s_{12}}{3}\,\big[m_{3}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})-m_{3}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{3})\big]\cdot\epsilon_{4}\;,\\
n_{23}&:=m_{2}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\cdot m_{2}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{4})+\frac{s_{23}}{3}\,\big[m_{3}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{1})-m_{3}(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{1})\big]\cdot\epsilon_{4}\;,\\
n_{31}&:=m_{2}(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{1})\cdot m_{2}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{4})+\frac{s_{31}}{3}\,\big[m_{3}(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})-m_{3}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{2})\big]\cdot\epsilon_{4}\;,\end{split}

(3.2.28)

where the various channels are sometimes identified with the standard names for the Mandelstam variables for four points:
12\rightarrow s
,
23\rightarrow t
,
31\rightarrow u
. Upon using the following symmetry properties of the products:

\begin{split}m_{2}(\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{j})+m_{2}(\epsilon_{j},\epsilon_{i})&=0\;,\\
m_{3}(\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{j},\epsilon_{k})-m_{3}(\epsilon_{k},\epsilon_{j},\epsilon_{i})&=0\;,\\
m_{3}(\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{j},\epsilon_{k})+m_{3}(\epsilon_{j},\epsilon_{k},\epsilon_{i})+m_{3}(\epsilon_{k},\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{j})&=0\;,\end{split}

(3.2.29)

the color-ordered amplitude can be recast in the compact form

{\cal A}_{1234}=i\,\left[\frac{n_{s}}{s}-\frac{n_{t}}{t}\right]\;.

(3.2.30)

We shall now illustrate how a different choice for the worldline would lead to the same result, albeit with comparatively greater effort.

Not-so-smart worldline

Choosing instead the worldline connecting the color-adjacent particles 1 and 4 in figure
3.2
, we have to compute three different contributions

{\cal A}_{1234}={\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{I}}+{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{II}}+{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{III}}\;,

(3.2.31)

corresponding to three different diagrams (see figure
3.3
). Explicitly

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{I}}

\displaystyle=-\Big\langle{\cal V}_{1}(+\infty)\,\int_{0}^{+\infty}{\hskip-17.07164ptd\tau}\,V_{2}(\tau)\,{\cal V}_{3}(0)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\ ,

(3.2.32)

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{II}}

\displaystyle=i\,\Big\langle{\cal V}_{1}(+\infty)\,c(0)V_{23}(0)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\ ,

(3.2.33)

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{III}}

\displaystyle=i\,\Big\langle{\cal V}_{1}(+\infty)\,c(0)\,V_{23}^{\rm pinch}(0)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\;,

(3.2.34)

where the phases are given by a factor of
i
for every insertion corresponding to a real interaction vertex. The first contribution
{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{I}}
arises from integrating the linear vertex for particle 2, with the range restricted to preserve the cyclic ordering of particles. Since gluons 2 and 3 are color-adjacent, the bilinear vertex
V_{23}(\tau)
is also needed, as discussed in sec.
3.1.4
. Using translation invariance, we place it at
\tau=0
.
These two contributions, however, are not sufficient to produce a gauge invariant amplitude, as we will demonstrate in sec.
3.3
. The missing part, which we denoted
{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{III}}
above, involves a composite operator,
V_{23}^{\rm pinch}(\tau)
, which attaches to the worldline the cubic subtree formed by particles 2 and 3. This pinch operator is nothing but a vertex operator
V_{\epsilon_{23},k_{23}}
of the standard form (
3.1.59
), with momentum
k_{2}+k_{3}
and multiparticle polarization given by
\epsilon^{\mu}_{23}=\tfrac{1}{s_{23}}m_{2}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})
, to be further discussed in the next section.

{\cal A}_{1234}=
x
1
2
3
\tau
0
x
4
+
x
1
2
3
0
x
4
+
x
1
2
3
0
x
4

Figure 3.3:
Diagrammatic representation of the color-ordered four-gluon amplitude
{\cal A}_{1234}
with the not-so-smart choice for the worldline. As before, the wavy line denotes the insertion of an integrated vertex operator, while the straight lines denote the insertion of unintegrated vertex operators at
\tau=0
.

We start the computation with the first diagram
{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{I}}
(
3.2.32
), whose calculation is akin to the one for the smart worldline. Taking transverse gluons and recalling the overlap
\langle{\cal C}\,c\ \bar{\cal C}\rangle=1
, it simplifies to

{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{I}}=-\big\langle\epsilon_{1}\cdot\bar{\alpha}(+\infty)e^{ik_{1}\cdot z(+\infty)}\,\int_{0}^{+\infty}{\hskip-17.07164ptd\tau}\,V_{2}(\tau)\,V_{3}(0)\,\epsilon_{4}\cdot\alpha(-\infty)e^{ik_{4}\cdot z(-\infty)}\big\rangle\;,

(3.2.35)

with the Koba-Nielsen scalar factor reducing to

\big\langle e^{i\sum_{i}k_{i}\cdot z(\tau_{i})}\big\rangle\ =e^{-i\,s_{12}\tau}\;.

(3.2.36)

Splitting the vertex operators into scalar and spin parts as in (
3.2.23
), and omitting the contraction of the plane waves, the numerator contributions are given by the following correlators

\begin{split}&\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm scal}_{2}(\tau)\,V^{\rm scal}_{3}(0)\,\cdots\big\rangle=-2i\,\delta(\tau)\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}+4\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{1}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot k_{4}\;,\\[8.53581pt]
&\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm spin}_{2}(\tau)\,V^{\rm scal}_{3}(0)\,\cdots\big\rangle+\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm scal}_{2}(\tau)\,V^{\rm spin}_{3}(0)\,\cdots\big\rangle=\\
&-4\left[\epsilon_{3}\cdot k_{4}\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{2}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,k_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\right)-\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{1}\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{3}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,k_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\right)\right]\;,\\[8.53581pt]
&\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm spin}_{2}(\tau)\,V^{\rm spin}_{3}(0)\,\cdots\big\rangle=\\
&-4\left[k_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,k_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{2}\right)-\epsilon_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,k_{2}\cdot k_{3}-\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{2}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{3}\right)\right]\;,\end{split}

(3.2.37)

which is the same as in (
3.2.25
) but for
\tau>0
and with
(1\leftrightarrow 2)
. Multiplying by the common scalar factor (
3.2.36
) and integrating over
\tau
we get

\displaystyle\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm scal}_{2}(\tau)\,V^{\rm scal}_{3}(0)\,\cdots\big\rangle=i\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\,\left(\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}+\frac{4\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{1}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot k_{4}}{s_{12}}\right)\;,

\displaystyle\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm spin}_{2}(\tau)\,V^{\rm scal}_{3}(0)\,\cdots\big\rangle+\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm scal}_{2}(\tau)\,V^{\rm spin}_{3}(0)\,\cdots\big\rangle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4i}{s_{12}}\left[\epsilon_{3}\cdot k_{4}\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{2}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,k_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\right)-\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{1}\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{3}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,k_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\right)\right]\;,

\displaystyle\big\langle\cdots\,V^{\rm spin}_{2}(\tau)\,V^{\rm spin}_{3}(0)\,\cdots\big\rangle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4i}{s_{12}}\left[k_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,k_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{2}\right)-\epsilon_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,k_{2}\cdot k_{3}-\epsilon_{1}\cdot k_{2}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{3}\right)\right]\;.

(3.2.38)

The second diagram (
3.2.33
), with the bilinear vertex

V_{23}(0)=\Big(2\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\alpha(0)\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot\bar{\alpha}(0)-2\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot\alpha(0)\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\bar{\alpha}(0)\Big)\,e^{i(k_{2}+k_{3})\cdot x(0)}\ ,

(3.2.39)

yields the local contribution

{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{II}}=2i\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\right)\ .

(3.2.40)

We can recast the following partial contribution in a clearer form through the products (
3.2.26
):

{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{I}}+{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{II}}=\frac{i}{s_{12}}\,m_{2}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cdot m_{2}(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4})+i\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot m_{3}(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4},\epsilon_{1})\ .

(3.2.41)

Finally, the third contribution (
3.2.34
) can be computed with the following pinch operator

V_{23}^{\rm pinch}(0)=\frac{i}{s_{23}}m_{2\,\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\left(\dot{x}^{\mu}(0)+4(k_{2}+k_{3})_{\nu}\,\alpha^{[\nu}(0)\bar{\alpha}^{\mu]}(0)\,e^{i(k_{2}+k_{3})\cdot x(0)}\right)\;.

(3.2.42)

Since the scalar factor coming from the plane waves is
1
, this contribution reduces to

\begin{split}&\left.\big\langle\cdots\,V_{23}^{\rm pinch}(0)\,\cdots\big\rangle\right|_{\rm scal}=\frac{i}{s_{23}}\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\,m_{2}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\cdot(k_{4}-k_{1})\;,\\[8.53581pt]
&\left.\big\langle\cdots\,V_{23}^{\rm pinch}(0)\,\cdots\big\rangle\right|_{\rm spin}=\frac{2i}{s_{23}}m_{2\,\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\left[\epsilon_{1}\cdot(k_{2}+k_{3})\,\epsilon_{4}^{\mu}-\epsilon_{4}\cdot(k_{2}+k_{3})\,\epsilon_{1}^{\mu}\right]\;,\end{split}

(3.2.43)

which yields the
t
-channel part of the amplitude

\begin{split}{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{III}}&=-\frac{1}{s_{23}}\,m_{2\,\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\left[\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{4}\,(k_{4}-k_{1})^{\mu}+2\epsilon_{1}\cdot(k_{2}+k_{3})\,\epsilon_{4}^{\mu}-2\epsilon_{4}\cdot(k_{2}+k_{3})\,\epsilon_{1}^{\mu}\right]\\
&=\frac{i}{s_{23}}\,m_{2}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\cdot m_{2}(\epsilon_{4},\epsilon_{1})\;.\end{split}

(3.2.44)

The final result is thus given by the sum of the three individual contributions, reproducing the expected expression

{\cal A}_{1234}=\frac{i}{s_{12}}\,m_{2}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cdot m_{2}(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4})+\frac{i}{s_{23}}\,m_{2}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\cdot m_{2}(\epsilon_{4},\epsilon_{1})+i\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot m_{3}(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4},\epsilon_{1})\;.

(3.2.45)

This shows that the worldline connecting two color-adjacent gluons requires as many correlators as field theory Feynman diagrams. In addition, we have verified that the outcome does not depend on the choice of the main worldline.

3.2.3
Path integral representation for Berends-Giele currents

As just seen in the latter case, the pinching contribution, where gluons 2 and 3 are fused into a cubic subtree, requires the introduction of the composite field

A_{23}^{\mu}(x)=\frac{1}{k_{23}^{2}}\,m_{2}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\;.

(3.2.46)

The form of
A_{23}^{\mu}
is usually determined by the Bern-Kosower pinching rules, or from the field theory color-ordered Feynman rules. In this last part, we will show that the composite field can be computed directly from a worldline path integral, with different boundary conditions.

In order to describe nonlinear fields via worldline correlators, we follow the scalar example studied in Ref.
[
87
]
, and consider a semi-infinite worldline. We take the affine parameter
\tau\in(-\infty,0]
, so that the worldline has one real boundary and an asymptotic one. Since the proper length of the line is still infinite, there is no modulus for the einbein, which we gauge fix to
e(\tau)=2
. At the asymptotic past we want, as before, to project onto the zero-momentum vacuum
\ket{1}={\cal B}\ket{0}
, so that we can create an asymptotic gluon state by inserting
{\cal V}_{\epsilon,k}(-\infty)
. The vacuum
\ket{1}
corresponds to the asymptotic boundary conditions

\dot{x}^{\mu}(-\infty)=0\;,\quad\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(-\infty)=0\;,\quad\bar{\cal B}(-\infty)={\cal B}(-\infty)=b(-\infty)=0\;.

(3.2.47)

At
\tau=0
instead, we want to project onto a position eigenstate which, in order to support a vector field, is at the same time a coherent state for
\alpha^{\mu}
:
\bra{x,\alpha}c
. This bra state corresponds to the boundary conditions

x^{\mu}(0)=x^{\mu}\;,\quad\alpha^{\mu}(0)=\alpha^{\mu}\;,\quad{\cal B}(0)={\cal C}(0)=c(0)=0\;.

(3.2.48)

Note that the appearance of the
c
ghost in the bra state
\bra{x,\alpha}c
, corresponding to the boundary condition
c(0)=0
, is due to the absence of
c
zero-modes on the semi-infinite line.
The BRST invariant action with these boundary conditions is given by

S_{\rm DN}=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\!d\tau\,\Big[\tfrac{1}{4}\,\dot{x}^{2}-i\,\bar{\alpha}\cdot\dot{\alpha}+i\,b\dot{c}+i\,\bar{\cal B}\dot{\cal C}+i\,\bar{\cal C}\dot{\cal B}\Big]\;,

(3.2.49)

where the subscript
DN
stands for Dirichlet–Neumann.

Coherently with the boundary conditions, the only ghost that admits a zero mode is
\bar{\cal C}
, so that the saturation requires
\big\langle\bar{\cal C}(\tau)\big\rangle=1
. To compute correlation functions, we expand the fields as backgrounds plus fluctuations

x^{\mu}(\tau)=x^{\mu}+z^{\mu}(\tau)\;,\quad\alpha^{\mu}(\tau)=\alpha^{\mu}+\kappa^{\mu}(\tau)\;,\quad\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)=\bar{\kappa}^{\mu}(\tau)\;,

(3.2.50)

with vanishing (asymptotic) boundary conditions

z^{\mu}(0)=0\;,\quad\dot{z}^{\mu}(-\infty)=0\;,\quad\kappa^{\mu}(0)=0\;,\quad\bar{\kappa}^{\mu}(-\infty)=0\;.

(3.2.51)

These lead to the two-point functions

\begin{split}\langle z^{\mu}(\tau)\,z^{\nu}(\sigma)\rangle&=-i\,\eta^{\mu\nu}\,G_{\rm DN}(\tau,\sigma)\;,\quad\langle\bar{\kappa}^{\mu}(\tau)\,\kappa^{\nu}(\sigma)\rangle=\eta^{\mu\nu}\,\theta(\tau-\sigma)\;,\\
G_{\rm DN}(\tau,\sigma)&=|\tau-\sigma|+(\tau+\sigma)\;,\quad\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G_{\rm DN}(\tau,\sigma)=\text{sgn}(\tau-\sigma)+1\;,\end{split}

(3.2.52)

with the Dirichlet–Neumann propagator obeying
G_{\rm DN}(0,\sigma)=0
and
\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G_{\rm DN}(-\infty,\sigma)=0
.

As a first example, we compute the one-point function of an unintegrated vertex operator placed at
\tau=-T
, to be sent to infinity. The path integral over the fluctuations is normalized to one, and we denote correlation functions by
\langle\cdots\rangle_{\rm DN}
to remind us of the nontrivial boundary conditions. The correlator yields

\begin{split}\Big\langle{\cal V}_{\epsilon,k}(-T)\Big\rangle_{\rm DN}&=e^{ik\cdot x}\Big\langle\epsilon\cdot\big(\alpha+\kappa(-T)\big)\,e^{ik\cdot z(-T)}\bar{\cal C}(-T)\Big\rangle_{\rm DN}\\
&=e^{ik\cdot x}\epsilon\cdot\alpha\Big\langle e^{ik\cdot z(-T)}\bar{\cal C}(-T)\Big\rangle_{\rm DN}=\alpha^{\mu}\epsilon_{\mu}\,e^{ik\cdot x}e^{-iTk^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(3.2.53)

We see that, as for the infinite line, if the momentum of the asymptotic state is off-shell, the correlator vanishes in the limit
T\rightarrow\infty
, while for
k^{2}=0
it gives
\alpha^{\mu}\epsilon_{\mu}\,e^{ik\cdot x}
, thus reproducing a linear on-shell state.

As a more interesting application, we try to reproduce the nonlinear field
A_{23}^{\mu}
in (
3.2.46
) by inserting
{\cal V}_{3}(-\infty)
and integrating
V_{2}(\tau)
over the line. Since both describe asymptotic particles, the momenta are on-shell and their polarizations are transverse. Taking care of the ghost zero mode saturation, the correlator reads

\begin{split}i\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\!d\tau\,\Big\langle V_{2}(\tau){\cal V}_{3}(-\infty)\Big\rangle_{\rm DN}&=i\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\Big\langle\Big(i\epsilon_{2}\cdot\dot{z}(\tau)+4ik_{2}^{[\mu}\epsilon_{2}^{\nu]}\big(\alpha_{\mu}+\kappa_{\mu}(\tau)\big)\bar{\kappa}_{\nu}(\tau)\Big)e^{ik_{2}\cdot z(\tau)}\\
&\phantom{=}\times\epsilon_{3}\cdot\big(\alpha+\kappa(-\infty)\big)e^{ik_{3}\cdot z(-\infty)}\bar{\cal C}(-\infty)\Big\rangle_{\rm DN}\;.\end{split}

(3.2.54)

The contraction of the two plane waves yields the common factor

\big\langle e^{ik_{2}\cdot z(\tau)}e^{ik_{3}\cdot z(-\infty)}\big\rangle_{z}=e^{ik_{23}^{2}\tau}\;,

(3.2.55)

where the subscript refers to the path integral over
z
. The term proportional to
\alpha\cdot\epsilon_{3}
contributes as

\begin{split}i\alpha\cdot\epsilon_{3}\Big\langle e^{ik_{2}\cdot z(\tau)}e^{ik_{3}\cdot z(-\infty)}i\epsilon_{2}\cdot\dot{z}(\tau)\Big\rangle_{z}&=-\alpha\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{3}\,e^{ik_{23}^{2}\tau}\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G_{\rm DN}(\tau,-\infty)\\
&=-2\,\alpha\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{3}\,e^{ik_{23}^{2}\tau}\;,\end{split}

(3.2.56)

while the term with
\epsilon_{3}\cdot\kappa(-\infty)
yields

\begin{split}-4\,k_{2}^{[\mu}\epsilon_{2}^{\nu]}\Big\langle e^{ik_{2}\cdot z(\tau)}e^{ik_{3}\cdot z(-\infty)}\big(\alpha_{\mu}+\kappa_{\mu}(\tau)\big)\bar{\kappa}_{\nu}(\tau)\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot\kappa(-\infty)\Big\rangle_{z,\kappa}=-4\,k_{2}^{[\mu}\epsilon_{2}^{\nu]}\,\alpha_{\mu}\epsilon_{3\nu}\,e^{ik_{23}^{2}\tau}\;.\end{split}

(3.2.57)

Finally, integrating over
\tau
we obtain

\begin{split}i\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\!d\tau\,\Big\langle V_{2}(\tau){\cal V}_{3}(-\infty)\Big\rangle_{\rm DN}&=\frac{2i}{k_{23}^{2}}\,\alpha_{\mu}\Big(\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{3}\,\epsilon_{3}^{\mu}-\epsilon_{3}\cdot k_{2}\,\epsilon_{2}^{\mu}+k_{2}^{\mu}\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\Big)\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\\
&=\frac{1}{k_{23}^{2}}\,\alpha_{\mu}\Big(m_{2}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})+i\,k_{23}^{\mu}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\Big)\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\\
&=\alpha_{\mu}\,\Big(A_{23}^{\mu}(x)+\Delta^{\mu}\lambda_{23}(x)\Big)=\alpha_{\mu}\,\tilde{A}_{23}^{\mu}(x)\;,\end{split}

(3.2.58)

with the composite gauge parameter

\lambda_{23}(x)=\frac{1}{k_{23}^{2}}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\;.

(3.2.59)

We have thus shown that the path integral with Dirichlet–Neumann boundary conditions reproduces the desired field
A_{23}^{\mu}
, modulo a linear gauge transformation.
We conclude by noting that, in the computation of the four-gluon amplitude, the difference between
\tilde{A}_{23}^{\mu}
and
A_{23}^{\mu}
drops from the correlation function, thus resulting in the same amplitude.

3.3
Ward identities

To establish that the amplitudes computed with this method are gauge invariant, we have to discuss the BRST symmetry of the path integral. Let us recall that to compute amplitudes we use the free action

S_{\text{free}}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\!\!\!d\tau\Big[\tfrac{1}{4}\,\dot{x}^{2}-i\,\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}\dot{\alpha}^{\mu}+iB_{i}\dot{C}^{i}\Big]\;,

(3.3.1)

with boundary conditions

\dot{x}^{\mu}(\pm\infty)=0\;,\quad\alpha^{\mu}(+\infty)=0\;,\quad\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(-\infty)=0\;,\quad B_{i}(\pm\infty)=0\;.

(3.3.2)

The above action with these boundary conditions is invariant under the BRST transformations

\begin{split}sx^{\mu}&=c\,\dot{x}^{\mu}-i({\cal C}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}+\bar{\cal C}\alpha^{\mu})\;,\\
s\alpha^{\mu}&=\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\cal C}\dot{x}^{\mu}\;,\quad s\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\bar{\cal C}\dot{x}^{\mu}\;,\\
sc&=i\,{\cal C}\bar{\cal C}\;,\quad s{\cal C}=0\;,\quad s\bar{\cal C}=0\;,\\
sb&=\tfrac{i}{4}\,\dot{x}^{2}\;,\quad s{\cal B}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\alpha\cdot\dot{x}-i\,{\cal C}b\;,\quad s\bar{\cal B}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\bar{\alpha}\cdot\dot{x}+i\,\bar{\cal C}b\;.\end{split}

(3.3.3)

These transformations can be modified by adding trivial terms that vanish on-shell as, for instance,
c\dot{c}
which can be added to
sc
. In (
3.3.3
) we chose the simplest form, with no such terms. The BRST differential so defined is nilpotent only on-shell:
s^{2}\approx 0
, where by
\approx
we denote equality up to equations of motion. This is expected from the gauge-fixed theory in Lagrangian form. If one reintroduces momenta
p_{\mu}
, the differential
s
can be made nilpotent off-shell. The Noether charge corresponding to the BRST symmetry is given by

Q=-\tfrac{1}{4}\,c\,\dot{x}^{2}+\tfrac{i}{2}\,({\cal C}\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\cal C}\alpha)\cdot\dot{x}-{\cal C}\bar{\cal C}\,b\;,

(3.3.4)

which yields the free BRST operator (
3.1.43
) upon canonical quantization. In the path integral formulation, one can use the charge
Q
inside correlation functions to generate BRST transformations. More specifically, given a functional
F
, its BRST transformation is given by the equal time commutator

[Q,F](\tau)=i\big(sF\big)(\tau)\;.

(3.3.5)

3.3.1
Operator product expansion and equal-time commutators

To discuss equal time commutators and other operator relations in the path integral formulation, we start with a brief detour into the operator product expansion (
OPE
). Similar to string theory, we will call operator product the product of functions, viewed inside correlators in the path integral:

F(\tau)\,G(\sigma):=\big\langle\cdots F(\tau)\,G(\sigma)\cdots\big\rangle\;.

(3.3.6)

These are then computed using Wick contractions and the basic two-point functions. The simplest example is the
OPE
of two
z^{\mu}
fluctuations, yielding

z^{\mu}(\tau)\,z^{\nu}(\sigma)=-i\,\eta^{\mu\nu}\,G(\tau,\sigma)+:z^{\mu}(\tau)\,z^{\nu}(\sigma):\;.

(3.3.7)

The normal ordering symbol amounts to the prescription of not performing Wick contractions within it. This immediately leads to
\langle\,:\!F(z)\!:\,\rangle=F(0)
, where
F(z)
is an analytic function of
z^{\mu}
. Another simple example is the
OPE
of
\dot{z}^{\mu}
(related on shell to the momentum operator via
p^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\,\dot{z}^{\mu}
) with a normal-ordered function of
z
:

\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau)\,:F\big(z(\sigma)\big):\;=-i\,\left(\text{sgn}(\tau-\sigma)-\frac{2\tau}{T}\right):\Delta^{\mu}F\big(z(\sigma)\big):+:\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau)F\big(z(\sigma)\big):\;,

(3.3.8)

where we used the two-point function
\dot{\phantom{a}}\!G(\tau,\sigma)=\text{sgn}(\tau-\sigma)-2\tau/T
.
The
OPE
can then be used to define equal time commutators via the regularized prescription

\begin{split}[A,B](\tau)&=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\Big(A(\tau+\epsilon)-A(\tau-\epsilon)\Big)\,B(\tau)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\tau-\epsilon}^{\tau+\epsilon}\!\!\!\!\!\!d\sigma\;\dot{A}(\sigma)\,B(\tau)\\
&=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}A(\tau)\,\Big(B(\tau-\epsilon)-B(\tau+\epsilon)\Big)=-\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\tau-\epsilon}^{\tau+\epsilon}\!\!\!\!\!\!d\sigma\;A(\tau)\,\dot{B}(\sigma)\;,\end{split}

(3.3.9)

where the equivalence of the two definitions complies with the (graded) antisymmetry of the commutator.
An important point of the above definition is that one takes the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0

after
computing the
OPE
at separate points. In particular, this assumes that the
only
operators in the interval
[\tau-\epsilon,\tau+\epsilon]
are
A
and
B
, for otherwise the result would not yield just the commutator
[A,B](\tau)
. This is an important subtlety that will play a role in the following.

As an example, we can compute the commutator
[\dot{z}^{\mu},:\!F(z)\!:]
. Using (
3.3.8
) and the definition for the commutator we obtain

[\dot{z}^{\mu},:\!F(z)\!:](\tau)=-2i\,:\Delta^{\mu}F\big(z(\tau)\big):+\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\Big(:\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau+\epsilon)F\big(z(\tau)\big):-:\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau-\epsilon)F\big(z(\tau)\big):\Big)\;.

(3.3.10)

Naively,
\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau+\epsilon)=\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau)
, since
\ddot{z}^{\mu}(\tau)\approx 0
on-shell. Inside correlation functions,
\ddot{z}^{\mu}(\tau)
contributes only to contact terms, since
\ddot{z}(\tau)\,z(\sigma)\sim\delta(\tau-\sigma)
. Now, it is important that in the regularized definition (
3.3.9
) one assumes that no other operator sits in the interval
[\tau-\epsilon,\tau+\epsilon]
. This ensures that
\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}:\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau+\epsilon)F\big(z(\tau)\big):
is regular, finally yielding

[\dot{z}^{\mu},:\!F(z)\!:](\tau)=-2i\,:\Delta^{\mu}F\big(z(\tau)\big):\;,

(3.3.11)

which is the expected result. One can similarly define the operator product at coincident points via Weyl ordering:

(AB)(\tau):=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(A(\tau+\epsilon)+A(\tau-\epsilon)\Big)\,B(\tau)\;,

(3.3.12)

which reproduces the
OPE
computed with the two-point functions at coincident points. For instance, taking the coincidence limit of (
3.3.8
), one has

\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau)\,:F\big(z(\tau)\big):\;=\frac{2i\tau}{T}\,:\Delta^{\mu}F\big(z(\tau)\big):+:\dot{z}^{\mu}(\tau)F\big(z(\tau)\big):\;.

(3.3.13)

A second useful example is the commutator of
\dot{z}^{2}
with functions of
z
, since
\frac{1}{4}\,\dot{z}^{2}
corresponds to the gauge-fixed Hamiltonian. Let us mention that one has to use the normal ordered expression
:\dot{z}^{2}(\tau):
to avoid divergences from self-contractions. Keeping this in mind, we omit explicit normal ordering symbols in the initial functions. To compute the commutator, we evaluate the
OPE
at separate points:

\frac{1}{4}\,\dot{z}^{2}(\tau\pm\epsilon)\,F\big(z(\tau)\big)=-\frac{1}{4}\,\left(\pm 1-\frac{2\tau}{T}\right)^{2}:\square F(z):-\frac{i}{2}\,\left(\pm 1-\frac{2\tau}{T}\right)\,:\dot{z}^{\mu}\Delta_{\mu}F(z):+\frac{1}{4}\,:\dot{z}^{2}F(z):

(3.3.14)

where on the right-hand side all
z^{\mu}
are evaluated at
\tau
. Above, we have already taken the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, taking into account that it is regular inside normal ordered expressions. The commutator is readily evaluated as

\tfrac{1}{4}\,[\dot{z}^{2},F(z)]=\frac{2\tau}{T}\,:\square F(z):-i:\dot{z}^{\mu}\Delta_{\mu}F(z):\;,

(3.3.15)

where, again, all
z^{\mu}
are
z^{\mu}(\tau)
. The above expression may look somewhat unfamiliar because of the
\square F
contribution linear in time. However, looking at the result (
3.3.13
) one can see that this is exactly the Weyl ordered expression at coincident points, so that

\tfrac{1}{4}\,[\dot{z}^{2},F(z)]=-i\,\dot{z}^{\mu}\Delta_{\mu}F(z)=-i\,\frac{dF}{d\tau}\;,

(3.3.16)

which is the Heisenberg equation of motion.

3.3.2
BRST cohomology of vertex operators

We can now show that on-shell vertex operators with transverse gluons are elements of the BRST cohomology. We start by showing that vertex operators with polarization
\epsilon_{\mu}=ik_{\mu}
are trivial, manifesting the on-shell residual gauge symmetry. Recall the unintegrated vertex operator
{\cal V}_{\epsilon,k}(\tau)
:

\begin{split}{\cal V}_{\epsilon,k}(\tau)&=W_{\epsilon,k}(\tau)+c(\tau)\,V_{\epsilon,k}(\tau)\;,\\
W_{\epsilon,k}(\tau)&=\epsilon_{\mu}\Big({\cal C}(\tau)\,\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)+\bar{\cal C}(\tau)\,\alpha^{\mu}(\tau)\Big)\,e^{ik\cdot x(\tau)}\;,\\
V_{\epsilon,k}(\tau)&=i\,\epsilon_{\mu}\Big(\dot{x}^{\mu}(\tau)+2\,k\cdot\alpha(\tau)\,\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)-2\,k\cdot\bar{\alpha}(\tau)\,\alpha^{\mu}(\tau)\Big)\,e^{ik\cdot x(\tau)}\;.\end{split}

(3.3.17)

Taking the polarization to be proportional to momentum, we have

{\cal V}_{ik,k}(\tau)=i\big({\cal C}\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\cal C}\alpha\big)\cdot k\,e^{ik\cdot z}-c\,k\cdot\dot{z}\,e^{ik\cdot z}\;,

(3.3.18)

where we have removed the zero mode
x_{0}
, which in amplitudes only gives momentum conservation. Using the
OPE
as described in the previous section, we see that
{\cal V}_{ik,k}(\tau)
is BRST-exact:

{\cal V}_{ik,k}(\tau)=[Q,e^{ik\cdot z}](\tau)\;.

(3.3.19)

In a similar fashion, the integrated vertex operator
V_{ik,k}(\tau)
is a total derivative:

V_{ik,k}(\tau)=i\,\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(e^{ik\cdot z(\tau)}\right)\;.

(3.3.20)

Coming now to BRST closure, we compute the
Q
-commutator of
{\cal V}_{\epsilon,k}
. Using the on-shell condition and transversality, we obtain

\{Q,{\cal V}_{\epsilon,k}\}=-3\,:f_{\mu\nu}(z)\,\alpha^{\mu}\bar{\alpha}^{\nu}{\cal C}\bar{\cal C}:-2i\,:c\,({\cal C}\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\cal C}\alpha)\cdot k\,f_{\mu\nu}(z)\,\alpha^{\mu}\bar{\alpha}^{\nu}:\;,

(3.3.21)

where
f_{\mu\nu}(z)=2i\,k_{[\mu}\epsilon_{\nu]}e^{ik\cdot z}
. This is nothing but the functional version of (
3.1.45
). We will now show that the right-hand side above vanishes inside all correlation functions. In particular, we want to prove that

\big\langle\cdots:F(\tau)\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)\bar{\cal C}(\tau):\cdots\big\rangle=0\;,\quad\langle\cdots:F(\tau)\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)\bar{\alpha}^{\nu}(\tau):\cdots\rangle=0\;,

(3.3.22)

for any functional
F(\tau)
and with arbitrary insertions of vertex operators outside the normal ordering. This is the path integral version of the statement, discussed in sec.
3.1.3
, that normal ordered operators with more than one barred oscillator annihilate every state in the
{\cal N}=1
sector of the Hilbert space.

To prove the first relation, we first notice that the equation of motion
\Delta_{\tau}{\bar{\cal C}}=0
holds everywhere on the line, since there are no insertions of the conjugate antighost
{\cal B}
. We can then push the ghost to the infinite past
\bar{\cal C}(\tau)=\bar{\cal C}(-\infty)
, where it saturates its zero mode. Now we write
\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)
as

\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)=\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}d\sigma\,\Delta_{\sigma}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\sigma)\;,

(3.3.23)

using the boundary condition
\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(-\infty)=0
. The integral above is zero almost everywhere, since the equation of motion
\Delta_{\tau}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}=0
holds except at insertions of
\alpha^{\nu}
, where it gives a contraction with a delta function. At this stage, we need to invoke the
U(1)
invariance of the vertex operators: every insertion of
\alpha^{\nu}
necessarily comes with either a ghost
\bar{\cal C}
, or another
\bar{\alpha}^{\rho}
. In the first case, the insertion does not contribute, since there is already a factor of
\bar{\cal C}
. In the second case, the above integral produces a factor of
\bar{\alpha}^{\rho}
at the insertion point. One then starts the same procedure with
\bar{\alpha}^{\rho}
until it reaches the asymptotic past, which concludes the proof. An analogous reasoning can be used to prove the second equation in (
3.3.22
).

Since (
3.3.22
) holds in all correlation functions, we use it as an operator equation in the sense of
OPE
.
We have thus shown that, inside correlation functions,
the unintegrated vertex operators are
Q
-closed:

\{Q,{\cal V}_{\epsilon,k}\}=0\;.

(3.3.24)

Using the same arguments, in particular that equations of motion hold when regularizing commutators by point splitting, one shows that the
Q
-commutator of the integrated vertex operator is a total derivative:

[Q,V_{\epsilon,k}]=i\,\frac{d}{d\tau}\,{\cal V}_{\epsilon,k}\;.

(3.3.25)

In the following, we will show how to use these relations to prove Ward identities.

3.3.3
Ward identities from BRST invariance

To see how to prove Ward identities, we start from the somewhat trivial example of the three-point amplitude
{\cal A}_{123}
.
For transverse gluons, the amplitude (
3.2.16
)

{\cal A}_{123}=i\,\Big[\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\,\epsilon_{3}\cdot(k_{1}-k_{2})+\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{1}\cdot(k_{2}-k_{3})+\epsilon_{3}\cdot\epsilon_{1}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot(k_{3}-k_{1})\Big]\;,

(3.3.26)

vanishes when taking any polarization
\epsilon^{\mu}_{i}
to be proportional to the corresponding momentum
k^{\mu}_{i}
, which is the Ward identity. In the worldline formulation, the amplitude is given by the correlator

{\cal A}_{123}=\big\langle{\cal V}_{1}(+\infty)\,{\cal V}_{2}(0)\,{\cal V}_{3}(-\infty)\big\rangle\;.

(3.3.27)

If we take the particle 1 to have
\epsilon^{\mu}_{1}=ik^{\mu}_{1}
, the corresponding vertex operator is exact:
{\cal V}_{1}(\tau)=[Q,e^{ik_{1}\cdot z}](\tau)
. The asymptotic vacuum
\ket{1}
is BRST invariant, which translates to the boundary condition
Q(\pm\infty)=0
. Since the vertex operator
{\cal V}_{1}
is inserted at
T/2\rightarrow+\infty
, the equal time commutator reduces to

{\cal V}_{1}(T/2)=-\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}e^{ik_{1}\cdot z(T/2)}Q(T/2-\epsilon)\;.

(3.3.28)

The idea is to “move” the BRST charge to the asymptotic past, where it annihilates the vacuum. To do so, we use the fact that
Q
is conserved, so that
\dot{Q}=0
except at insertions of other operators. Since there is no operator insertion until
\tau=0
, we have
Q(T/2-\epsilon)=Q(\tau_{*})
for any
\tau_{*}\in(0,T/2-\epsilon]
. To move
Q
across
{\cal V}_{2}(0)
we pick a commutator contribution at
\tau=0
. After that,
Q(\tau)
remains constant for negative times until it approaches
{\cal V}_{3}(-T/2)
:

Q(T/2-\epsilon)\,{\cal V}_{2}(0)=-{\cal V}_{2}(0)\,Q(-T/2+\epsilon)+\{Q,{\cal V}_{2}\}(0)=-{\cal V}_{2}(0)\,Q(-T/2+\epsilon)\;,

(3.3.29)

upon using the closure of
{\cal V}_{2}(0)
. Since
{\cal V}_{3}
is at the boundary, and
Q(-\infty)=0
, approaching it from the left coincides with the commutator:

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}Q(-T/2+\epsilon)\,{\cal V}_{3}(-T/2)=\{Q,{\cal V}_{3}\}(-T/2)=0\;,

(3.3.30)

which proves the Ward identity
{\cal A}_{k_{1}23}=0
.

For the next example, we consider the four-point amplitude, computed with the smart worldline:

{\cal A}_{1234}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\!\!\!\!\!\!d\tau\,\Big\langle{\cal V}_{2}(+\infty)\,{\cal V}_{3}(0)\,V_{1}(\tau)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\;.

(3.3.31)

Upon taking
\epsilon_{2}^{\mu}=ik_{2}^{\mu}
we have again that the corresponding vertex operator is
Q
-exact:

{\cal V}_{2}(T/2)=-\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}e^{ik_{2}\cdot z(T/2)}Q(T/2-\epsilon)\;.

(3.3.32)

We can use the same arguments to push
Q
to the asymptotic past, except when it has to go through the integrated vertex
V_{1}(\tau)
, picking a contribution from the commutator. Upon regularizing the asymptotic time with
T/2\rightarrow+\infty
we obtain

\begin{split}-\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\,&\Big\langle e^{ik_{2}\cdot z(T/2)}Q(T/2-\epsilon)\,{\cal V}_{3}(0)\,V_{1}(\tau)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-T/2)\Big\rangle\\
&=\Big\langle e^{ik_{2}\cdot z(T/2)}{\cal V}_{3}(0)\,[Q,V_{1}](\tau)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-T/2)\Big\rangle=i\,\Big\langle e^{ik_{2}\cdot z(T/2)}{\cal V}_{3}(0)\,\dot{\cal V}_{1}(\tau)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-T/2)\Big\rangle\;,\end{split}

(3.3.33)

where we used (
3.3.25
). Upon integrating over
\tau
, we end up with

{\cal A}_{1k_{2}34}=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}i\,\Big\langle e^{ik_{2}\cdot z(T/2)}{\cal V}_{3}(0)\,\big({\cal V}_{1}(T/2)-{\cal V}_{1}(-T/2)\big)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-T/2)\Big\rangle

(3.3.34)

where the notation
{\cal A}_{1k_{2}34}
highlights the use of a longitudinal polarization for particle 2.
Regardless of the detailed structure, the above expression vanishes in the limit
T\rightarrow\infty
, because it has off-shell plane waves at asymptotic times. To see this, let us focus on the first term above with
{\cal V}_{1}(T/2)
. The Wick contraction of the plane waves gives

\big\langle e^{i(k_{1}+k_{2})\cdot z(T/2)}e^{ik_{3}\cdot z(0)}e^{ik_{4}\cdot z(-T/2)}\big\rangle=\exp\left\{-\tfrac{i}{2}\,(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}T\right\}\xrightarrow{T\rightarrow\infty}0\;,

(3.3.35)

since
(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}=s\neq 0
. We thus see that the Ward identity
{\cal A}_{1k_{2}34}=0
holds, thanks to the unrestricted integration range of
V_{1}(\tau)
. From this discussion, one can also see that off-shell correlation functions (for which
T
is a modulus) do not obey linear Ward identities.

3.3.3.1
Ward identity for the color-adjacent worldline

Using the not-so-smart worldline connecting particles 1 and 4, the four-gluon color-ordered amplitude requires three separate correlation functions. Recalling that every vertex (except the ones at infinity) requires an additional factor of
i
, these are given by (
3.2.32
), (
3.2.33
) and (
3.2.34
)

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{I}}

\displaystyle=(i)^{2}\Big\langle{\cal V}_{1}(+\infty)\,\int_{0}^{+\infty}{\hskip-17.07164ptd\tau}\,V_{2}(\tau)\,{\cal V}_{3}(0)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\;,

(3.3.36)

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{II}}

\displaystyle=i\,\Big\langle{\cal V}_{1}(+\infty)\,{\cal V}_{23}(0)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\;,

(3.3.37)

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{III}}

\displaystyle=i\,\Big\langle{\cal V}_{1}(+\infty)\,{\cal V}_{23}^{\rm pinch}(0)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\;.

(3.3.38)

As we have discussed in the previous section, the second contribution
{\cal A}_{1234}^{\mathrm{II}}
comes from the insertion of the quadratic vertex operator

{\cal V}_{23}(\tau)=4\,c(\tau)\,\epsilon^{[\mu}_{2}\epsilon_{3}^{\nu]}\alpha_{\mu}(\tau)\,\bar{\alpha}_{\nu}(\tau)\,e^{i(k_{2}+k_{3})\cdot x(\tau)}\;,

(3.3.39)

corresponding to the quartic vertex. The third contribution, instead, comes from “pinching” the gluons 2 and 3 into a cubic subtree attached to the worldline. This is done by inserting an unintegrated vertex operator (
3.1.58
), corresponding to a nonlinear field with momentum
k_{23}^{\mu}=k^{\mu}_{2}+k^{\mu}_{3}
and composite polarization

\epsilon_{23}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{k_{23}^{2}}\,m_{2}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\;,

(3.3.40)

written in terms of the product (
3.2.26
).

The necessity for the second and third contributions to the amplitude can be understood from studying its Ward identities. To this end, we take the first gluon to have the pure gauge polarization
\epsilon_{1}^{\mu}=ik_{1}^{\mu}
, so that
{\cal V}_{1}=[Q,e^{ik_{1}\cdot x}]
. Following the procedure described before, the three correlation functions contribute as

\begin{split}{\cal A}_{k_{1}234}^{\mathrm{I}}&=i\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!d\tau\,\Big\langle e^{ik_{1}\cdot z(+\infty)}\,\frac{d}{d\tau}{\cal V}_{2}(\tau)\,{\cal V}_{3}(0)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle=-i\,\Big\langle e^{ik_{1}\cdot z(+\infty)}\,{\cal V}_{2}(0)\,{\cal V}_{3}(0)\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\;,\\
{\cal A}_{k_{1}234}^{\mathrm{II}}&=-i\,\Big\langle e^{ik_{1}\cdot z(+\infty)}\,\{Q,{\cal V}_{23}(0)\}\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\;,\\
{\cal A}_{k_{1}234}^{\mathrm{III}}&=-i\,\Big\langle e^{ik_{1}\cdot z(+\infty)}\,\{Q,{\cal V}_{23}^{\rm pinch}(0)\}\,{\cal V}_{4}(-\infty)\Big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(3.3.41)

Compared to the smart worldline, the first correlator does not vanish, since the
\tau
integral receives a boundary contribution from
{\cal V}_{2}(\tau)
colliding with
{\cal V}_{3}(0)
. Instead of computing the full correlation functions, we will determine the
OPE

{\cal V}_{2}(0){\cal V}_{3}(0)
at coinciding points, and show that it cancels
\{Q,{\cal V}_{23}(0)+{\cal V}_{23}^{\rm pinch}(0)\}
.

For later convenience, let us rewrite the unintegrated vertex operator in the compact form

{\cal V}_{i}=\epsilon^{\mu}_{i}\big(S_{\mu}+i\,c\,\dot{x}_{\mu}\big)e^{ik_{i}\cdot x}+2i\,c\,k^{\mu}_{i}\epsilon^{\nu}_{i}\,S_{\mu\nu}\,e^{ik_{i}\cdot x}\;,

(3.3.42)

where the time dependence is left implicit and we have introduced the ghost-number one vector
S^{\mu}
and the Lorentz spin generator
S^{\mu\nu}
, defined by

S^{\mu}=\bar{\cal C}\alpha^{\mu}+{\cal C}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}\;,\quad S^{\mu\nu}=\alpha^{\mu}\bar{\alpha}^{\nu}-\alpha^{\nu}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}\;.

(3.3.43)

Notice that, for
k_{i}^{2}=0
and
k_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{i}=0
, the vertex operator is normal ordered.
In computing the
OPE
, we will leave equal-time products of
\dot{x}^{\mu}
and functions of
x^{\mu}
as they are, without taking contractions. This corresponds to the Weyl ordering of the related operators. For instance, the product
e^{ik_{i}\cdot x(\tau)}e^{ik_{j}\cdot x(\tau)}=e^{i(k_{i}+k_{j})\cdot x(\tau)}
is Weyl ordered, not normal ordered, unless the propagator
G(\tau,\tau)
vanishes. For equal-time products of
\alpha^{\mu}
and
\bar{\alpha}^{\nu}
we will use instead the
OPE
at coincident times

\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)\,\alpha^{\nu}(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\,\eta^{\mu\nu}+:\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)\,\alpha^{\nu}(\tau):\;,

(3.3.44)

to take advantage of the fact that normal ordered expressions with more than one barred field vanish in all correlation functions due to charge conservation, c.f. (
3.3.22
). Some useful examples are

\begin{split}S^{\mu}S^{\nu}&=-:{\cal C}\bar{\cal C}(\alpha^{\mu}\bar{\alpha}^{\nu}-\alpha^{\nu}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}):\;=0\;,\\
S^{\mu\nu}S^{\rho}&=\tilde{S}^{[\mu}\eta^{\nu]\rho}+:S^{\mu\nu}S^{\rho}:\;=\tilde{S}^{[\mu}\eta^{\nu]\rho}\;,\end{split}

(3.3.45)

where in the final results we have discarded all terms vanishing inside correlators, and we have defined a second ghost-number one vector

\tilde{S}^{\mu}=\bar{\cal C}\alpha^{\mu}-{\cal C}\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}\;.

(3.3.46)

Armed with these relations, we can determine the equal-time
OPE
of the two vertex operators
{\cal V}_{2}(0)
and
{\cal V}_{3}(0)
. Omitting the time dependence, we have

\begin{split}{\cal V}_{2}{\cal V}_{3}&=\Big(\epsilon^{\mu}_{2}\big(S_{\mu}+i\,c\,\dot{x}_{\mu}\big)+2i\,c\,k^{\mu}_{2}\epsilon^{\nu}_{2}\,S_{\mu\nu}\Big)\Big(\epsilon^{\rho}_{3}\big(S_{\rho}+i\,c\,\dot{x}_{\rho}\big)+2i\,c\,k^{\rho}_{3}\epsilon^{\sigma}_{3}\,S_{\rho\sigma}\Big)e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\\
&=2i\,c\Big(\epsilon^{[\mu}_{2}\epsilon^{\nu]}_{3}\dot{x}_{\mu}S_{\nu}+k_{2}^{[\mu}\epsilon_{2}^{\nu]}\tilde{S}_{\mu}\epsilon_{3\nu}-k_{3}^{[\mu}\epsilon_{3}^{\nu]}\tilde{S}_{\mu}\epsilon_{2\nu}\Big)e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\\
&=c\,\tilde{S}_{\mu}\,m_{2}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}+2i\,c\,\epsilon^{[\mu}_{2}\epsilon^{\nu]}_{3}\Big(\dot{x}_{\mu}S_{\nu}+\tilde{S}_{\mu}k_{23\nu}\Big)e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\;,\end{split}

(3.3.47)

The quadratic vertex operator (
3.3.39
) can be written as

{\cal V}_{23}=2\,c\,\epsilon^{\mu}_{2}\epsilon^{\nu}_{3}\,S_{\mu\nu}\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\;,

(3.3.48)

and its equal-time commutator with
Q
can be computed as
\{Q,{\cal V}_{23}\}=is({\cal V}_{23})
, where the BRST differential
s
on the worldline variables is given by (
3.3.3
). Its action on functions is defined by the Leibniz rule, with the resulting expressions corresponding to Weyl ordering. Discarding again all terms with two barred fields in normal ordering, we obtain

is({\cal V}_{23})=-2i\,c\,\epsilon^{[\mu}_{2}\epsilon^{\nu]}_{3}\Big(\dot{x}_{\mu}S_{\nu}+\tilde{S}_{\mu}k_{23\nu}\Big)e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\;,

(3.3.49)

yielding the
OPE
result

{\cal V}_{2}{\cal V}_{3}+\{Q,{\cal V}_{23}\}=c\,\tilde{S}_{\mu}\,m_{2}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\;,

(3.3.50)

where all operators above are evaluated at equal times.

At this stage, the missing ingredient to establish the Ward identity is to prove that the right-hand side of (
3.3.50
) is
Q
-exact and, in particular, given by

c\,\tilde{S}_{\mu}\,m_{2}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}=-\{Q,{\cal V}_{23}^{\rm pinch}\}\;.

(3.3.51)

To this end, we consider the vertex operator for an off-shell field

{\cal V}_{A}=A^{\mu}(k)\big(S_{\mu}+i\,c\,\dot{x}_{\mu}\big)e^{ik\cdot x}+2i\,c\,k^{\mu}A^{\nu}(k)\,S_{\mu\nu}\,e^{ik\cdot x}\;,

(3.3.52)

where in general
k^{2}\neq 0
and
k\cdot A(k)\neq 0
. Its
Q
-commutator is readily computed, yielding

\{Q,{\cal V}_{A}\}=c\,\tilde{S}^{\mu}\big(-k^{2}A_{\mu}+k_{\mu}k\cdot A\big)\,e^{ik\cdot x}\;.

(3.3.53)

For the case at hand,
{\cal V}_{23}^{\rm pinch}:={\cal V}_{A_{23}}
is of the form (
3.3.52
) for a composite field
A_{23}^{\mu}
, where the momentum
k_{23}^{\mu}=k_{2}^{\mu}+k_{3}^{\mu}
is off-shell and the polarization is given by

\epsilon_{23}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{k_{23}^{2}}\,m_{2}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}):=\frac{i}{k_{23}^{2}}\,\big(\epsilon_{2}\cdot k_{3}\,\epsilon_{3}^{\mu}-\epsilon_{3}\cdot k_{2}\,\epsilon_{2}^{\mu}+(k_{2}-k_{3})^{\mu}\,\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}\big)\;.

(3.3.54)

One can easily check that
k_{23}\cdot m_{2}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})=0
(this is essentially the Ward identity for the three-point case), so that (
3.3.53
) yields

\begin{split}\{Q,{\cal V}_{23}^{\rm pinch}\}&=c\,\tilde{S}_{\mu}\big(-k_{23}^{2}\epsilon_{23}^{\mu}+k_{23}^{\mu}k_{23}\cdot\epsilon_{23}\big)\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}=c\,\tilde{S}_{\mu}\big(-k_{23}^{2}\epsilon_{23}^{\mu}\big)\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\\
&=-c\,\tilde{S}_{\mu}\,m_{2}^{\mu}(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\,e^{ik_{23}\cdot x}\;,\end{split}

(3.3.55)

thus proving (
3.3.51
) and hence the Ward identity.

Let us conclude by emphasizing that the above discussion demonstrates how the form of the composite field (
3.2.46
) can also be inferred (albeit in a somewhat roundabout way) by demanding the Ward identities. Moreover, as expected, the Ward identity remains unchanged whether one uses
A_{23}^{\mu}
or its gauge-transformed version
\tilde{A}_{23}^{\mu}
(
3.2.58
), since the two fields differ by a gauge transformation:

\{Q,{\cal V}_{\tilde{A}_{23}}\}=\{Q,{\cal V}_{A_{23}}\}+\{Q,[Q,\lambda_{23}]\}=\{Q,{\cal V}_{A_{23}}\}\;,

(3.3.56)

where
\lambda_{23}(x)
is given by (
3.2.59
) and we have used
\{Q,Q\}=0
together with the graded Jacobi identity of (anti)commutators.

3.4
Final remarks

In this chapter, we have quantized the bosonic spinning particle on the open worldline, with the aim of providing a first-quantized description of gluon scattering amplitudes. To this end, we considered an open worldline of infinite proper length
[
109
,
203
,
90
,
210
]
, with boundary conditions corresponding to zero-momentum asymptotic vacua. In this way, all external gluons (including the two at the asymptotic endpoints of the line) are described by insertions of suitable vertex operators, thus mimicking the worldsheet techniques of string perturbation theory. In particular, the infinite length of the line allowed us to bypass the LSZ procedure
[
210
,
87
]
, in that the correlation functions already provide the on-shell and amputated amplitudes. Contrary to string theory, however, linear vertex operators are not sufficient to compute arbitrary amplitudes. In general, one needs nonlinear vertex operators, describing the insertion of entire subtrees on the main worldline. While this is typically achieved by using the so-called Bern-Kosower pinching rules
[
73
,
71
,
75
,
226
]
, here we have argued that these nonlinear vertex operators can be obtained as well from worldline path integrals on a semi-infinite open geometry
[
87
]
. Finally, to test our results, we have shown how the Ward identities of the scattering amplitudes descend from the BRST invariance of the worldline correlators (see Ref.
[
12
]
for a related discussion).

The framework presented here should be extended to arbitrary multiplicities and, possibly, to loop amplitudes. In particular, one would like to be able to prove gauge invariance of arbitrary amplitudes by extending the BRST analysis introduced here to general correlation functions. Besides the point of principle, having a first-quantized description of Yang–Mills amplitudes could help in shedding light on the so-called duality between color and kinematics in gauge theories
[
66
,
68
]
, which underpins the double copy construction of gravity amplitudes
[
69
,
67
,
5
]
. More specifically, it would prove beneficial to connect the off-shell algebraic approach of Refs.
[
82
,
89
,
85
]
to the worldline techniques employed in Refs.
[
10
,
9
]
, where an algorithm combining worldline integration by parts and Bern-Kosower pinching rules was shown to produce gauge theory numerators obeying color-kinematics duality.
Since the bosonic spinning particle can also accommodate gravitons in its spectrum, it would be interesting to extend the present framework to the spin-two sector of the theory. There, one expects to need multi-graviton vertices of arbitrary order, but the linear ones should essentially be the product of two gluon vertex operators, thus showing a worldline incarnation of the double copy, along the lines of Refs.
[
232
,
45
]
.

Appendix

Appendix 3.A
Path integral in phase space

To study the propagator directly in momentum space, we wish to consider the path integral representation of the matrix element

\langle p^{\prime}|e^{-iHT}|p\rangle=\int DpDx\ e^{iS}\;,

(3.A.1)

where one could take
H=p^{2}
as needed in the main text. We study the simpler case

H=0
, which already captures all the essential aspects of our discussion.
For simplicity, we consider just one target space dimension.
Then, the left-hand side of (
3.A.1
) reduces to

\langle p^{\prime}|p\rangle=2\pi\delta(p-p^{\prime})\;,

(3.A.2)

which should be reproduced by the path integral on the right-hand side. To take into account the
boundary conditions, that should be imposed on
p(\tau)
only, the action takes the form

S=\int_{0}^{T}d\tau\,(-\dot{p}x)\;.

(3.A.3)

Of course, one could perform the path integral by first integrating over
p
and then over
x
, which works well
for nontrivial quadratic Hamiltonians, like the free one,
H=p^{2}
. This is well-known.
Here we wish to path integrate in
x
first, leaving the path integral on
p
as the last one.
To do this, we notice that the paths
x(\tau)
should not contain any boundary conditions, so that we can
expand them by extracting the constant path
x_{0}
. That is, we parametrize

x(\tau)=x_{0}+\tilde{x}(\tau)

(3.A.4)

with
\int_{0}^{T}d\tau\,\tilde{x}(\tau)=0
.
Then, we can write

\int Dp\,D\tilde{x}\,dx_{0}\ e^{-i\int_{0}^{T}\!d\tau\,\dot{p}x}=\int Dp\,D\tilde{x}\,\underbrace{dx_{0}\,\ e^{-ix_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\!d\tau\,\dot{p}}}_{\sim\delta(p-p^{\prime})}e^{-i\int_{0}^{T}\!d\tau\,\dot{p}\tilde{x}}\;,

(3.A.5)

where
p
and
p^{\prime}
denote the boundary conditions that must be imposed on
p(\tau)
.
Of course,
\int_{0}^{T}\!d\tau\,\dot{p}=p^{\prime}-p
.
Then, path integrating over
\tilde{x}(\tau)
produces a delta functional
\delta(\dot{p})

leading to

\int Dp\,Dx\ e^{-i\int_{0}^{T}\!d\tau\,\dot{p}x}\ \sim\ \delta(p-p^{\prime})\int Dp\,\delta(\dot{p})\ \sim\ \text{Det}(\partial_{\tau})\delta(p-p^{\prime})\ \sim\ 2\pi\delta(p-p^{\prime})\;,

(3.A.6)

where the combined unfixed constants have been chosen to match the expected result in (
3.A.2
).
This way of computing the path integral allows us to bypass configuration space and obtain
results directly in momentum space. Presumably, perturbation theory can be introduced by building further
on the above expression.

Let us also briefly outline the derivation of the other “phase-space” path integral involved in our derivation, the one for the bosonic oscillators,

\displaystyle Z(\alpha,\bar{\alpha},\theta)=\int_{\bar{\alpha}(0)=\bar{\alpha}}^{\alpha(1)=\alpha}\!\!\!D\bar{\alpha}D\alpha\ e^{\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\,\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\partial_{\tau}-i\theta)\alpha_{\mu}(\tau)+\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}\alpha_{\mu}(0)}=e^{e^{-i\theta}\alpha\cdot\bar{\alpha}}\ ,

(3.A.7)

which was, for example, discussed and used in Ref.
[
11
]
. It corresponds to the usual (Euclidean) path integral for the harmonic oscillator, though with an imaginary frequency
\omega=-i\theta
.

For
\theta=0
, the latter reduces to the path integral representation of the scalar product of the coherent states,

\displaystyle\langle\alpha|\bar{\alpha}\rangle=e^{\alpha\cdot\bar{\alpha}}=\int_{\bar{\alpha}(0)=\bar{\alpha}}^{\alpha(1)=\alpha}\!\!\!D\bar{\alpha}D\alpha\ e^{\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\,\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}\dot{\alpha}_{\mu}(\tau)+\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}\alpha_{\mu}(0)}\;,

(3.A.8)

which can be used to fix the path integral normalization as follows. Let us split the fields into background parts – which satisfy the equations of motion
\dot{\alpha}^{\mu}=\dot{\bar{\alpha}}^{\mu}=0
and the boundary conditions – and quantum fluctuations as

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mu}(\tau)=\alpha^{\mu}+\kappa^{\mu}(\tau)\,,\quad\kappa^{\mu}(1)=0\;,

(3.A.9)

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)=\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}+\bar{\kappa}^{\mu}(\tau)\,,\quad\bar{\kappa}^{\mu}(0)=0\ .

(3.A.10)

Thus, we get

\displaystyle e^{\alpha\cdot\bar{\alpha}}=e^{\alpha\cdot\bar{\alpha}}\int_{\bar{\kappa}(0)=0}^{\kappa(1)=0}\!\!\!D\bar{\kappa}D\kappa\ e^{\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\,\bar{\kappa}^{\mu}\dot{\kappa}_{\mu}(\tau)}\ ,

(3.A.11)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\int_{\bar{\kappa}(0)=0}^{\kappa(1)=0}\!\!\!D\bar{\kappa}D\kappa\ e^{\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\,\bar{\kappa}^{\mu}\dot{\kappa}_{\mu}(\tau)}=1\ .

(3.A.12)

We can now apply the background splitting to the
\theta
-dependent path integral. We have,

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mu}(\tau)=e^{i\theta\tau}\big(e^{-i\theta}\alpha^{\mu}+\kappa^{\mu}(\tau)\big)\,,\quad\kappa^{\mu}(1)=0\;,

(3.A.13)

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}(\tau)=e^{-i\theta\tau}\big(\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}+\bar{\kappa}^{\mu}(\tau)\big)\,,\quad\bar{\kappa}^{\mu}(0)=0\ ,

(3.A.14)

and, in turn,

\displaystyle Z(\alpha,\bar{\alpha},\theta)=e^{e^{-i\theta}\alpha\cdot\bar{\alpha}}\int_{\bar{\kappa}(0)=0}^{\kappa(1)=0}\!\!\!D\bar{\kappa}D\kappa\ e^{\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\,\bar{\kappa}^{\mu}\dot{\kappa}_{\mu}(\tau)}=e^{e^{-i\theta}\alpha\cdot\bar{\alpha}}\ .

(3.A.15)

One may consult Ref.
[
11
]
for further details.

Spin 2

Chapter 4
BRST quantization of the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
spinning particle

In this chapter, we turn to the description of both massless and massive spin-2 particles within the Worldline Formalism, with the aim of illustrating one of the contexts where the method arguably proves most powerful: the perturbative evaluation of the Seeley–DeWitt coefficients. As a preliminary step towards these computations, we devote the present chapter to the BRST quantization of the worldline model known as the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
supersymmetric spinning particle, with and without a mass. The massless case is reviewed following Ref.
[
81
]
, both to introduce the model and to highlight the subtleties relevant for the calculations of Chapter
5
. We then move on to the massive case, presenting in detail the model employed in the computations of Chapter
6
. Our treatment serves a twofold purpose: first, to explore different mechanisms for endowing spinning particle models with mass; and second, to formulate a first-quantized description of linearized massive gravity, both in flat and in curved backgrounds. In the latter case, we find that the nilpotency of the BRST charge is preserved only on Einstein spacetimes with vanishing cosmological constant, which thus emerges as the unique consistent background. This will be the starting point for the computations developed in the following chapters.

Conventions

The target space of our worldline model is a
d
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime, equipped with the metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{diag}(-,+,\cdots,+)
, which is used to raise and lower spacetime indices. Greek letters
\mu,\nu,\dots
denote spacetime indices
(\mu,\nu=0,1,\dots,d-1)
, while capital Latin letters
I,J,\dots
are reserved for internal
SO({\mathcal{N}})
indices
(I,J=1,\dots,{\mathcal{N}})
.

We further employ the following notation for the graded commutators:

[\cdot,\cdot\}=\begin{cases}\{\cdot,\cdot\}\quad\text{anticommutator if both variables are fermionic,}\\
[\cdot,\cdot]\quad\;\;\text{commutator otherwise.}\end{cases}

4.1
O({\mathcal{N}})
massless spinning particles

Before focusing on the
\mathcal{N}=4
spinning particle, we first recall some general features of spinning particle models, which have already been discussed in the
introductory section on the Worldline Formalism
. In this section, we summarize the relevant equations for two main reasons: first, to facilitate comparison with the forthcoming massive case; and second, to re-establish our notation, since, unlike in the previous chapters, the spin degrees of freedom are now described by Grassmann-odd (fermionic) variables.

The phase space action of such models depends on the particle spacetime coordinates
x^{\mu}
together with
{\cal N}
real fermionic superpartners
\Xi^{\mu}_{I}
, and the phase-space action is given by

S=\int d\tau\left[p_{\mu}\dot{x}^{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}\Xi_{\mu}\cdot\dot{\Xi}^{\mu}-e\,H-i\chi^{I}\,q_{I}-a^{IJ}\,J_{IJ}\right]\ ,

(4.1.1)

The canonical coordinates
(x^{\mu},p_{\mu},\Xi^{\mu}_{I})\,
upon quantization, are subject to the canonical commutation relations (CCR)

[x^{\mu},p_{\nu}]=i\,\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\ ,\quad\{\Xi^{\mu}_{I},\Xi^{\nu}_{J}\}=\delta_{IJ}\,\eta^{\mu\nu}\ .

(4.1.2)

The worldline supergravity multiplet in one dimension
(e,\chi^{I},a_{IJ})
contains the einbein
e
, the
{\mathcal{N}}
gravitinos
\chi
, and the gauge field
a
. On the other hand, the first-class constraints are represented by the Hamiltonian
H
, the supercharges
q_{I}
, and the
R
-symmetry algebra generators
J_{IJ}
. They can be expressed as

H:=\frac{1}{2}\,p^{\mu}p_{\mu}\ ,\quad q_{I}:=\Xi_{I}^{\mu}\,p_{\mu}\ ,\quad J_{IJ}:=i\,\Xi_{[I}^{\mu}\,\Xi_{J]_{\mu}}\ .

(4.1.3)

Hamiltonian and supercharges together form the
SUSY
algebra

\{q_{I},q_{J}\}=2\,\delta_{IJ}\,H\ ,\quad[q_{I},H]=0\ .

(4.1.4)

While the
R
-symmetry algebra reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}[J_{IJ},q_{K}]&=i\left(\delta_{JK}q_{I}-\delta_{IK}q_{J}\right)\ ,\\
[J_{IJ},J_{KL}]&=i\left(\delta_{JK}J_{IL}-\delta_{IK}J_{JL}-\delta_{JL}J_{IK}+\delta_{IL}J_{JK}\right)\ .\end{split}

(4.1.5)

In the following, we will refer only to (
4.1.4
) as “
{\mathcal{N}}

SUSY
algebra”, since it will be appropriate to distinguish the two algebras in light of the future BSRT procedure. It is rather convenient to work with a complex redefinition of the original fermionic variables
\Xi_{I}\to(\xi_{i},\bar{\xi}^{i})
, namely

\xi^{\mu}_{i}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\Xi^{\mu}_{i}+i\,\Xi^{\mu}_{i+\nicefrac{{{\mathcal{N}}}}{{2}}})\ ,\quad\bar{\xi}^{\mu i}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\Xi^{\mu}_{i}-i\,\Xi^{\mu}_{i+\nicefrac{{{\mathcal{N}}}}{{2}}})\ ,\quad\text{with}\;i=1,\dots,\tfrac{{\mathcal{N}}}{2}

(4.1.6)

for the case of even
{\mathcal{N}}
, i.e. for particles with integer spin. The respective CCR (
4.1.2
) become

\{\bar{\xi}^{i}_{\mu},\xi_{j}^{\nu}\}=\delta^{i}_{j}\,\delta^{\nu}_{\mu}\ ,\quad\{\bar{\xi}^{i}_{\mu},\bar{\xi}^{j}_{\nu}\}=0=\{\xi_{i}^{\mu},\xi_{j}^{\nu}\}\ .

(4.1.7)

Accordingly, both the supercharges and the
R
-symmetry generators split under the complex redefinition: we report the explicit expressions for the particle models of interest, namely the spin one and the spin two. By choosing a specific Fock vacuum, an arbitrary state
\ket{\Psi}
in the Hilbert space is isomorphic to the wavefunction
\Psi(x,\xi)
, on which the conjugated momenta act as derivatives

p_{\mu}=-i\partial_{\mu}\ ,\quad\bar{\xi}_{\mu}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{\mu}}\ .

(4.1.8)

Thus, for the case
{\mathcal{N}}=2
, the spin one model, one has the splitting
q_{I}\to(q,\bar{q})
and
J_{IJ}\to\mathrm{J}
as follows

\begin{array}[]{l}q:=-i\,\xi^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\\[5.69054pt]
\bar{q}:=-i\,\partial^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{\mu}}\end{array}\ ,\qquad\mathrm{J}:=\xi^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{\mu}}-\frac{d}{2}\ ,

(4.1.9)

where the shift
-\tfrac{d}{2}
in the definition of the
R
-symmetry generator is a quantum effect stemming from an antisymmetric ordering for the Grassmann variables
[
30
]
. For the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
case, the spin two, one has
q_{I}\to(q_{i},\bar{q}^{i})
and the
SO(4)
-symmetry generators are realized maintaining manifest covariance only under a
u(2)
-subalgebra
J_{IJ}\to(\mathrm{J}_{i}{}^{j},\mathrm{Tr}^{ij},\mathrm{G}_{ij})
, explicitly realized as
1
1

1

In the following, whether a dot
\cdot
indicates contractions on internal or spacetime indices should be clear within the context.

\begin{array}[]{l}q_{i}:=-i\,\xi_{i}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\\[5.69054pt]
\bar{q}^{i}:=-i\,\partial^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i}^{\mu}}\end{array}\ ,\qquad\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{J}_{i}{}^{j}:=\xi_{i}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{j}}-\tfrac{d}{2}\,\delta_{i}^{j}\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{Tr}^{ij}:=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\xi_{i}\cdot\partial\xi_{j}}\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{G}_{ij}:=\xi_{i}\cdot\xi_{j}\end{array}\ .

(4.1.10)

Note that the only surviving components of the
\mathrm{Tr}
and
\mathrm{G}
operators are those with
i\neq j
.

4.2
BRST quantization

Although BRST quantization was already discussed in Chapter
2
, we shall review it here, since the presence of fermionic rather than bosonic variables modifies the analysis in several non-trivial respects.
2
2

2

We refer to
this
talk by Ivo Sachs for a brilliant exposition of the methodology
[
219
]
.

Let us start with the massless
{\mathcal{N}}=4
spinning particle with a four-dimensional target space. The internal indices take values
i=1,2
and the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
worldline
SUSY
algebra is explicitly realized as

\displaystyle\{q_{i},\bar{q}^{j}\}=2\,\delta_{i}^{j}\,H\ ,\quad[q_{i},H]=[\bar{q}^{i},H]=\{q_{i},q_{j}\}=\{\bar{q}^{i},\bar{q}^{j}\}=0\ .

(4.2.1)

The initial step of the BRST procedure entails an enlargement of the Hilbert space to realize ghost-antighost pairs of operators
(g^{\alpha},P_{\alpha})
associated with each constraint
C_{\alpha}
, with opposite Grassmann parity of the latter – that is, anticommuting ghosts for bosonic constraints and commuting ghosts for fermionic constraints – and canonical graded commutation relation

[P_{\alpha},g^{\beta}\}=\delta^{\beta}_{\alpha}\ .

(4.2.2)

Hence, we assign the fermionic pair
(c,b)
to the Hamiltonian, and the bosonic superghost pairs
(\bar{\gamma}^{i},\beta_{i})
and
(\gamma_{i},\bar{\beta}^{i})
to the supercharges
q_{i}
and
\bar{q}^{i}\,
respectively, obeying

\{b,c\}=1\ ,\quad[\beta_{i},\bar{\gamma}^{j}]=\delta_{i}^{j}\ ,\quad[\bar{\beta}^{j},\gamma_{i}]=\delta_{i}^{j}\ ,

(4.2.3)

with ghost number assignments

\displaystyle{\mathrm{gh}(c,\gamma_{i},\bar{\gamma}^{i})=+1}\ ,

(4.2.4)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{gh}(b,\beta_{i},\bar{\beta}^{i})=-1}\ .

(4.2.5)

The second step consists of constructing the BRST operator
\mathcal{Q}
. It is realized as follows
[
246
]

\mathcal{Q}:=g^{\alpha}C_{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}(-1)^{\epsilon(g_{\beta})}g^{\beta}g^{\alpha}f_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\gamma}P_{\gamma}\ ,

(4.2.6)

where
\epsilon(g_{\beta})
is the Grassmann parity
3
3

3

We employ the following convention: the parity of
g^{\alpha}
is
0
if
g^{\alpha}
is Grassmann even and
1
if
g^{\alpha}
is Grassmann odd.
of the ghost
g^{\beta}
. Equation (
4.2.6
) holds exactly when the structure functions
f_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\gamma}
are constant,
4
4

4

Recall that the constraints satisfy upon quantization a graded algebra of the form

[C_{\alpha},C_{\beta}\}=f_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\gamma}C_{\gamma}

which defines the structure functions
f_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\gamma}
.
but its validity extends to more general settings as well
[
146
]
. It can be derived by demanding the following properties for the BRST operator:

•

It has to be anticommuting and of ghost number
+1
.

•

It has to act on the operators, corresponding to the original phase space variables prior to quantization, as the gauge transformations with the ghost variables replacing the gauge parameters. This, together with the latter requirement, is enough to constrain the structure to be
\mathcal{Q}=g^{\alpha}C_{\alpha}+\dots

•

Finally, the BRST charge has to be nilpotent. This determines the second structure of (
4.2.6
) as can be checked by direct computation of
\{\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q}\}
.

Note that the BRST charge is nilpotent
by construction
as long as the associated algebra is first-class. In more general cases, higher-order terms may appear and need to be determined by the nilpotency condition. Specializing to the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
case, the BRST operator associated with the first-class algebra (
4.2.1
) is

\mathcal{Q}=c\,H+\gamma_{i}\,\bar{q}^{i}+\bar{\gamma}^{i}\,q_{i}-2\bar{\gamma}^{i}\gamma_{i}\,b\ .

(4.2.7)

To verify its nilpotency, it is first convenient to define
\bm{\nabla}:=\gamma\cdot\bar{q}+\bar{\gamma}\cdot q
, such that for any operator of the form (
4.2.7
