) one always finds the following potential obstruction-terms to its nilpotency

\mathcal{Q}^{2}=-2\,\bar{\gamma}\cdot\gamma\,H+\bm{\nabla}^{2}-2c\,\cancel{\left[H,\bm{\nabla}\right]}\ ,

(4.2.8)

which are vanishing in the present case since

\bm{\nabla}^{2}=\gamma_{i}\bar{\gamma}^{j}\{\bar{q}^{i},q_{j}\}\implies-2\,\bar{\gamma}\cdot\gamma\,H+\bm{\nabla}^{2}=0\ .

(4.2.9)

Notice the critical role played by the presence of the first-class algebra (
4.2.1
) in both the cancellation of (
4.2.8
) and of (
4.2.9
). The same does not hold anymore in more general cases. In the next section, we will illustrate how to address this issue.

The careful reader might wonder about the absence of a set of ghosts associated with the
so(4)
constraints. The key idea of the BRST procedure, as first developed in Ref.
[
108
]
providing a first-quantized description of the gluon from a “fermionic” worldline model, is to treat the
R
-symmetry constraints and the
SUSY
ones on different footings. The formers are imposed as constraints on the BRST Hilbert space, thus defining precisely the general dependence of the string field
\Psi
on the spacetime fields content.
5
5

5

Regarding terminology, we deliberately confuse “string field” and “BRST wavefunction” since
\Psi
plays the same role in the worldline theory as it would in string field theory: its expansion as a linear combination of first-quantized states display coefficients which correspond to ordinary particle fields
[
257
,
177
,
240
]
.
It is within this restricted Hilbert space that the cohomology of the BRST charge has to be studied. The procedure remains consistent as long as the
R
-symmetry constraints (
4.1.10
) commute with the BRST charge: in order to achieve that, it is necessary to extend
(\mathrm{J},\mathrm{Tr},\mathrm{G})\to({\mathcal{J}},{{\cal T}\!r},{\mathcal{G}})
as follows

\begin{split}{\mathcal{J}}_{i}{}^{j}&:=\xi_{i}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{j}+\gamma_{i}\bar{\beta}^{j}-\bar{\gamma}^{j}\beta_{i}-2\,\delta_{i}^{j}\ ,\\[5.69054pt]
{{\cal T}\!r}^{ij}&:=\bar{\xi}^{i}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{j}+\bar{\gamma}^{i}\bar{\beta}^{j}-\bar{\gamma}^{j}\bar{\beta}^{i}\ ,\\[5.69054pt]
{\mathcal{G}}_{ij}&:=\xi_{i}\cdot\xi_{j}+\gamma_{i}\beta_{j}-\gamma_{j}\beta_{i}\ .\end{split}

(4.2.10)

The relevant
so(4)
generators to be imposed as constraints on the BRST Hilbert space are the
two number operators

{\mathcal{J}}_{i}:={\mathcal{J}}_{i}{}^{i}
(
i
not summed), namely the diagonal entries of
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}{}^{j}
, the
Young antisymmetrizer

{\mathcal{Y}}:={\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{2}
, and finally the
trace

{{\cal T}\!r}
, which implement the maximal reduction of the model. We collectively denote these constraints as
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}:=({\mathcal{J}},{\mathcal{Y}},{{\cal T}\!r})
. The BRST system is defined as follows

\begin{split}&\mathcal{Q}\,\Psi=0\;,\quad\delta\Psi=\mathcal{Q}\,\Lambda\;,\\[5.69054pt]
&{\cal T}_{\alpha}\,\Psi=0\;,\quad{\cal T}_{\alpha}\,\Lambda=0\;,\end{split}

(4.2.11)

and its consistency is ensured by
[\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}]=0
. This is indeed equivalent to saying that we are studying the cohomology of
\mathcal{Q}
on the restricted Hilbert space of fixed
R
-charge defined by
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{red}}:=\ker\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
. The system above will serve as the starting point for BRST quantization in both the massless and massive cases.

4.2.1
Massless graviton on a flat spacetime

The ghost vacuum
\ket{0}
is chosen such that it is annihilated by
(b,\bar{\gamma}^{i},\bar{\beta}^{i})
, so that a general state
\ket{\Psi}
in the BRST extended Hilbert space is isomorphic to the wavefunction
\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,c,\gamma,\beta)
, on which the antighosts act as derivatives, i.e.

b=\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\ ,\quad\bar{\gamma}^{i}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta_{i}}\ ,\quad\bar{\beta}^{i}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma_{i}}\ .

(4.2.12)

In the following, we collectively denote the ghost oscillators as
{\mathfrak{g}}:=(c,\gamma,\beta)
. The BRST operator, making explicit the d’Alembertian
\Box=\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}
while adjusting the coefficients, acts as

\mathcal{Q}=c\,\Box+\gamma_{i}\bar{q}^{i}-q_{i}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta_{i}}-\gamma_{i}\,\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\beta_{i}\partial c}\ ,

(4.2.13)

while, regarding the
so(4)
generators

\begin{split}{\mathcal{J}}_{i}&=N_{\xi_{i}}+N_{\gamma_{i}}+N_{\beta_{i}}-1\ ,\\[5.69054pt]
{\mathcal{Y}}&=\xi_{1}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}+\gamma_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma_{2}}+\beta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta_{2}}\ ,\\[5.69054pt]
{{\cal T}\!r}&=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\xi_{1}\cdot\partial\xi_{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\gamma_{1}\partial\beta_{2}}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\gamma_{2}\partial\beta_{1}}\ ,\end{split}

(4.2.14)

where we have explicitly expressed the number operators, counting the number of oscillators with a fixed flavor index, and where we solved ambiguities in the definition of
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
by choosing a symmetric ordering for the ghosts.

It is useful for future reference to highlight the intermediate steps of the calculation. The first condition imposed by
\Psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{red}}
, namely
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}\ket{\Psi}=0
, significantly reduces the components of
\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})
to

\displaystyle\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=a_{\mu\nu}\,\xi^{\mu}_{1}\xi^{\nu}_{2}+b_{\mu}\,\xi_{1}^{\mu}\gamma_{2}+C_{\mu}\,\xi_{1}^{\mu}\beta_{2}+d_{\mu}\,\gamma_{1}\xi_{2}^{\mu}+e\,\gamma_{1}\beta_{2}+f_{\mu}\,\beta_{1}\xi_{2}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+g\,\beta_{1}\gamma_{2}+k\,\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}+l\,\beta_{1}\beta_{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+a^{\ast}_{\mu\nu}\,\xi^{\mu}_{1}\xi^{\nu}_{2}c+b^{\ast}_{\mu}\,\xi_{1}^{\mu}\gamma_{2}c+C^{\ast}_{\mu}\,\xi_{1}^{\mu}\beta_{2}c+d^{\ast}_{\mu}\,\gamma_{1}\xi_{2}^{\mu}c+e^{\ast}\,\gamma_{1}\beta_{2}c+f^{\ast}_{\mu}\,\beta_{1}\xi_{2}^{\mu}c

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+g^{\ast}\,\beta_{1}\gamma_{2}c+k^{\ast}\,\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}c+l^{\ast}\,\beta_{1}\beta_{2}c\ .

(4.2.15)

The latter expression is essentially a Taylor expansion in powers of
c
taking the form
\Psi=\chi+\chi^{\ast}c
, where both terms
\chi
and
\chi^{\ast}
of the wavefunction contain oscillators and spacetime-dependent field components. Requiring the entire wavefunction to have fixed Grassmann parity and fixed ghost number forces the field components in
\chi^{\ast}
to have opposite parities and ghost numbers decreased by one compared to those in
\chi
. It is important to note that we are not yet in the position to correctly identify (anti)fields: we first need to reduce
\Psi
to include only the
BV
spectrum of the theory. Imposing the two remaining conditions we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&b_{\mu}=-d_{\mu}\ ,\quad C_{\mu}=-f_{\mu}\ ,\quad e=-g\ ,\quad k=0\ ,\quad l=0\ ,\\
&b^{\ast}_{\mu}=-d^{\ast}_{\mu}\ ,\quad C^{\ast}_{\mu}=-f^{\ast}_{\mu}\ ,\quad e^{\ast}=-g^{\ast}\ ,\quad k^{\ast}=0\ ,\quad l^{\ast}=0\ ,\end{split}

(4.2.16)

together with
a_{\mu\nu}=a_{\nu\mu}
using
{\mathcal{Y}}\ket{\Psi}=0
, while from
{{\cal T}\!r}\ket{\Psi}=0
we get

e=\tfrac{1}{2}a^{\mu}_{\mu}\ ,\quad e^{\ast}=\tfrac{1}{2}a^{\ast\mu}_{\mu}\ .

(4.2.17)

Now we can indeed interpret
\Psi
as a spacetime Batalin–Vilkovisky string field, that contains the whole minimal
BV
spectrum of pure gravity along with auxiliary fields: we assign Grassmann parity and ghost number to the component fields such that the entire wavefunction
\Psi
has total even parity and ghost number zero. It becomes explicit with the following identifications for the fields

\displaystyle a_{\mu\nu}\longrightarrow h_{\mu\nu}\quad a^{\mu}_{\mu}\longrightarrow h^{\mu}_{\mu}=:h\quad C^{\ast}_{\mu}\longrightarrow v_{\mu}\quad C_{\mu}\longrightarrow\zeta_{\mu}\ ,

(4.2.18)

and for the corresponding antifields

\displaystyle a^{\ast}_{\mu\nu}\longrightarrow h^{\ast}_{\mu\nu}\quad b_{\mu}\longrightarrow v^{\ast}_{\mu}\quad b^{\ast}_{\mu}\longrightarrow\zeta^{\ast}_{\mu}\ .

(4.2.19)

Grassmann parities and ghost numbers can be read from Table
4.1
. The most general state in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{red}}
is then

\displaystyle\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=h_{\mu\nu}(x)\,\xi^{\mu}_{1}\xi^{\nu}_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,h(x)\,(\gamma_{1}\beta_{2}-\gamma_{2}\beta_{1})-\tfrac{i}{2}\,v_{\mu}(x)\,(\xi^{\mu}_{1}\beta_{2}-\xi^{\mu}_{2}\beta_{1})c

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\tfrac{i}{2}\,\zeta_{\mu}(x)\,(\xi^{\mu}_{1}\beta_{2}-\xi^{\mu}_{2}\beta_{1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+h^{\ast}_{\mu\nu}(x)\,\xi^{\mu}_{1}\xi^{\nu}_{2}c+\tfrac{1}{2}\,h^{\ast}(x)\,(\gamma_{1}\beta_{2}-\gamma_{2}\beta_{1})c-\tfrac{i}{2}\,v^{\ast}_{\mu}(x)\,(\xi^{\mu}_{1}\gamma_{2}-\xi^{\mu}_{2}\gamma_{1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\tfrac{i}{2}\,\zeta^{\ast}_{\mu}(x)\,(\xi^{\mu}_{1}\gamma_{2}-\xi^{\mu}_{2}\gamma_{1})c\ .

(4.2.20)

It contains the graviton
h_{\mu\nu}
and its trace
h
, an auxiliary vector field
v_{\mu}
, and the diffeomorphism ghost
\zeta_{\mu}
, while the remaining components are the corresponding antifields.

BV
role

Ghost number

0

Table 4.1:
List of fields in the physical sector of the
massless

{\mathcal{N}}=4
model with the corresponding ghost number and Grassmann parity.

The final step involves evaluating the field equations of the theory, to verify that the model correctly reproduces a first-quantized representation of linearized gravity. This can be accomplished through the BRST closure equation

\mathcal{Q}\,\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})=0

(4.2.21)

at ghost number zero, which produces the massless free spin two field equation

\Box h_{\mu\nu}-2\,\partial_{(\mu}\partial\cdot h_{\nu)}+\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}h=0\ ,

(4.2.22)

and

\Box h-\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu}=0\ ,

(4.2.23)

which implies a vanishing linearized Ricci scalar. To conclude this section, one is left to verify the accurate reproduction of the gauge symmetry: this is achieved from the ghost number zero part of

\delta\Psi=\mathcal{Q}\,\Lambda\ ,

(4.2.24)

where
\Lambda\in\ker\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
contains the gauge parameters of the associated symmetry while having the same functional form as
\Psi
, with overall odd parity and ghost number
-1
, i.e.

\Lambda=i\varepsilon_{\mu}(x)\,(\xi^{\mu}_{1}\beta_{2}-\xi^{\mu}_{2}\beta_{1})+\cdots\ ,

(4.2.25)

where the gauge parameter
\varepsilon_{\mu}
has even parity and ghost number zero. As expected, the result is

\delta h_{\mu\nu}=2\partial_{(\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu)}\ .

(4.2.26)

For future reference, the closure equation at ghost number one is reported below:

\Box\,\zeta_{\mu}=0\;,\quad\partial_{(\mu}\,\zeta_{\nu)}=0\ .

(4.2.27)

Equations (
4.2.27
) represent a set of equations of motion for the diffeomorphism ghost
\zeta_{\mu}(x)
.

4.2.2
Massless graviton on a curved background

The consistent coupling of the spinning particles to more general backgrounds beyond flat spacetime is a rather delicate matter
[
173
]
. In previous works, a quantization
à la
Dirac has been extensively employed. However, when the
SUSY
algebra fails to be first-class this method loses its validity, potentially leading to the misleading conclusion that quantization is not feasible in more general cases. As already mentioned, this limitation was evident in the case of the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
spinning particle, for which only certain restricted backgrounds were found to be viable until it was realized that the technique of BRST quantization offers a way to explore more general backgrounds, as recently discussed in Refs.
[
79
,
181
]
. In this section, we review the exploration of such possibility.

The coupling of the massless
{\mathcal{N}}=4
spinning particle to a curved background with metric
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
is realized by the covariantization of the derivatives, i.e.

\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}:=\partial_{\mu}+\omega_{\mu\,ab}\,\xi^{a}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{b}\ ,\quad\text{with}\quad[\hat{\nabla}_{\mu},\hat{\nabla}_{\nu}]=R_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}\,\xi^{\lambda}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\sigma}\ ,

(4.2.28)

where fermions carry flat Lorentz indices so that
\xi^{\mu}_{i}:=e^{\mu}_{a}(x)\,\xi^{a}_{i}
, introducing a background vielbein
e_{\mu}^{a}(x)
and the torsion-free spin connection
\omega_{\mu\,ab}
. The covariant derivative operator
\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}
reproduces the effect of the usual covariant (partial) derivative
\nabla_{\mu}
(
\partial_{\mu}
) on the tensorial (scalar) components contained in the wavefunction (
4.2.1
), e.g.

\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})=\nabla_{\mu}h_{\alpha\beta}(x)\,\xi_{1}^{\alpha}\xi_{2}^{\beta}+\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}h(x)\,(\gamma_{1}\beta_{2}-\gamma_{2}\beta_{1})+\dots\ .

(4.2.29)

The presence of a general background manifests itself as deformations of the original BRST system: indeed the supercharges become

{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}:=-i\,\xi_{i}^{a}\,e^{\mu}_{a}\,\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\ ,\quad\bar{\mathfrak{q}}^{i}:=-i\,\bar{\xi}^{i\,a}\,e^{\mu}_{a}\,\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\ .

(4.2.30)

The main consequence is that the
SUSY
algebra does not close anymore:
6
6

6

As to not further burden the notation we denote
\bm{R}_{\mu\nu}:=R_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}\,\xi^{\lambda}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\sigma}
and
\bm{R}:=R_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}\,\xi^{\mu}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\nu}\,\xi^{\lambda}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\sigma}
.

\displaystyle\{{\mathfrak{q}}_{i},{\mathfrak{q}}_{j}\}=-\xi^{\mu}_{i}\xi^{\nu}_{j}\,\bm{R}_{\mu\nu}\;,\quad\{\bar{\mathfrak{q}}^{i},\bar{\mathfrak{q}}^{j}\}=-\bar{\xi}^{\mu\,i}\bar{\xi}^{\nu\,j}\,\bm{R}_{\mu\nu}\;,\quad\{{\mathfrak{q}}_{i},\bar{\mathfrak{q}}^{j}\}=-\delta_{i}^{j}\nabla^{2}-\xi^{\mu}_{i}\bar{\xi}^{\nu\,j}\,\bm{R}_{\mu\nu}\ ,

\displaystyle[\nabla^{2},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}]=i\,\xi^{\mu}_{i}\big(2\,\bm{R}_{\mu\nu}\,\hat{\nabla}^{\nu}-\nabla^{\lambda}\bm{R}_{\lambda\mu}-R_{\mu\nu}\hat{\nabla}^{\nu}\big)\ ,

\displaystyle[\nabla^{2},\bar{\mathfrak{q}}^{i}]=i\,\bar{\xi}^{\mu\,i}\big(2\,\bm{R}_{\mu\nu}\,\hat{\nabla}^{\nu}-\nabla^{\lambda}\bm{R}_{\lambda\mu}-R_{\mu\nu}\hat{\nabla}^{\nu}\big)\ ,

(4.2.31)

where the Laplacian is defined as

\nabla^{2}:=g^{\mu\nu}\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\hat{\nabla}_{\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}\,\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\,\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda}\ .

(4.2.32)

The BRST operator needs to be deformed as well, requiring the construction of an ansatz due to the fact that the associated algebra is no longer first-class. The general approach involves considering the same general form (
4.2.7
), but with the inclusion of more general terms accounting for the obstruction to the first-class character of the associated algebra, namely the curvature in the present case. Therefore, possible non-minimal couplings to the curvature, collectively denoted as
{\mathfrak{R}}
, must be incorporated inside the Hamiltonian. These couplings may act as obstructions to the nilpotency of the BRST operator, and the BRST analysis aims to determine which of these terms persist to ensure a nilpotent
\mathcal{Q}
. Avoiding higher powers of ghost momenta and assuming that derivatives are deformed only through minimal coupling, the ansatz for the BRST charge is

\mathcal{Q}=c\,{\mathcal{D}}+\bm{\nabla}+\bar{\gamma}\cdot\gamma\,b\ ,

(4.2.33)

where
{\mathcal{D}}
is the deformed Hamiltonian in its operatorial form

{\mathcal{D}}:=\nabla^{2}+{\mathfrak{R}}\ ,\quad\text{with}\quad{\mathfrak{R}}:=R_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}\,\xi^{\mu}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\nu}\,\xi^{\lambda}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\sigma}+\kappa R\ ,

(4.2.34)

with
\kappa
a coefficient to be determined, and the
\bm{\nabla}
operator has been conveniently redefined as

\bm{\nabla}:=-i\,S^{\mu}\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\ ,\quad\text{with}\quad S^{\mu}:=\bar{\gamma}\cdot\xi^{\mu}+\gamma\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\mu}\ .

(4.2.35)

Note that only the
so(4)
constraints
{\cal T}_{\alpha}
(
4.2.14
) remain unchanged, therefore the wavefunction
\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})
is still expressed as in (
4.2.1
).

The BRST analysis starts with the general expression for
\mathcal{Q}^{2}
, namely the extension of its flat spacetime counterpart (
4.2.8
) with adjusted coefficients:

\mathcal{Q}^{2}=\bm{\nabla}^{2}+\bar{\gamma}\cdot\gamma\,{\mathcal{D}}+c\,\left[{\mathcal{D}},\bm{\nabla}\right]\ ,

(4.2.36)

which remains valid regardless of the specific spinning particle model coupled to a general curved background. In general, (
4.2.36
) includes two independent obstructions, which in this case are given by

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}^{2}+\bar{\gamma}\cdot\gamma\,{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,S^{\mu}S^{\nu}\,\bm{R}_{\mu\nu}+\bar{\gamma}\cdot\gamma\,{\mathfrak{R}}\ ,

(4.2.37)

\displaystyle\left[{\mathcal{D}},\bm{\nabla}\right]

\displaystyle=-iS^{\mu}\nabla^{\lambda}\bm{R}_{\lambda\mu}+iS^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\bm{R}+\kappa[\,R,\bm{\nabla}]\ .

(4.2.38)

Recall that the BRST cohomology is defined on the reduced Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{red}}
, that is, one needs to evaluate the obstructions on
\ker{\cal T}_{\alpha}
. Therefore, for the sake of the cohomology, it suffices to ensure nilpotency of the BRST charge
when acting
on the physical sector of the theory:

\mathcal{Q}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\ker}{\cal T}_{\alpha}}}{{=}}0\quad\text{i.e.}\quad\mathcal{Q}^{2}\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\small!}}}}{{=}}}0\ .

(4.2.39)

The effect is for instance that any contribution with at least three barred oscillators is set to zero. Equations (
4.2.37
)–(
4.2.38
) get then reduced to

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}^{2}+\bar{\gamma}\cdot\gamma\,{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\ker}{\cal T}_{\alpha}}}{{=}}-\,\bar{\gamma}\cdot\gamma\left(2\,R_{\mu\nu}\,\xi^{\mu}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\nu}-\kappa\,R\right)\ ,

(4.2.40)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left[{\mathcal{D}},\bm{\nabla}\right]&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ker{\cal T}_{\alpha}}}{{=}}-iS^{\mu}\nabla^{\lambda}\bm{R}_{\lambda\mu}+i\big(2\nabla^{\lambda}\bm{R}_{\lambda\mu}\gamma\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\mu}-S^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}R_{\nu\lambda}\xi^{\nu}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\lambda}\big)\\
&\phantom{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ker{\cal T}_{\alpha}}}{{=}}}\;+i\kappa\,S^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}R\ .\end{split}

(4.2.41)

The conclusion is that the nilpotency of the BRST charge, in the massless case, is achieved only on Einstein manifolds, i.e.

\mathcal{Q}^{2}\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})=0\;\iff\;R_{\mu\nu}=\lambda\,g_{\mu\nu}\ ,

(4.2.42)

upon setting
\kappa=\tfrac{1}{2}
. Let us clarify this crucial point for the upcoming discussion: while the obstruction given in (
4.2.41
) vanishes by itself on Einstein spaces, the one in (
4.2.40
) is zero
only
when acting on the physical sector:

\mathcal{Q}^{2}\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})\ni-2\lambda\,\bar{\gamma}\cdot\gamma\left(\xi^{\mu}\cdot\bar{\xi}_{\mu}-1\right)\,\Psi(x,\xi\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})=0\ .

(4.2.43)

The closure equation evaluated using the deformed BRST charge yields the correct field equations for a massless
7
7

7

It is a rather fascinating topic the concept of mass on a general spacetime, which is not well defined. In particular, one should be careful to call a particle
massless
: see
[
121
]
for a discussion on the connection between gauge invariance, masslessness, and null cone propagation.
graviton on Einstein spaces, namely

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}h_{\mu\nu}-2\nabla_{(\mu}\nabla\cdot h_{\nu)}+\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}h+2R_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}h^{\alpha\beta}=0\ ,

(4.2.44)

together with

\displaystyle(\nabla^{2}+\lambda)h-\nabla^{\mu}\nabla^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu}=0\ .

(4.2.45)

The correct gauge symmetry
\delta h_{\mu\nu}=2\nabla_{(\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu)}
is obtained as well.

4.3
Giving mass to the graviton

In this section, we present two methods to confer a mass to a spinning particle model. The first one, the Scherk–Schwarz mechanism, is analogous to the Kaluza–Klein compactification. This has already been encountered in Chapter
2
, but with bosonic oscillators. In the second method, the auxiliary oscillators approach, the model is treated as a truncation of the RNS open superstring
[
180
]
. We employ both methods to give mass to the graviton on a flat background and subsequently discuss the results. However, before delving into the graviton case, we begin by examining the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
scenario, in order to introduce the techniques and investigate the role of the mass as a potential obstruction to nilpotency of the BRST charge.

4.3.1
{\mathcal{N}}=2
massive spinning particle and the Proca Theory

The massless
{\mathcal{N}}=2
spinning particle has been thoroughly examined in previous works, leading to a first-quantized description of massless antisymmetric tensor fields of arbitrary rank
[
63
,
172
,
191
,
192
,
95
]
. It has been shown how to provide a first-quantized description of the photon both on a flat and on a curved spacetime
[
30
,
181
]
. Additionally, the coupling to a non-abelian background has been explored, reproducing the non-linear Yang–Mills equations
[
108
]
upon BRST quantization.

The massive scenario has undergone investigation as well: the procedure
à la
Kaluza–Klein has been implemented in Refs.
[
95
,
29
]
where it has been shown how to embed the model in an
arbitrary curved
spacetime background. This has allowed for the utilization of this model in the context of worldline quantum field theory formalism, particularly in modeling classical scatterings of compact objects in general relativity (see
[
210
,
195
]
and related literature). More recently, the coupling to both an abelian and a non-abelian vector background field has been investigated in Ref.
[
99
]
, where the authors first introduced the auxiliary oscillators approach.

4.3.1.1
Mass improvement on flat spacetime

The main features of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
model can be immediately derived from section
4.1
by setting
I=1,2
and are collected in detail in Ref.
[
29
]
. In this chapter, we will provide a concise overview of the essential elements of the BRST quantization of the massive case on flat and, most notably, on curved background as a toy model for the subsequent analysis, which is not covered in the literature mentioned above.

The procedure consists of starting with the massless theory formulated in one dimension higher, and subsequently reducing the dimensionality of the target space through the introduction of suitable constraints
[
36
]
. We consider the model to live in a flat spacetime of the form
\mathcal{M}_{d}\times S^{1}
of
D=d+1
dimensions with coordinates and worldline superpartners given by

\displaystyle x^{M}=(x^{\mu},x^{D})\ ,\quad\xi^{M}=(\psi^{\mu},\theta)\ ,\quad\bar{\xi}^{M}=(\bar{\psi}^{\mu},\bar{\theta})\ ,

(4.3.1)

with the index splitting
M=(\mu,D)
. The idea is to gauge the compact direction
x^{D}
, corresponding to
S^{1}
, by imposing the first-class constraint

p_{D}-m=0\ .

(4.3.2)

This results in the phase space action (
4.1.1
) having a leftover term
\sim\dot{x}^{D}
, which can be regarded as a total derivative and thus dropped. The CCR (
4.1.2
) are realized explicitly as

[x^{\mu},p_{\nu}]=i\,\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\ ,\quad\{\bar{\psi}^{\mu},\psi^{\nu}\}=\eta^{\mu\nu}\ ,\quad\{\bar{\theta},\theta\}=1\ ,

(4.3.3)

with the other (anti)commutators being zero. The
{\mathcal{N}}=2

SUSY
constraints get modified by the presence of the mass, taking the form

\displaystyle H=\frac{1}{2}\left(p^{\mu}p_{\mu}+m^{2}\right)\ ,\quad q=p_{\mu}\psi^{\mu}+m\theta\ ,\quad\bar{q}=p_{\mu}\bar{\psi}^{\mu}+m\bar{\theta}\ ,

(4.3.4)

while the
R
-symmetry generator (
4.1.9
) becomes

\mathrm{J}=\psi^{\mu}\bar{\psi}_{\mu}+\theta\bar{\theta}-\frac{d+1}{2}\ .

(4.3.5)

Together they still satisfy the same
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetry algebra despite the mass improvement

\displaystyle\{q,\bar{q}\}=2H\ ,\quad[q,H]=[\bar{q},H]=\{q,q\}=\{\bar{q},\bar{q}\}=0\ ,

(4.3.6)

which successfully remains first-class. Note the key role played by the surviving fermionic coordinates coming from the extra dimension
\theta:=\xi^{D}
and
\bar{\theta}:=\bar{\xi}^{D}
, which are responsible for the introduction of the mass term into the theory. Indeed, in the limit where
(\theta,\bar{\theta})\to(0,0)
the massless theory must be recovered as a consistency check.

At this point, the BRST quantization on a four-dimensional flat spacetime proceeds smoothly, just as described for the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
case. Upon enlarging the Hilbert space to realize the set of ghost operators
(c,\bar{\gamma},\gamma)
with relative momenta
(b,\beta,\bar{\beta})
as in (
4.2.3
), the BRST charge is constructed as usual

\mathcal{Q}=c\,H+\gamma\,\bar{q}+\bar{\gamma}\,q-2\bar{\gamma}\gamma\,b\ ,

(4.3.7)

and the
so(2)
constraint has to be extended to include ghost contributions as follows

{\mathcal{J}}:=\psi^{\mu}\bar{\psi}_{\mu}+\theta\bar{\theta}+\gamma\bar{\beta}-\bar{\gamma}\beta-\tfrac{3}{2}\ ,

(4.3.8)

with the usual prescription employed to resolve ambiguities. Nilpotency of
\mathcal{Q}
is still guaranteed, despite the mass improvement, since the associated algebra is first-class

\mathcal{Q}^{2}=0\ ,

(4.3.9)

and the BRST system is consistent since
[\mathcal{Q},{\mathcal{J}}]=0
. The physical sector is defined as the eigenspace of
{\mathcal{J}}
with a fixed
R
-charge
-\tfrac{1}{2}
,
8
8

8

This condition is equivalent to demanding that
\Psi\in{\ker}{\tilde{\mathcal{J}}}
, with
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}
being the shifted
SO(2)
constraint
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{J}}+\tfrac{1}{2}
. The procedure remains consistent as long as
[\mathcal{Q},\tilde{\mathcal{J}}]=0
. Eventually, the shift can be interpreted as the introduction of a Chern–Simons term selecting the desired degrees of freedom, as in Ref.
[
29
]
.
i.e. physical states are eigenstates
{\mathcal{J}}\ket{\Psi}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\ket{\Psi}
and are isomorphic to wavefunctions

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi(x,\psi,\theta\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})&=A_{\mu}(x)\psi^{\mu}-i\varphi(x)\theta+\phi(x)\beta c+\Phi(x)\beta\\[2.84526pt]
&\phantom{=}+A_{\mu}^{\ast}(x)\psi^{\mu}c-i\varphi^{\ast}(x)\theta c+\phi^{\ast}(x)\gamma+\Phi^{\ast}(x)\gamma c\ .\end{split}

(4.3.10)

Requiring
\Psi
to be Grassmann-odd and have ghost number zero, it can be interpreted as a spacetime
BV
string field displaying the complete minimal
BV
spectrum of the Proca theory along with an auxiliary field. The Grassmann parities and ghost numbers of the components can be found in Table
4.2
. In contrast to the massless case, the physical fields include not only the massive spin one
A_{\mu}
but also the Stückelberg scalar
\varphi
. The inclusion of the Stückelberg scalar becomes necessary to restore the
U(1)
gauge symmetry
[
239
]
, which is broken due to the introduction of the mass. Notably, the spectrum also includes the associated scalar ghost
\Phi
in the spectrum.

BV
role

Field

Grassmann parity

Ghost number

massive spin one

A_{\mu}

0

0

Stückelberg scalar

\varphi

0

0

auxiliary scalar

\phi

0

0

scalar ghost

\Phi

1

1

Table 4.2:
List of fields in the physical sector of the
massive

{\mathcal{N}}=2
model with the corresponding ghost number and Grassmann parity.

The field equations, upon solving for the auxiliary field, are

\displaystyle\left(\Box-m^{2}\right)A_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}\partial\cdot A-m\partial_{\mu}\varphi

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(4.3.11)

\displaystyle\Box\varphi+m\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(4.3.12)

which are indeed the Proca field equations with the scalar
\varphi(x)
playing the role of Stückelberg field, while the gauge symmetry, from
\delta\Psi=\mathcal{Q}\Lambda
with

\Lambda=i\Sigma(x)\beta+\cdots\ ,

(4.3.13)

is

\delta A_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\Sigma\ ,\quad\delta\varphi=-m\,\Sigma\ ,

(4.3.14)

where
\Sigma(x)
is a local gauge parameter, of even parity and with ghost number zero.

Let us end by noting that the same results can be obtained either by employing the auxiliary oscillators approach, although it is not explicitly shown here, or with the “bosonic” worldline model of Chapter
2
.

4.3.1.2
Mass obstruction on curved spacetime

We are finally in the position to investigate whether the presence of the mass plays a role when a curved background is considered, generalizing the massless BRST analysis of Ref.
[
181
]
. The coupling of the massive
{\mathcal{N}}=2
spinning particle to gravity is realized by the covariantization of the reduced model just discussed, with the mass already present in the theory. The supercharges are then deformed as follows

{\mathfrak{q}}:=-i\,\psi^{a}\,e^{\mu}_{a}\,\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\ +m\theta\ ,\quad\bar{\mathfrak{q}}:=-i\,\bar{\psi}^{a}\,e^{\mu}_{a}\,\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}+m\bar{\theta}\ .

(4.3.15)

Unlike the situation with the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
spinning particle (
4.2.2
), the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
algebra (
4.3.6
) remains first-class regardless of a particular background, upon a suitable redefinition of the Hamiltonian

{\mathcal{D}}:=\nabla^{2}-m^{2}+\bm{R}\ .

(4.3.16)

Indeed, the
SUSY
algebra takes the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{{\mathfrak{q}},\bar{\mathfrak{q}}\}=-{\mathcal{D}}\ ,\quad\{{\mathfrak{q}},{\mathfrak{q}}\}=\{\bar{\mathfrak{q}},\bar{\mathfrak{q}}\}&=0\ ,\\
[{\mathfrak{q}},{\mathcal{D}}]=[\bar{\mathfrak{q}},{\mathcal{D}}]&=0\ ,\end{split}

(4.3.17)

where the vanishing of the top-right commutators is guaranteed by the cyclic identity for the Riemann tensor. The corresponding BRST operator is defined as usual
\mathcal{Q}=c\,{\mathcal{D}}+\bm{\nabla}+\bar{\gamma}\gamma\,b
, with potential obstructions incorporated into the definition of
\bm{\nabla}
with respect to the massless case:

\bm{\nabla}:=-i\,S^{\mu}\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}+m\rho\ ,\quad\rho:=\bar{\gamma}\,\theta+\gamma\,\bar{\theta}\ .

(4.3.18)

The existence of an associated first-class algebra (
4.3.17
) ensures the nilpotency of the BRST operator without any further conditions on the background metric,
9
9

9

Actually, the only requirement is that of a torsionless connection, i.e.
T^{\mu}_{\nu\rho}:=\Gamma^{\mu}_{[\nu\rho]}=0
.
which is expected from
QFT
considerations. While this has been already established for the massless case, the same holds even in the massive one, as the mass does not affect the algebra, as we now shall work out explicitly. Starting from the general expression

\mathcal{Q}^{2}=\bm{\nabla}^{2}+\bar{\gamma}\gamma\,{\mathcal{D}}+c\,\left[{\mathcal{D}},\bm{\nabla}\right]\ ,

(4.3.19)

one finds the following independent obstructions

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}^{2}+\bar{\gamma}\gamma\,{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}S^{\mu}S^{\nu}\bm{R}_{\mu\nu}+\cancel{\bar{\gamma}\gamma\,m^{2}}+\bar{\gamma}\gamma\left(\cancel{-m^{2}}+\bm{R}\right)\ ,

(4.3.20)

\displaystyle\left[{\mathcal{D}},\bm{\nabla}\right]

\displaystyle=-iS^{\mu}\nabla^{\lambda}\bm{R}_{\lambda\mu}+iS^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\bm{R}\ .

(4.3.21)

In the present case, evaluating the BRST cohomology on the reduced Hilbert space,
\ker\mathcal{\tilde{\mathcal{J}}}
, has the effect of setting to zero any obstruction of the form
\mathcal{O}^{AB}\bar{Z}_{A}\bar{Z}_{B}
with
\mathcal{O}^{AB}
arbitrary operators since an arbitrary state in the physical sector has the form

\Upsilon_{A}(x)\,Z^{A}+\Omega_{B}(x)\,Z^{B}\,c\quad\text{for}\quad Z^{A}=(\psi^{\mu},\theta,\gamma,\beta)\ .

(4.3.22)

This is enough to conclude that indeed

\mathcal{Q}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ker\mathcal{\tilde{\mathcal{J}}}}}{{=}}0

(4.3.23)

without further conditions on the background; in other words, the massive
{\mathcal{N}}=2
spinning particle can be coupled to off-shell gravity. Remarkably, the mass plays no role in the BRST algebra, as it is trivially canceled in (
4.3.20
) and commutes with any operator inside (
4.3.21
) being constant. It is worth noting that introducing a term proportional to the Ricci curvature inside the Hamiltonian (
4.3.16
)
{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{D}}+\kappa R
, would force
\kappa
to be zero (either that or a vanishing Ricci scalar
R=0
). This is dictated by the BRST algebra (
4.3.20
)–(
4.3.21
), as any term of that form is incompatible with the closure of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2

SUSY
algebra (
4.3.6
). Interestingly, the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
spinning particle appears to select only the minimal coupling to the background, as we shall check from the equations of motion. From the closure equation
\mathcal{Q}\Psi=0
, one finds

\displaystyle(\nabla^{2}-m^{2})A_{\mu}-R_{\mu\nu}A^{\nu}+\nabla_{\mu}\phi

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(4.3.24)

\displaystyle(\nabla^{2}-m^{2})\varphi-im\phi

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(4.3.25)

\displaystyle\phi+im\varphi-i\nabla_{\mu}A^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(4.3.26)

which, upon eliminating the auxiliary field, yield

\displaystyle(\nabla^{2}-m^{2})A_{\mu}-\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}A^{\nu}+R_{\mu\nu}A^{\nu}-m\nabla_{\mu}\varphi

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(4.3.27)

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\varphi+m\nabla_{\mu}A^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\ .

(4.3.28)

Equations (
4.3.27
)–(
4.3.28
) represent the field equations of the Proca theory on a general curved spacetime in its Stückelberg formulation. In particular, the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
spinning particle provides a first-quantized version of the minimal extension for a theory of a free massive vector field in curved spacetime, without the inclusion of possible non-minimal couplings to the background in the corresponding spacetime quantum field theory
[
58
,
98
]
. Their absence is attributed to the reasons previously discussed. As a final note, the gauge symmetry, using (
4.3.13
), reads

\displaystyle\delta A_{\mu}=\nabla_{\mu}\Sigma\ ,\quad\delta\varphi=-m\,\Sigma\ .

(4.3.29)

4.3.2
{\mathcal{N}}=4
massive spinning particle and linearized massive gravity

In the remainder of this section, the methods previously discussed are exploited to give a mass to the graviton, considering a flat target spacetime for the time being.

4.3.2.1
Dimensional reduction approach

The first method proceeds along the lines of what has been shown for the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
case, namely through the reduction of the higher-dimensional massless model. The fermionic coordinates carry a flavor index

\displaystyle\xi^{M}_{i}=(\psi^{\mu}_{i},\theta_{i})\ ,\quad\bar{\xi}^{Mi}=(\bar{\psi}^{\mu i},\bar{\theta}^{i})\ ,

(4.3.30)

with CCR

\{\bar{\psi}^{\mu i},\psi^{\nu}_{j}\}=\delta^{i}_{j}\,\eta^{\mu\nu}\ ,\quad\{\bar{\theta}^{i},\theta_{j}\}=\delta^{i}_{j}\ .

(4.3.31)

The same applies to the
SUSY
constraints (
4.1.3
)

\displaystyle H=\frac{1}{2}\left(p^{\mu}p_{\mu}+m^{2}\right)\ ,\quad q_{i}=p\cdot\psi_{i}+m\theta_{i}\ ,\quad\bar{q}^{i}=p\cdot\bar{\psi}^{i}+m\bar{\theta}^{i}\ ,

(4.3.32)

that satisfy the same first-class algebra

\displaystyle\{q_{i},\bar{q}^{j}\}=2\delta_{i}^{j}H\ ,\quad[q_{i},H]=[\bar{q}^{i},H]=\{q_{i},q_{j}\}=\{\bar{q}^{i},\bar{q}^{j}\}=0

(4.3.33)

despite the mass improvement. Regarding the
so(4)
symmetry constraints, equations (
4.1.10
) become

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{J}_{i}{}^{j}&=\psi_{i}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi_{j}}+\theta_{i}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{j}}-\frac{d+1}{2}\,\delta_{i}^{j}\ ,\\
\mathrm{Tr}^{ij}&=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\psi_{i}\cdot\partial\psi_{j}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta_{i}\;\partial\theta_{j}}\ ,\\
\mathrm{G}_{ij}&=\psi_{i}\cdot\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}\;\theta_{j}\ .\end{split}

(4.3.34)

At this point, the BRST quantization proceeds as outlined in section
4.2
, with the BRST system defined by (
4.2.11
). The BRST operator (
4.2.13
) takes the usual form when acting on wavefunctions
\Psi(x,\psi,\theta\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})

\mathcal{Q}=c\,(\Box-m^{2})+\gamma_{i}\,\bar{q}^{i}-q_{i}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta_{i}}-\gamma_{i}\,\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\beta_{i}\partial c}

(4.3.35)

and is still nilpotent, for the same reasons outlined in the previous section. The relevant
so(4)
generators (
4.2.14
) to be imposed on the extended BRST Hilbert space are extended to commute with
\mathcal{Q}
, including the extra fermionic oscillators

\begin{split}{\mathcal{J}}_{i}&=N_{\psi_{i}}+N_{\theta_{i}}+N_{\gamma_{i}}+N_{\beta_{i}}-\frac{d-1}{2}\ ,\\[5.69054pt]
{\mathcal{Y}}&=\psi_{1}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi_{2}}+\theta_{1}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{2}}+\gamma_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma_{2}}+\beta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta_{2}}\ ,\\[5.69054pt]
{{\cal T}\!r}&=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\psi_{1}\cdot\partial\psi_{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta_{1}\;\partial\theta_{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\gamma_{1}\partial\beta_{2}}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\gamma_{2}\partial\beta_{1}}\ ,\end{split}

(4.3.36)

The physical subspace has a fixed
U(1)\times U(1)
charge corresponding to
\tfrac{3-d}{2}
. Let us comment on the fact that only in three spacetime dimensions the wavefunction
\Psi\in\ker{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
, which becomes relevant when dealing with the construction of the worldline path integral.
10
10

10

This is the content of Chapter
6
.
In four spacetime dimensions, physical states are charged
-\tfrac{1}{2}
, and we shall consider the shifted operator
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{J}}+\tfrac{1}{2}
for the sake of notational simplicity. To emphasize the impact of the introduction of a mass term, we follow the same steps as in the massless case to impose the condition
\Psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{red}}
, with the reduced Hilbert space defined as the kernel of
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{Y}},{{\cal T}\!r}
(
4.3.36
), still collectively denoted as
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
. From
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{i}\ket{\Psi}=0
descends the general form of the wavefunction

\displaystyle\Psi(x,\psi,\theta\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=\left.\Psi\right|_{m=0}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+n\,\theta_{1}\gamma_{2}+p\,\theta_{1}\beta_{2}+q\,\gamma_{1}\theta_{2}+r\,\beta_{1}\theta_{2}+s_{\mu}\,\theta_{1}\psi_{2}^{\mu}+t_{\mu}\,\psi_{1}^{\mu}\theta_{2}+u\,\theta_{1}\theta_{2}

(4.3.37)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+n^{\ast}\,\theta_{1}\gamma_{2}c+p^{\ast}\,\theta_{1}\beta_{2}c+q^{\ast}\,\gamma_{1}\theta_{2}c+r^{\ast}\,\beta_{1}\theta_{2}c+s^{\ast}_{\mu}\,\theta_{1}\psi_{2}^{\mu}c+t^{\ast}_{\mu}\,\psi_{1}^{\mu}\theta_{2}c+u^{\ast}\,\theta_{1}\theta_{2}c\ ,

where in the first line
\left.\Psi\right|_{m=0}
denotes the massless wavefunction (
4.2.1
). The condition
{\mathcal{Y}}\ket{\Psi}=0
produces, in addition to the “massless” contribution (
4.2.16
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&n=-q\ ,\quad p=-r\ ,\quad s_{\mu}=t_{\mu}\ ,\\
&n^{\ast}=-q^{\ast}\ ,\quad p^{\ast}=-r^{\ast}\ ,\quad s^{\ast}_{\mu}=t^{\ast}_{\mu}\ ,\end{split}

(4.3.38)

while the remaining
{{\cal T}\!r}\ket{\Psi}=0
produces

\displaystyle e=\tfrac{1}{2}a^{\mu}_{\mu}+\tfrac{1}{2}u\ ,\quad e^{\ast}=\tfrac{1}{2}a^{\ast\mu}_{\mu}+\tfrac{1}{2}u^{\ast}\ .

(4.3.39)

It is now possible to identify the
BV
spectrum of the theory, which becomes evident after renaming the field components as in (
4.2.18
) along with

\displaystyle s_{\mu}\longrightarrow A_{\mu}\quad u\longrightarrow\varphi\quad p\longrightarrow\phi\quad p^{\ast}\longrightarrow\Phi

(4.3.40)

and, for the corresponding antifields,

\displaystyle s^{\ast}_{\mu}\longrightarrow A^{\ast}_{\mu}\quad u^{\ast}\longrightarrow\varphi^{\ast}\quad n\longrightarrow\phi^{\ast}\quad n^{\ast}\longrightarrow\Phi^{\ast}\ .

(4.3.41)

Requiring
\Psi
to be Grassmann-even and have ghost number zero the field content gets assigned with the corresponding Grassmann parities and ghost numbers as reported in Table
4.3
. The most general string field
\Psi
in
\ker\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
reads

\displaystyle\Psi(x,\psi,\theta\,|\,{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=h_{\mu\nu}(x)\,\psi^{\mu}_{1}\psi^{\nu}_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,h(x)\,(\gamma_{1}\beta_{2}-\gamma_{2}\beta_{1})-\tfrac{i}{2}\,v_{\mu}(x)\,(\psi^{\mu}_{1}\beta_{2}-\psi^{\mu}_{2}\beta_{1})c

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\tfrac{i}{2}\,\zeta_{\mu}(x)\,(\psi^{\mu}_{1}\beta_{2}-\psi^{\mu}_{2}\beta_{1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-iA_{\mu}(x)\,(\theta_{1}\psi_{2}^{\mu}+\psi_{1}^{\mu}\theta_{2})-\varphi(x)\,(2\theta_{1}\theta_{2}+\gamma_{1}\beta_{2}-\gamma_{2}\beta_{1})+\phi(x)\,(\theta_{1}\beta_{2}-\theta_{2}\beta_{1})c

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\Phi(x)\,(\theta_{1}\beta_{2}-\theta_{2}\beta_{1})\ ,

(4.3.42)

where, for the sake of simplicity, the antifields content is left implicit.
