\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=\frac{z}{60(z+1)^{2}}

(5.1.27)

where in reaching the last-but-one line we used the regulated Green equation for the component
F
of the fermionic propagator
\Delta_{\rm{AF}\,}
, see eqs. (
5.B.7
), (
5.B.16
) and (
5.B.17
) of Appendix
5.B
, and then removed the regularization by sending the extra dimensions to zero (
d\to 0
). Finally, taking into account the
\omega
partner of the integrals in (
5.1.27
), one gets the following result:

\displaystyle\langle S_{\rm C1}S_{\rm C2}\rangle=\left(\frac{1}{120}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{R}{180D}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\right)\left(\frac{z}{(z+1)^{2}}+\frac{\omega}{(\omega+1)^{2}}\right)\;.

(5.1.28)

Once having worked out systematically all the corrections up to and including order
T^{3}
related to the connected graphs, it only remains to exponentiate them and, finally, Taylor expand in
T
so as to reach the desired order and get the full result.

5.1.4
Seeley-DeWitt coefficients

The calculation of the various terms in the perturbative expansion delivers the following coefficients in the perturbative series (
5.1.18
), including the newly found
a_{3}(D)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{0}(D)&=\frac{D(D-3)}{2}\end{split}

(5.1.29)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{1}(D)&=\frac{D^{2}-3D-36}{12}\;R\end{split}

(5.1.30)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{2}(D)&=\frac{5D^{3}-17D^{2}-354D-720}{720D}\;R^{2}+\frac{D^{2}-33D+540}{360}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\end{split}

(5.1.31)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{3}(D)&=\frac{35D^{4}-147D^{3}-3670D^{2}-13560D-30240}{90720D^{2}}\;R^{3}+\frac{7D^{3}-230D^{2}+3357D+12600}{15120D}\;R\,R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\\[3.00003pt]
&\phantom{=}+\frac{17D^{2}-555D-15120}{90720}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{D^{2}-39D-1080}{3240}\;R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta}R^{\mu\rho\sigma\nu}R_{\rho}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\sigma}\ .\end{split}

(5.1.32)

The above expressions are understood to be gauge-invariant, as they have been calculated specifically on Einstein spaces for the reasons previously discussed. As we shall discuss later, the newly computed coefficient
a_{3}(D)
plays a key role in quantum gravity in six dimensions, as it is related to the logarithmic divergences, hence it will be useful to read its value explicitly in this case as well. In
D=6
it reduces to

\displaystyle\left.a_{3}\right|_{D=6}

\displaystyle=-\frac{799}{11340}\;R^{3}+\frac{481}{1680}\;R\,R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}-\frac{991}{5040}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}-\frac{71}{180}\,R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta}R^{\mu\rho\sigma\nu}R_{\rho}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\sigma}\ .

(5.1.33)

Also, its expression on maximally symmetric spaces (MSS) may be useful for future reference. As we are not aware of any such calculation carried out in the literature we list it here. Using the relations in Appendix
5.A
, we find that (
5.1.32
) on MSS collapses into

\displaystyle a_{3}^{\rm MSS}(D)=\frac{35D^{6}-217D^{5}-3257D^{4}-9239D^{3}+37470D^{2}+183672D-302400}{90720(D-1)^{2}D^{2}}\;R^{3}\ ,

(5.1.34)

yielding in
D=6

\displaystyle\left.a_{3}^{\rm MSS}\right|_{D=6}=-\frac{3181}{63000}\;R^{3}\ .

(5.1.35)

5.1.4.1
Ghost and Graviton separately

For future comparison with the results of the heat kernel method calculation, it is useful to project on the degrees of freedom of the ghost and graviton respectively. To this task, we must use the right measures in the path integral (
5.1.14
). These can be found generalizing to our case the worldline partition functions of Ref.
[
43
]
leading us to

\displaystyle P_{\rm gh}(z,\omega)=\frac{(z+1)^{D}}{z^{2}}\frac{1}{\omega}\ ,

(5.1.36)

\displaystyle P_{\rm gr}(z,\omega)=\frac{2(z+1)^{D}}{\omega z^{2}}+\frac{(\omega+1)^{D-2}(z+1)^{D-2}(z-\omega)^{2}(\omega z-1)}{2\omega^{3}z^{3}}\ .

(5.1.37)

Indeed, note that

P_{\rm gr}(z,\omega)-2\,P_{\rm gh}(z,\omega)=P(z,\omega)\ .

(5.1.38)

It is therefore possible to obtain the contributions to the Seeley-DeWitt coefficients coming only from the ghost (graviton) projecting onto the desired Hilbert space via
P_{\rm gh}\,(P_{\rm gr})
. Let us emphasize that these results are not new
per se
since the heat kernel procedure requires calculating them individually and then putting them together, as we shall see. What is new here is the possibility of obtaining them also from the worldline viewpoint of the
{\cal N}=4
particle. Therefore, regarding the ghost we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{0}^{\rm gh}(D)&=D\end{split}

(5.1.39)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{1}^{\rm gh}(D)&=\frac{D+6}{6}\;R\end{split}

(5.1.40)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{2}^{\rm gh}(D)&=\frac{5D^{2}+58D+180}{360D}\;R^{2}+\frac{D-15}{180}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\end{split}

(5.1.41)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{3}^{\rm gh}(D)&=\frac{35D^{3}+588D^{2}+3512D+7560}{45360D^{2}}\;R^{3}+\frac{7D^{2}-62D-714}{7560D}\;R\,R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{2}\\[3.00003pt]
&\phantom{=}+\frac{17D-252}{45360}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{D-18}{1620}\;R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta}R^{\mu\rho\sigma\nu}R_{\rho}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\sigma}\ ,\end{split}

(5.1.42)

while concerning the graviton

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{0}^{\rm gr}(D)&=\frac{1}{2}D(D+1)\end{split}

(5.1.43)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{1}^{\rm gr}(D)&=\frac{1}{12}\left(D^{2}+D-12\right)\;R\end{split}

(5.1.44)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{2}^{\rm gr}(D)&=\frac{\left(5D^{2}+3D-122\right)}{720}\;R^{2}+\frac{\left(D^{2}-29D+480\right)}{360}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\end{split}

(5.1.45)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{3}^{\rm gr}(D)&=\frac{35D^{3}-7D^{2}-1318D+488}{90720D}\;R^{3}+\frac{7D^{3}-202D^{2}+3109D+9744}{15120D}\;R\,R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{2}\\[3.00003pt]
&\phantom{=}+\frac{17D^{2}-487D-16128}{90720}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{D^{2}-35D-1152}{3240}\;R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta}R^{\mu\rho\sigma\nu}R_{\rho}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\sigma}\ .\end{split}

(5.1.46)

One can easily see that the coefficient
a_{0}(D)
correctly reproduces the expected degrees of freedom in
D
dimensions. Moreover, it is immediate to check that by summing up the contributions as prescribed by (
5.1.38
) we obtain the correct total coefficients (
5.1.29
)–(
5.1.32
).

5.2
Heat kernel method

The gauge-invariant coefficients computed from the
\mathcal{N}=4
spinning particle can be obtained in an equivalent but completely independent manner by exploiting the heat kernel method. In this section, we apply it to the case of Euclidean perturbative quantum gravity, to reproduce the coefficients (
5.1.30
)–(
5.1.32
) and provide a strong consistency check for our computations.

5.2.1
Relevant formulae

We recall here, for ease of reference, the main equations from the
prelude to Part II
, which will be needed to carry out the heat kernel computation. In particular, recall that an elliptic second-order differential operator can be written in the form

\mathcal{Q}=-\nabla_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\!({\cal A})}^{2}-V

(5.2.1)

and that the associated heat kernel
K(x,x^{\prime};T)
admits, for small Euclidean time
T\to 0^{+}
, the coincident-point expansion

K(x,x;T)\sim(4\pi T)^{-\frac{D}{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}T^{j}a_{j}(x)\ ,

(5.2.2)

in terms of the local heat kernel coefficients
a_{j}(x)
. These coefficients determine the divergent structure of the one-loop effective action, which can be expressed as

\Gamma=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}{T}}{T}\ \exp{(-T m^2)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}{{}^{D}x}\,\sqrt{g}}{(4\pi T)^{\frac{D}{2}}}\ \operatorname{sTr}{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}T^{j}a_{j}(x)}\ .

(5.2.3)

The problem of finding the
UV
divergences of the effective action is then reduced to the computation of Seeley-DeWitt coefficients for a generic theory, which has already been carried out up to
a_{4}(x)

[
21
]
. For application to perturbative quantum gravity, we will consider here the first four coefficients, i.e. from
a_{0}(x)
to
a_{3}(x)
. The fourth coefficient
a_{3}(x)
, in particular, has been computed for the first time by Gilkey
[
176
]
, and later confirmed by Avramidi through a fully covariant method
[
19
]
.
6
6

6

It is important to note that the notation we employ here for the Riemann tensor, Ricci tensor, and scalar is the same of Ref.
[
40
,
26
]
, reported in Eq. (
5.A.1
). Some references, notably Refs.
[
176
,
250
]
, adopt different conventions.

We now list the general results for the coefficients corresponding to the operator (
5.2.1
) up to
a_{3}(x)
, as taken from
[
176
,
26
]
. Consider an exponentiated form of the heat kernel series:

\operatorname{sTr}{\left[\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}T^{j}a_{j}(x)\right]}:=\operatorname{sTr}{\left[\exp{\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty} T^j \alpha_j(x)\right)}\right]}\ .

(5.2.4)

It is useful to define, for the sake of brevity, the remainder

\beta_{j}(x):=a_{j}(x)-\alpha_{j}(x)\ ,

(5.2.5)

which is evaluated, up to the third order, as

\beta_{0}=\beta_{1}=0\ ,\quad\beta_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}\ ,\quad\beta_{3}=\frac{1}{6}\alpha_{1}^{3}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\ .

(5.2.6)

The coefficients
\alpha_{j}(x)
are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{0}(x)&=\mathbbm{1}\;,\end{split}

(5.2.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{1}(x)&=\frac{1}{6}R\mathbbm{1}+V\;,\end{split}

(5.2.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{2}(x)&=\frac{1}{6}\nabla^{2}\left(\frac{1}{5}R\mathbbm{1}+V\right)+\frac{1}{180}\left(R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}-R_{\mu\nu}^{2}\right)\mathbbm{1}+\frac{1}{12}\Omega_{\mu\nu}^{2}\;,\end{split}

(5.2.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{3}(x)&=\frac{1}{7!}\left[18\nabla^{4}R+17(\nabla_{\mu}R)^{2}-2(\nabla_{\mu}R_{\nu\sigma})^{2}-4\nabla_{\mu}R_{\nu\sigma}\nabla^{\nu}R^{\mu\sigma}+9(\nabla_{\alpha}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma})^{2}-8R_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{2}R^{\mu\nu}\right.\\
&\phantom{=}+12R^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}R+12R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\nabla^{2}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+\frac{8}{9}R_{\mu}{}^{\nu}R_{\nu}{}^{\sigma}R_{\sigma}{}^{\mu}-\frac{8}{3}R_{\mu\nu}R_{\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\rho\nu\sigma}\\
&\phantom{=}\left.-\frac{16}{3}R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu}{}_{\rho\sigma\tau}R^{\nu\rho\sigma\tau}+\frac{44}{9}R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma}R_{\rho\sigma}{}^{\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{80}{9}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\alpha\rho\beta}R^{\nu}{}_{\alpha}{}^{\sigma}{}_{\beta}\right]\mathbbm{1}\\
&\phantom{=}+\frac{2}{6!}\left[8(\nabla_{\mu}\Omega_{\nu\sigma})^{2}+2(\nabla^{\mu}\Omega_{\mu\nu})^{2}+12\Omega_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{2}\Omega^{\mu\nu}-12\Omega_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Omega_{\nu}{}^{\sigma}\Omega_{\sigma}{}^{\mu}+6R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Omega^{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\rho\sigma}-4R_{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\mu\sigma}\Omega^{\nu}{}_{\sigma}\right.\\
&\phantom{=}\left.+6\nabla^{4}V+30(\nabla_{\mu}V)^{2}+4R_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{\mu}\nabla^{\nu}V+12\nabla_{\mu}R\nabla^{\mu}V\right]\ .\end{split}

(5.2.10)

They will be used in the next section.

5.2.2
Euclidean quantum gravity

Consider a
D
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
(\mathcal{M},\bm{G})
equipped with a metric tensor
\bm{G}
having Euclidean signature. The starting point for our treatment of gravity is the Einstein–Hilbert action,

S_{\rm EH}[\bm{G}]=-\frac{1}{k^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{D}x}\ \sqrt{G}\left[R(\bm{G})-2\Lambda\right]\ ,

(5.2.11)

where
k^{2}:=16\pi G_{\rm N}
, being
G_{\rm N}
the Newton constant,
R(\bm{G})
is the Ricci scalar computed from
\bm{G}
, and
G:=\left|\det{G_{\mu\nu}}\right|
. A cosmological constant
\Lambda
has also been included. By employing the background field method, we split the metric tensor
\bm{G}
into a fixed classical background
\bm{g}
and “small” quantum perturbations
\bm{h}
, namely:

G_{\mu\nu}(x)=g_{\mu\nu}(x)+h_{\mu\nu}(x)\ .

(5.2.12)

As a consequence of this splitting, the action (
5.2.11
) can be expanded in power series in the fluctuations
\bm{h}
. Since we are interested in the one-loop level of accuracy, we will be concerned with the second-order term in
\bm{h}
, which reads

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{D}x}\sqrt{g}\left[-\frac{1}{4}h^{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla^{2}+2\Lambda-R\right)h_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{8}h\left(\nabla^{2}+2\Lambda-R\right)h-\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\mu}h\right)^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\phantom{=\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{D}x}\sqrt{g}+}\left.-\frac{1}{2}\left(h^{\mu\lambda}h_{\lambda}{}^{\nu}-hh^{\mu\nu}\right)R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mu\lambda}h^{\nu\rho}R_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}\right]\ .

(5.2.13)

It is important to note that in (
5.2.2
) the Ricci tensor
R_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu}(\bm{g})
and scalar
R=R(\bm{g})
, as well as covariant derivatives
\nabla_{\mu}=\nabla_{\mu}(\bm{g})
, are computed with respect to the background metric
\bm{g}
. The gauge symmetries acting on
\bm{h}
and leaving the background metric
\bm{g}
invariant are then BRST quantized by introducing the ghost
c
and antighost
\overline{c}
fields, and adding to the action the Slavnov variation of the de Donder gauge-fixing function
f_{\mu}=\nabla^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\mu}h
, see for example
[
43
]
for further details. The final result is

S_{2}[\bm{h},c,\overline{c}]=S_{\rm gr}[\bm{h}]+S_{\rm gh}[c,\overline{c}]\ ,

(5.2.14)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\rm gr}[\bm{h}]&=\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{D}x}\sqrt{g}\left[-\frac{1}{4}h^{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla^{2}+2\Lambda-R\right)h_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{8}h\left(\nabla^{2}+2\Lambda-R\right)h\right.\\
&\phantom{=\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{D}x}\sqrt{g}+}\left.-\frac{1}{2}\left(h^{\mu\lambda}h_{\lambda}{}^{\nu}-hh^{\mu\nu}\right)R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mu\lambda}h^{\nu\rho}R_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}\right]\ ,\end{split}

(5.2.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\rm gh}[c,\overline{c}]&=\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{D}x}\sqrt{g}\ \bar{c}^{\mu}\left(\nabla^{2}c_{\mu}+R_{\mu\nu}c^{\nu}\right)\ .\end{split}

(5.2.16)

We are now able to identify from the actions
S_{\rm gr}
and
S_{\rm gh}
the invertible kinetic operators for the graviton and ghost fields, denoted by
F_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}
, respectively. By setting

S_{\rm gr}[\bm{h}]=\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{D}x}\ \sqrt{g}\ \frac{1}{2}h_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad S_{\rm gh}[c,\overline{c}]=\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{D}x}\ \sqrt{g}\ \bar{c}_{\mu}\mathcal{F}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}c^{\nu}\ ,

(5.2.17)

and exploiting the properties of Einstein spaces, we find

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}{}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{\mu}^{\alpha}\delta_{\nu}^{\beta}+\delta_{\mu}^{\beta}\delta_{\nu}^{\alpha}\right)\nabla^{2}-R_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}{}_{\nu}{}^{\beta}-R_{\mu}{}^{\beta}{}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}

(5.2.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mu}_{\ \nu}

\displaystyle=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\left(\nabla^{2}+\frac{1}{D}R\right)\ ,

(5.2.19)

where the graviton operator indices are raised and lowered with the DeWitt supermetric

\gamma^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}:=\frac{1}{4}\left(g^{\mu\alpha}g^{\nu\beta}+g^{\mu\beta}g^{\nu\alpha}-g^{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\right)\ ,\quad\gamma_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}=g_{\mu\alpha}g_{\nu\beta}+g_{\mu\beta}g_{\nu\alpha}-\frac{2}{D-2}g_{\mu\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\ .

(5.2.20)

The reasons for immediately reducing to Einstein spaces — that is, to compute the coefficients directly on-shell — are twofold. Firstly, this allows us a direct comparison with the worldline results, even separately for ghost and graviton, since the on-shell condition is forced by the quantum consistency of the
\mathcal{N}=4
spinning particle. Secondly, proceeding otherwise the results would not be gauge-invariant, but rather would depend on the gauge chosen, as expected for gauge theories, see for instance the recent analysis carried out in Ref.
[
94
]
.

Since (
5.2.18
)–(
5.2.19
) are elliptic second-order differential operators, they can be treated within the heat kernel expansion, and the coefficients (
5.2.7
)–(
5.2.10
) can be computed by identifying the explicit formulae for
\mathbbm{1}
,
V
and
\Omega_{\mu\nu}
. The ghost field configuration space is
D
-dimensional, and by comparing (
5.2.19
) with (
5.2.1
), as well as recalling that

[\nabla_{\mu},\nabla_{\nu}]c^{\rho}=R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho}{}_{\sigma}c^{\sigma}\ ,

(5.2.21)

we conclude that the substitutions to be performed in the heat kernel coefficients (
5.2.7
)–(
5.2.10
) are

\begin{cases}\mathbbm{1}\ \leftrightarrow\ \delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\\[3.00003pt]
V\ \leftrightarrow\ \dfrac{1}{D}R\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\\[5.0pt]
(\Omega_{\mu\nu})^{\rho}_{\ \sigma}\ \leftrightarrow\ R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho}{}_{\sigma}\ .\end{cases}

(5.2.22)

Note that in this expression the indices
\mu
,
\nu
label the different elements of the gauge field strength
\Omega_{\mu\nu}
, which are
D\times D
matrices whose components are given by the (spacetime) indices
\rho
,
\sigma
. On the other hand, for the graviton field the configuration space is
\frac{1}{2}D(D+1)
dimensional (space of symmetric tensors) and the substitutions to be performed are

\begin{cases}\mathbbm{1}\ \leftrightarrow\ \delta_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\\
V\ \leftrightarrow\ \mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\alpha\beta}\\
(\Omega_{\mu\nu})_{\rho\sigma}{}^{\alpha\beta}\ \leftrightarrow\ R_{\rho\sigma}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\mu\nu}\end{cases}

(5.2.23)

where

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu\nu}{}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{\mu}^{\alpha}\delta_{\nu}^{\beta}+\delta_{\mu}^{\beta}\delta_{\nu}^{\alpha}\right)

(5.2.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle:=R_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}{}_{\nu}{}^{\beta}+R_{\mu}{}^{\beta}{}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\ ,

(5.2.25)

and the commutator is given by a symmetrized version of the Riemann tensor

[\nabla_{\mu},\nabla_{\nu}]h_{\rho\sigma}=R_{\rho\sigma}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\mu\nu}\,h_{\alpha\beta}\ ,

(5.2.26)

where

R_{\rho\sigma}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\mu\nu}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{\rho}^{\alpha}R_{\sigma}{}^{\beta}{}_{\mu\nu}+\delta_{\rho}^{\beta}R_{\sigma}{}^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}+\delta_{\sigma}^{\alpha}R_{\rho}{}^{\beta}{}_{\mu\nu}+\delta_{\sigma}^{\beta}R_{\rho}{}^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}\right)\ .

(5.2.27)

At this point, the computations are tedious but straightforward, see Appendix
5.C
for details. The final results for the ghost and graviton fields separately are precisely the coefficients (
5.1.39
)–(
5.1.42
) and (
5.1.43
)–(
5.1.46
) obtained from the Worldline Formalism. The total coefficients for the physical graviton, according to the supertrace appearing in (
5.2.3
), as recognized also
from (
5.1.38
), are given by

{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[a_{j}\right]}={\textrm{Tr}}{\left[a_{j}^{\rm gr}\right]}-2{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[a_{j}^{\rm gh}\right]}\ .

(5.2.28)

Again, the results obtained reproduce the ones coming from worldline computations (
5.1.30
)–(
5.1.32
), providing a strong cross-check for the correctness of both.

5.3
On one-loop divergences of quantum gravity

The coefficients (
5.1.30
)–(
5.1.32
), which include the newly computed coefficient
a_{3}(D)
, allow for further investigations of the issue of divergences in the quantum theory of gravity. Thus, let us review some crucial results from the literature and discuss how our newly-calculated coefficient
a_{3}(D)
fits into the picture, thus providing us with additional confirmation of the validity of our result. We focus our discussion on the spacetime of dimensions
D=4
and
D=6
, as in these cases there are no additional divergences on top of the one we have already computed (new divergences start to appear from
D=8
onwards). The type of divergences arising in quantum gravity emerge naturally from the representation of the one-loop effective action with a short proper time expansion, which we can read both from the worldline viewpoint (
5.1.18
) and from the heat kernel one (
5.2.3
):

\Gamma[g_{\mu\nu}]=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dT}{T^{1+\frac{D}{2}}}\int\frac{d^{D}x_{0}}{\left(4\pi\right)^{\frac{D}{2}}}\sqrt{g(x_{0})}\left[a_{0}+a_{1}T+a_{2}T^{2}+a_{3}T^{3}+\mathcal{O}(T^{4})\right]\ .

(5.3.1)

We are interested in studying the
UV
divergences that arise from the
T\rightarrow 0
limit of the proper time integration. Setting
D=4
we recognize that possible divergences arise from the coefficients
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}
,
with
a_{2}
being associated with the logarithmic divergence. In
D=6
, also
a_{3}
gives rise to an additional divergence, the logarithmic one in that dimension.

One may wonder how to relate the
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
pole of dimensional regularization in
QFT
, widely present in the literature, with our situation. To address this point, it is useful to evaluate the proper time integral term by term in (
5.3.1
), to display the gamma function dependence. We find

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dT}{T^{1+\frac{D}{2}}}T^{p}\,e^{-m^{2}T}=(m^{2})^{\frac{D}{2}-p}\,\Gamma\left(p-\frac{D}{2}\right)\ ,

(5.3.2)

where
p=2,3
correspond to our cases of interest
D=4,6
, respectively. Now, using dimensional regularization, namely taking
D=2p-2\epsilon
and expanding the gamma function, we see the appearance of the usual
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
pole as the leading divergent term: it corresponds precisely to the logarithmic divergences seen in dimensional regularization
[
228
]
.

In general, one has to deal also with infrared divergences: for the sake of our discussion, they can be avoided either by introducing an upper cutoff in the proper time or by keeping a “small” mass regulator
m
, as we have done above.

5.3.1
Pure gravity in four dimensions

It has long been known since the pioneering work of ’t Hooft and Veltman
[
1
]
, that pure gravity with vanishing cosmological constant is a renormalizable theory at one-loop in
D=4
. It is free of logarithmic divergences, while other divergences are not seen in dimensional regularization, and in any case they can be eliminated by renormalization. The same does not hold in the case of a non-vanishing cosmological constant, as found by Christensen and Duff
[
102
]
. Let us briefly review these statements in the light of our calculations. Setting
D=4
in (
5.3.1
) we see that the different powers of
T
give rise to the quartic, quadratic, and logarithmic divergences parametrized by
a_{0}
,
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, respectively. From (
5.1.31
), we read

\left.a_{2}\right|_{D=4}=-\frac{29}{40}\,R^{2}+\frac{53}{45}\,R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\ .

(5.3.3)

These numerical values for the one-loop four-dimensional logarithmic divergences of quantum gravity with non-vanishing cosmological constant coincide precisely with those calculated long ago by Christensen and Duff.
7
7

7

For comparison, one has to make evident the cosmological constant term with the on-shell condition (
5.1.1
), which reads
R=4\Lambda
.
The term proportional to
R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}
could be neglected, as thanks to the Chern–Gauss–Bonnet theorem for four-dimensional Einstein manifolds it is proportional to a total derivative, and thus eliminable from the effective action, but the remaining term proportional
to
R^{2}
cannot be renormalized away by redefining the parameters of the Einstein–Hilbert action. The theory is not renormalizable.

On the other hand, setting the cosmological constant to vanish, one finds that the on-shell background satisfies
R_{\mu\nu}=0
, and thus
R=0
. The logarithmic divergence reduces to

a_{2}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}D=4\\[0.81949pt]
\Lambda=0\end{subarray}}=\frac{53}{45}\,R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}

(5.3.4)

which in four dimensions is a total derivative, as discussed earlier, and can be eliminated from the effective action. Thus, one recovers the result that the one-loop logarithmic divergences of pure quantum gravity without cosmological constant vanish in four dimensions. This property does not hold true anymore at two-loops, as found by Goroff and Sagnotti
[
178
]
and verified by van de Ven
[
244
]
.
Returning to the one-loop divergences for vanishing cosmological constant, one finds that also
a_{1}
vanishes. This leaves only the quartic divergence proportional to
a_{0}
, which gives the number of degrees of freedom of the graviton. It requires a renormalization of the cosmological constant back to zero, which makes the theory rather unnatural in the technical sense of ’t Hooft
[
2
]
, but in any case renormalizable at one-loop.

For arbitrary nonzero values of the cosmological constant, the quadratic divergence related to
a_{1}
is not vanishing anymore and its value at
D=4

\left.a_{1}\right|_{D=4}=-\frac{8}{3}\,R

(5.3.5)

reproduces the gauge-invariant result already computed in Refs.
[
43
,
207
]
. It can be renormalized away by redefining the Newton constant. Finally, the coefficient
a_{3}
gives rise to a finite term in the four-dimensional effective action, but its physical meaning is unclear. It is gauge invariant, but infrared divergences invalidate a local expansion of the effective action as delivered by the small proper time approximation of the heat kernel, which is useful to locate the
UV
divergences.

5.3.2
Pure gravity in six dimensions

The newly computed coefficient
a_{3}
(
5.1.32
), allows us to see what happens in six spacetime dimensions. Setting
D=6
in the coefficients (
5.1.29
)–(
5.1.32
) we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.a_{0}\right|_{D=6}&=9\;,\qquad\qquad\quad\left.a_{1}\right|_{D=6}=-\frac{3}{2}\;R\;,\qquad\qquad\quad\left.a_{2}\right|_{D=6}=-\frac{11}{20}\;R^{2}+\frac{21}{20}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\end{split}

(5.3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.a_{3}\right|_{D=6}&=-\frac{799}{11340}\;R^{3}+\frac{481}{1680}\;R\,R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}-\frac{991}{5040}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}-\frac{71}{180}\;R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta}R^{\mu\rho\sigma\nu}R_{\rho}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\sigma}\ ,\end{split}

(5.3.7)

that furnish the full list of one-loop divergences of quantum gravity with cosmological constant in six dimensions. We stress that these coefficients are gauge invariant, and thus any other method of calculation should reproduce these values.

A comparison with the literature can be made by setting the cosmological constant to zero and considering the logarithmic divergence parametrized by
a_{3}
, which reduces to

a_{3}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}D=6\\[0.81949pt]
\Lambda=0\end{subarray}}=-\frac{991}{5040}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}-\frac{71}{180}\,R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta}R^{\mu\rho\sigma\nu}R_{\rho}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\sigma}\ .

(5.3.8)

These two remaining terms are proportional to two invariants that are generally independent of each other. However, it turns out that in six dimensions there exists an integral relation that connects them. It involves the use of the Chern–Gauss–Bonnet theorem and the introduction of the Euler character
\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})
, as explained in Ref.
[
247
]
and discussed more extensively in Appendix
5.D
. Bottom line, we can further simplify the coefficient
a_{3}
, which becomes

a_{3}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}D=6\\[0.81949pt]
\Lambda=0\end{subarray}}=\frac{9}{15120}\;R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}\;.

(5.3.9)

It encodes the one-loop logarithmic divergences of pure gravity in six dimensions. We are now in the position of carrying out a comparison with the literature: our value in (
5.3.9
) is in complete agreement with van Nieuwenhuizen’s pioneering calculation
[
248
]
, besides a computational error in the numerical factor in his equation (81), already noted a year later by Critchley
[
107
]
.
Furthermore, confirmation of this value is also found in more recent works, see for instance
[
174
,
130
]
.

As for the general case of arbitrary cosmological constant, we are not aware of similar calculations, although they would certainly be interesting to pursue to further verify our findings.

5.4
Final remarks

In this work, we have investigated the computation of counterterms necessary for the renormalization of the one-loop effective action of quantum gravity with cosmological constant in arbitrary dimensions. Our results are complete for dimensions
D<8
. The counterterms have been computed on-shell, so they furnish gauge invariant quantities characteristic of the quantum theory of gravity.

Our main contribution was the determination of the Seeley-DeWitt coefficient
a_{3}(D)
of perturbative quantum gravity, which to our knowledge has never been reported in its full generality in the literature. When restricted to six dimensions, it parameterizes the logarithmic divergence which was previously known only for the case of vanishing cosmological constant. To cross-check our calculations, we have used two distinct methods: a first-quantized description of the graviton in terms of the
{\cal N}=4
spinning particle and the time-honored heat kernel method, finding complete agreement.

While the utility of heat kernel methods is well-known and they keep being employed in many contexts, see for example
[
44
,
100
]
for some recent applications to trace anomalies,
first-quantized methods for treating the graviton with the
{\cal N}=4
spinning particle are more recent and we have championed them here to show their usefulness. In this respect, it would be interesting to extend the present analysis to the first quantized model that describes the
{\cal N}=0
supergravity
[
88
]
, i.e. the particle theory that has in its spectrum the graviton, the dilaton, and the antisymmetric tensor
B_{\mu\nu}
, as well as extend the present methods to the
U({\mathcal{N}})
spinning particles
[
206
,
41
,
42
,
39
]
to find a useful first-quantized way of describing gravitational theories on complex (Kähler) manifolds, and finally also consider double copy features on the worldline
[
45
]
to address gravitational aspects from a different perspective.

Appendices

Appendix 5.A
Basis of invariants on Einstein manifolds

We use the following conventions for the curvature tensors:

[\nabla_{\mu},\nabla_{\nu}]V^{\lambda}=R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\rho}V^{\rho}\ ,\ \ \ R_{\mu\nu}=R_{\lambda\mu}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu}\ ,\ \ \ R=R^{\mu}{}_{\mu}>0\ {\rm on\ spheres.}

(5.A.1)

A
D
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundaries can be described through an (infinite) basis of curvature monomials
\mathcal{K}_{i}^{n}
. These are geometric invariants of order
n
in the Riemann tensor, Ricci tensor, and scalar curvature, with two covariant derivatives counting as a Riemann tensor. They have been introduced by
[
168
]
and recently reviewed in Refs.
[
137
,
116
]
.
At order
n=3
we use the basis considered in Ref.
[
25
]
which is made of
17
independent invariants:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathcal{K}_{1}=R^{3}&\mathcal{K}_{2}=RR_{\mu\nu}^{2}&\mathcal{K}_{3}=RR_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\\[8.53581pt]
\mathcal{K}_{4}=R_{\mu}{}^{\rho}R_{\rho}{}^{\nu}R_{\nu}{}^{\mu}&\mathcal{K}_{5}=R_{\mu\nu}R_{\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\mu\nu\sigma}&\mathcal{K}_{6}=R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\rho\sigma\lambda}R^{\nu}{}_{\rho\sigma\lambda}\\[8.53581pt]
\mathcal{K}_{7}=R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma}R_{\rho\sigma}{}^{\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}&\mathcal{K}_{8}=R_{\mu\rho\sigma\nu}R^{\rho\alpha\beta\sigma}R_{\alpha}{}^{\mu\nu}{}_{\beta}&\mathcal{K}_{9}=R\nabla^{2}R\\[8.53581pt]
\mathcal{K}_{10}=R_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{2}R^{\mu\nu}&\mathcal{K}_{11}=R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\nabla^{2}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}&\mathcal{K}_{12}=R^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}R\\[8.53581pt]
\mathcal{K}_{13}=(\nabla_{\mu}R_{\rho\sigma})^{2}&\mathcal{K}_{14}=\nabla_{\mu}R_{\nu\rho}\nabla^{\nu}R^{\mu\rho}&\mathcal{K}_{15}=(\nabla_{\alpha}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma})^{2}\\[8.53581pt]
\mathcal{K}_{16}=\nabla^{2}R^{2}&\mathcal{K}_{17}=\nabla^{4}R\ .&\end{array}

All other terms cubic in the curvature are linear combinations of the above invariants after taking into account the symmetry properties and the Bianchi identities of the Riemann tensor.

On Einstein manifolds, the basis (
5.A
) can be reduced further. Einstein metrics are defined by the equation

R_{\mu\nu}=\lambda g_{\mu\nu}

(5.A.8)

that upon contraction leads to
R=\lambda D
. From the second Bianchi identity one finds that
R
and
\lambda
are constant for
D>2

\nabla^{\mu}R_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\nu}R\quad\longrightarrow\quad(D-2)\nabla_{\nu}\lambda=0\quad\longrightarrow\quad\nabla_{\nu}R=0

(5.A.9)

and then from (
5.A.8
), one finds that the Ricci tensor is also covariantly constant for
D>2

\nabla_{\alpha}R_{\mu\nu}=0\,.

(5.A.10)

Let us consider now the second Bianchi identity, namely

\nabla_{\rho}R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}+\nabla_{\beta}R_{\mu\nu\rho\alpha}+\nabla_{\alpha}R_{\mu\nu\beta\rho}=0\,,

(5.A.11)

by contracting the above identity with
g^{\rho\mu}
and using (
5.A.10
) one gets

\nabla^{\mu}R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}=0\ .

(5.A.12)

We are now in the position to reduce the six-dimensional basis of invariants (
5.A
) on Einstein manifolds to a minimal set of independent ones, namely

\mathcal{E}_{1}=R^{3}\;,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathcal{E}_{2}=R\,R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\;,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathcal{E}_{3}=R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}\;,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathcal{E}_{4}=R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta}R^{\mu\rho\sigma\nu}R_{\rho}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\sigma}\;.

(5.A.13)

Indeed we have

\mathcal{K}_{9}=\mathcal{K}_{10}=0\ ,\qquad\mathcal{K}_{2}=\frac{1}{D}\mathcal{E}_{1}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{K}_{4}=-\mathcal{K}_{5}=\frac{1}{D^{2}}\mathcal{E}_{1}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{K}_{6}=\frac{1}{D}\mathcal{E}_{2}\ .

(5.A.14)

Moreover, the only term of (
5.A
) containing covariant derivatives and non-vanishing on Einstein manifolds, i.e.
\mathcal{K}_{11}
,
8
8

8

Since, up to a total derivative term, we have
\mathcal{K}_{15}=-\mathcal{K}_{11}
.
can be written as

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\nabla^{2}R^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\rho}\left(\nabla^{\beta}R^{\mu\nu\rho\alpha}+\nabla^{\alpha}R^{\mu\nu\beta\rho}\right)=-2R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\rho}\nabla^{\beta}R^{\mu\nu\rho\alpha}

\displaystyle=-2R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla^{\beta}\nabla_{\rho}R^{\rho\alpha\mu\nu}+R^{\beta}{}_{\lambda}R^{\lambda\alpha\mu\nu}+R_{\rho}{}^{\beta\alpha}{}_{\lambda}R^{\rho\lambda\mu\nu}+R_{\rho}{}^{\beta\mu}{}_{\lambda}R^{\rho\alpha\lambda\nu}+R_{\rho}{}^{\beta\nu}{}_{\lambda}R^{\rho\alpha\mu\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=-2R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}R_{\rho}{}^{\beta\alpha}{}_{\lambda}R^{\rho\lambda\mu\nu}+\frac{2}{D}R\,R_{\mu\nu\alpha\lambda}^{2}-4R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}R_{\rho}{}^{\beta\mu}{}_{\lambda}R^{\rho\alpha\lambda\nu}

\displaystyle=-R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}R^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\rho\lambda}R^{\rho\lambda\mu\nu}+\frac{2}{D}RR_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{2}+4R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}R^{\nu\lambda\rho\alpha}R_{\lambda}{}^{\mu\beta}{}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{D}\mathcal{E}_{2}-\mathcal{E}_{3}+4\mathcal{E}_{4}\ ,

(5.A.15)

where we made use of the second Bianchi identity, antisymmetry of the Riemann tensor and

[\nabla_{\alpha},\nabla_{\beta}]R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=R_{\alpha\beta\mu}{}^{\lambda}R_{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}+R_{\alpha\beta\nu}{}^{\lambda}R_{\mu\lambda\rho\sigma}+R_{\alpha\beta\rho}{}^{\lambda}R_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}+R_{\alpha\beta\sigma}{}^{\lambda}R_{\mu\nu\rho\lambda}\ .

(5.A.16)

Finally, let us discuss how the basis (
5.A.13
) further simplifies on maximally symmetric spaces (MSS), which form a subset of the Einstein ones, where the Riemann tensor is given by

R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}=\frac{R}{D(D-1)}\left(g_{\mu\alpha}g_{\nu\beta}-g_{\mu\beta}g_{\nu\alpha}\right)\ .

(5.A.17)

The invariants previously defined in (
5.A.13
) all collapse to
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, and one finds

\mathcal{E}_{2}=\frac{2}{D(D-1)}\mathcal{E}_{1}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{E}_{3}=\frac{4}{D^{2}(D-1)^{2}}\mathcal{E}_{1}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{E}_{4}=-\frac{D-2}{D^{2}(D-1)^{2}}\mathcal{E}_{1}\ .

(5.A.18)

These relations allow us to evaluate the newly-computed coefficient
a_{3}(D)
on MSS, giving (
5.1.34
) as a result.

Appendix 5.B
Worldline computations

5.B.1
Worldline propagators

The worldline propagators for the
\mathcal{N}=4
spinning particle descend from the free action (
5.1.15
). Regarding the bosonic quantum fluctuations
q^{\mu}(\tau)
, we considered
DBC
during the evaluation of the derivative expansion of the effective action, namely
q^{\mu}(0)=q^{\mu}(1)=0
. This leads to the
DBC
worldline propagator defined by the two-point function

\langle q^{\mu}(\tau)q^{\nu}(\sigma)\rangle=-2Tg^{\mu\nu}(x_{0})\Delta_{{\rm D}}(\tau,\sigma)\ ,

(5.B.1)

where

\Delta_{{\rm D}}(\tau,\sigma)=\left(\tau-1\right)\sigma\,\theta\left(\tau-\sigma\right)+\left(\sigma-1\right)\tau\,\theta\left(\sigma-\tau\right)\;.

(5.B.2)

We also list the derivatives of the
DBC
propagator

\displaystyle{}^{\bullet}\!\Delta_{\rm D}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sigma-\theta(\sigma-\tau)

(5.B.3)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}_{\rm D}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\tau-\theta(\tau-\sigma)

(5.B.4)

\displaystyle{}^{\bullet}\!\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}_{\rm D}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=1-\delta(\tau-\sigma)

(5.B.5)

\displaystyle{}^{\bullet\bullet}\!\Delta_{\rm D}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\delta(\tau-\sigma)\;.

(5.B.6)

Concerning the fermionic fields
\psi^{a}_{i}(\tau)
, we list here their propagator

\langle\psi^{a}_{i}(\sigma)\bar{\psi}^{b}_{j}(\tau)\rangle=\delta^{ab}\Delta_{\rm{AF}}\,{}_{ij}(\tau,\sigma)=\delta^{ab}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}F(z,\tau,\sigma)&0\\
0&F(\omega,\tau,\sigma)\\
\end{array}\right)

(5.B.7)

where each entry in the matrix is a
{\cal N}=2
fermionic propagator
[
30
]
defined as

\displaystyle F(z,\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=z^{-(\tau-\sigma)}\left(\frac{1}{z+1}\right)\left(z\,\theta(\tau-\sigma)-\theta(\sigma-\tau)\right)

(5.B.8)

\displaystyle F(\omega,\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\omega^{-(\tau-\sigma)}\left(\frac{1}{\omega+1}\right)\left(\omega\,\theta(\tau-\sigma)-\theta(\sigma-\tau)\right)\ .

(5.B.9)

Next, we have the ghost variable propagators, defined as

\displaystyle\langle a^{\mu}(\tau)a^{\nu}(\sigma)\rangle

\displaystyle=2Tg^{\mu\nu}(x_{0})\Delta_{{}_{\rm gh}}(\tau,\sigma)=2Tg^{\mu\nu}(x_{0})\delta(\tau-\sigma)

(5.B.10)

\displaystyle\langle b^{\mu}(\tau)c^{\nu}(\sigma)\rangle

\displaystyle=-4Tg^{\mu\nu}(x_{0})\Delta_{{}_{\rm gh}}(\tau,\sigma)=-4Tg^{\mu\nu}(x_{0})\delta(\tau-\sigma)\;.

(5.B.11)

As previously stated, the calculation involving these propagators may result in products/derivatives of delta distributions, which are ill-defined, but also divergent quantities such as
\delta(\tau,\tau)
, therefore one needs to regularize the path integral. In the present chapter, we chose worldline dimensional regularization, which consists in continuing the compact time direction with the addition of
d
non-compact extra dimensions, i.e. extending the space
\tau\in[0,1]\rightarrow t^{\alpha}=\left(\tau,\mathbf{t}\right)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. More details can be found for example in Ref.
[
28
]
; here we list the dimensional regularized expression of propagators, exploited during intermediate steps when performing computations. The
d+1
extended propagators read

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm D}(t,s)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{-2}{(\pi m)^{2}+\mathbf{k}^{2}}\sin(\pi m \tau)\sin(\pi m \sigma)\,e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{t}-\mathbf{s})}

(5.B.12)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm gh}(t,s)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}2\sin(\pi m\tau)\sin(\pi m \sigma)e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{t}-\mathbf{s})}=\delta(\tau-\sigma)\delta^{d}(t-s)

(5.B.13)

\displaystyle F(\theta,t,s)

\displaystyle=-i\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\sum_{r\in Z+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2\pi r\gamma^{0}+k\cdot\gamma-\theta}{(2\pi r)^{2}+\mathbf{k}^{2}-\theta^{2}}\,e^{2\pi ir(\tau-\sigma)}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{t}-\mathbf{s})}

(5.B.14)

where in the dimensional regularized expressions one has
\mathbf{t}
as
d
-dimensional vector and
\gamma^{\alpha}
are the Dirac matrices in the
(d+1)
-dimensional extended space. Each extended worldline propagator satisfies a generalization of its own one-dimensional Green equation

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}\Delta_{\rm D}(t,s)

\displaystyle=\delta^{d+1}(t-s)

(5.B.15)

\displaystyle\left(\overset{\rightarrow}{\not{\partial}}+i\theta\right)F(\theta,t,s)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\rm AF}(\tau-\sigma)\delta^{d}(t-s)

(5.B.16)

\displaystyle F(\theta,t,s)\left(-\overset{\leftarrow}{\not{\partial}}+i\theta\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\rm AF}(\tau-\sigma)\delta^{d}(t-s)\ ,

(5.B.17)

where
\delta_{\rm AF}
is the delta distribution acting on antiperiodic functions on
[0,1]
and where a slashed derivative is the usual contraction between derivative and gamma matrices. For computational purposes, the index contractions in
d+1
dimensions serve mostly as a bookkeeping device to keep track of which derivative can be contracted to which vertex to produce the
(d+1)
-dimensional delta function. The delta functions in (
5.B.15
)–(
5.B.17
) are only to be used in
d+1
dimensions; then, by using partial integration one casts the various loop integrals in a form that can be computed by sending
d\to 0
first. At this stage, one can use the one-dimensional propagators (
5.B.2
)–(
5.B.7
), and
\gamma^{0}=1
(with no extra factors arising from the Dirac algebra in
d+1
dimensions).

5.B.2
Analysis of perturbative contributions

In this appendix, we will give the essential details on the evaluation of the path integral average and its subsequent modular integration (
5.1.17
), which produces the Seeley-DeWitt coefficients to be inserted in (
5.1.18
). As anticipated in Section
5.1.3
, in order to find all the possible contributions to order
T^{3}
we have to expand the exponential with the interacting action (
5.1.3
), written more compactly as in (
5.1.21
) which in particular carries the following vertices

\displaystyle S_{\rm K1}=\int d\tau\;\frac{1}{3}R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta}\,q^{\alpha}q^{\beta}\left(\dot{q}^{\mu}\dot{q}^{\nu}+\text{\rm gh}\right)\hskip 5.69046pt,\hskip 5.69046ptS_{\rm C1}=\int d\tau\;\frac{1}{2}R_{\alpha\mu ab}\,\dot{q}^{\mu}q^{\alpha}\,\bar{\psi}^{a}\cdot\psi^{b}\hskip 5.69046pt,

(5.B.18)

\displaystyle DS_{\rm C1}=\int d\tau\;\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{\alpha}R_{\beta\mu ab}\,\dot{q}^{\mu}q^{\alpha}q^{\beta}\,\bar{\psi}^{a}\cdot\psi^{b}\hskip 5.69046pt,\hskip 5.69046ptD^{2}S_{\rm C1}=\int d\tau\;\frac{1}{8}\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta}R_{\gamma\mu ab}\,\dot{q}^{\mu}q^{\alpha}q^{\beta}q^{\gamma}\,\bar{\psi}^{a}\cdot\psi^{b}\hskip 5.69046pt,

(5.B.19)

\displaystyle S_{\rm C2}=\int d\tau\;\frac{1}{24}R^{\tau}{}_{\alpha\beta\mu}R_{\gamma\tau ab}\,\dot{q}^{\mu}q^{\alpha}q^{\beta}q^{\gamma}\,\bar{\psi}^{a}\cdot\psi^{b}\hskip 5.69046pt,\hskip 5.69046ptS_{\rm F}=\int d\tau\;R_{abcd}\,\bar{\psi}^{a}\cdot\psi^{b}\bar{\psi}^{c}\cdot\psi^{d}\hskip 5.69046pt,

(5.B.20)

\displaystyle DS_{\rm F}=\int d\tau\;q^{\alpha}\nabla_{\alpha}R_{abcd}\,\bar{\psi}^{a}\cdot\psi^{b}\bar{\psi}^{c}\cdot\psi^{d}\hskip 5.69046pt,\hskip 5.69046ptD^{2}S_{\rm F}=\int d\tau\;\frac{1}{2}q^{\alpha}q^{\beta}\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta}R_{abcd}\,\bar{\psi}^{a}\cdot\psi^{b}\bar{\psi}^{c}\cdot\psi^{d}\hskip 5.69046pt,

(5.B.21)

\displaystyle S_{\rm V}=-\int d\tau\;\Omega R\ .

(5.B.22)

Through Wick contractions they result in a plethora of different terms, most of which either give rise to disconnected diagrams (so that they are easily taken care of afterward, as connected diagrams exponentiate and there is no need to compute them anew) or can easily be shown to vanish. To start with, we see that the vertex
S_{\rm V}
in (
5.B.22
) does not depend on
q
,
\psi
nor on
\bar{\psi}
, thus it can be taken out of the path integral and it remains exponentiated (as will be all connected worldline diagrams). Let’s see some other illustrative examples. For instance, a disconnected diagram arises from the term

-\frac{1}{8}T\langle S_{\rm K1}S_{\rm F}^{2}\rangle\sim\hbox to133.98pt{\vbox to51.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.54518pt\lower-25.6848pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\sim\text{disconnected}\;.

(5.B.23)

Other diagrams can be shown to be zero exploiting (anti)symmetry of the tensor structures and/or of the resulting propagators, like

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{32T^{2}}\langle S_{\rm K1}^{2}S_{\rm C1}\rangle

\displaystyle\sim R_{\mu\nu ab}\langle\bar{\psi}^{a}\cdot\psi^{b}\rangle=0

(5.B.24)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\langle S_{\rm K1}S_{\rm C1}S_{\rm F}\rangle

\displaystyle\sim R_{ab}R_{\mu\nu}{}^{ab}R_{\alpha\rho\sigma\beta}=0\ .

(5.B.25)

Lastly, others are simply zero after explicitly calculating the integrals, as happens for

\langle S_{\rm C2}S_{\rm F}\rangle
.

We report now the surviving contributions (
5.1.22
) in full glory:

\displaystyle\langle e^{-S_{\rm int}}\big\rangle\Big|_{T^{3}}

\displaystyle=\langle S_{\rm KIN}\rangle

(A)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\frac{1}{96T}R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta}R_{\gamma\lambda ab}R_{\delta\epsilon cd}\int_{012}\langle\dot{q}^{\mu}_{0}\dot{q}^{\nu}_{0}q^{\alpha}_{0}q^{\beta}_{0}\dot{q}^{\lambda}_{1}q^{\gamma}_{1}\dot{q}^{\epsilon}_{2}q^{\delta}_{2}\rangle\langle\bar{\psi}^{a}_{1}\cdot\psi_{1}^{b}\bar{\psi}^{c}_{2}\cdot\psi_{2}^{d}\rangle

(B)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{1}{48}R_{\alpha\mu ab}R^{\tau}{}_{\beta\lambda\nu}R_{\rho\tau cd}\int_{01}\langle\dot{q}^{\mu}_{0}q^{\alpha}_{0}\dot{q}^{\nu}_{1}q^{\beta}_{1}q^{\lambda}_{1}q^{\rho}_{1}\rangle\langle\bar{\psi}^{a}_{0}\cdot\psi_{0}^{b}\bar{\psi}^{c}_{1}\cdot\psi_{1}^{d}\rangle

(C)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hfil\displaystyle\begin{split}\phantom{=}+\frac{1}{18}\nabla_{\alpha}R_{\beta\mu ab}\nabla_{\lambda}R_{\rho\nu cd}\int_{01}\langle\dot{q}^{\mu}_{0}q^{\alpha}_{0}q^{\beta}_{0}\dot{q}^{\nu}_{1}q^{\lambda}_{1}q^{\rho}_{1}\rangle\,\langle\bar{\psi}^{a}_{0}\cdot\psi^{b}_{0}\bar{\psi}^{c}_{1}\cdot\psi^{d}_{1}\rangle\\
\phantom{=}+\frac{1}{16}\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta}R_{\lambda\mu ab}R_{\rho\nu cd}\int_{01}\langle\dot{q}^{\mu}_{0}q^{\alpha}_{0}q^{\beta}_{0}q^{\lambda}_{0}\dot{q}^{\nu}_{1}q^{\rho}_{1}\rangle\,\langle\bar{\psi}^{a}_{0}\cdot\psi^{b}_{0}\bar{\psi}^{c}_{1}\cdot\psi^{d}_{1}\rangle\end{split}\end{aligned}

(D)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{T}{8}R_{\alpha\mu ab}R_{\beta\nu cd}R_{efgh}\int_{012}\langle\dot{q}^{\mu}_{0}\dot{q}^{\nu}_{1}q^{\alpha}_{0}q^{\beta}_{1}\rangle\langle\bar{\psi}^{a}_{0}\cdot\psi^{b}_{0}\bar{\psi}^{c}_{1}\cdot\psi^{d}_{1}\psi^{e}_{2}\cdot\bar{\psi}^{f}_{2}\psi^{g}_{2}\cdot\bar{\psi}^{h}_{2}\rangle

(E)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{1}{6}R_{abcd}R_{efgh}R_{lmno}\int_{012}\langle\bar{\psi}^{a}_{0}\cdot\psi^{b}_{0}\,\bar{\psi}^{c}_{0}\cdot\psi^{d}_{0}\bar{\psi}^{e}_{1}\cdot\psi^{f}_{1}\,\bar{\psi}^{g}_{1}\cdot\psi^{h}_{1}\bar{\psi}^{l}_{2}\cdot\psi^{m}_{2}\,\bar{\psi}^{n}_{2}\cdot\psi^{o}_{2}\rangle

(F)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hfil\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{=}+\frac{T^{2}}{2}\nabla_{\alpha}R_{abcd}\nabla_{\beta}R_{efgh}\int_{01}\langle\dot{q}^{\alpha}_{0}\dot{q}^{\beta}_{1}\rangle\langle\bar{\psi}^{a}_{0}\cdot\psi^{b}_{0}\,\bar{\psi}^{c}_{0}\cdot\psi^{d}_{0}\bar{\psi}^{e}_{1}\cdot\psi^{f}_{1}\,\bar{\psi}^{g}_{1}\cdot\psi^{h}_{1}\rangle\\
&\phantom{=}+\frac{T^{2}}{2}\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta}R_{abcd}R_{efgh}\int_{01}\langle\dot{q}^{\alpha}_{0}\dot{q}^{\beta}_{0}\rangle\langle\bar{\psi}^{a}_{0}\cdot\psi^{b}_{0}\,\bar{\psi}^{c}_{0}\cdot\psi^{d}_{0}\bar{\psi}^{e}_{1}\cdot\psi^{f}_{1}\,\bar{\psi}^{g}_{1}\cdot\psi^{h}_{1}\rangle\ .\end{split}\end{aligned}

(G)

We decided to keep both the ghost fields and the term
S_{\rm KIN}
implicit so as not to further burden the notation, and we combined the two terms of pure spin connection with covariant derivatives
\frac{1}{2}DS_{\rm C1}^{2}+D^{2}S_{\rm C1}S_{\rm C1}
in eq. (
D
) and the two terms of the Taylor expansion of the four-fermions action
\frac{1}{2}T^{2}DS_{\rm F}^{2}+T^{2}D^{2}S_{\rm F}S_{\rm F}
in eq. (
G
), for reasons that will become clear shortly thereafter. Below we illustrate the main steps of the calculation for each contribution.

(A)

The first contribution we called
S_{\rm KIN}
to indicate economically the sum of all terms arising
only
from the pure kinetic part of the interacting action. In the notation previously presented it would read

S_{\rm KIN}=-\frac{1}{384T^{3}}S_{\rm K1}^{3}+\frac{1}{32T^{2}}DS_{\rm K1}^{2}+\frac{S_{\rm K1}}{16T^{2}}\left(S_{\rm K2}+D^{2}S_{\rm K1}\right)-\frac{1}{4T}S_{\rm K3}\ .

(5.B.26)

It has already been computed in Ref.
[
51
]
(eq. 20) and can be read out, translated in our basis (
5.A.13
), from the exponential of connected diagrams as

\langle S_{\rm KIN}\rangle=\frac{T^{3}}{7!}\left(-\frac{16}{9}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{1}}{D^{2}}+\frac{2}{3}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{2}}{D}+\frac{17}{9}\mathcal{E}_{3}+\frac{28}{9}\mathcal{E}_{4}\right)\ .

(5.B.27)

(B)

Regarding the contribution obtained by coupling the bosonic kinetic term and the spin connection, once the contractions have been evaluated, one can use integration by parts and
DR
to reduce integrals to a set of independent ones in
z
, modulo their
\omega
partner.
9
9

9

This should also be understood for forthcoming worldline integrals when the fermionic integrands depend
only
on the Wilson variable
z
.
We list here such integrals

\displaystyle\int_{012}{}^{\bullet}\!\Delta(\tau,\sigma)\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}(\tau,\sigma){}^{\bullet}\!\Delta(\tau,\rho)\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}(\tau,\rho)F(z,\sigma,\rho)F(z,\rho,\sigma)=-\frac{z}{120\left(1+z\right)^{2}}\ ,

(5.B.28)

\displaystyle\int_{012}{}^{\bullet}\!\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}(\tau,\rho){}^{\bullet}\!\Delta(\tau,\tau)\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}(\tau,\sigma)\Delta(\sigma,\rho)F(z,\sigma,\rho)F(z,\rho,\sigma)=-\frac{1}{720}\frac{z}{(z+1)^{2}}\ ,

(5.B.29)

so that the final result for
-\frac{1}{8T}\langle S_{\rm K1}S^{2}_{\rm SC1}\rangle
reads

T^{3}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{2}}{D}\left[-\frac{1}{90}\frac{z}{(z+1)^{2}}-\frac{1}{90}\frac{\omega}{(\omega+1)^{2}}\right]+T^{3}\mathcal{E}_{3}\left[-\frac{z}{60\left(1+z\right)^{2}}-\frac{\omega}{60\left(1+\omega\right)^{2}}\right]\ .

(5.B.30)

(C)

The contribution of pure spin connection has already been evaluated in Section
5.1.3
; we report here the result for completeness:

\langle S_{\rm C1}S_{\rm C2}\rangle=\left(\frac{1}{180}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{2}}{D}+\frac{1}{120}\mathcal{E}_{3}\right)\left[\frac{z}{(z+1)^{2}}+\frac{\omega}{(\omega+1)^{2}}\right]\ .

(5.B.31)

(D)

The contribution of pure spin connection with covariant derivatives is made up of the sum of two pieces: using Einstein manifolds simplifications one can collect the whole result, with only the following diagrams to be evaluated

\displaystyle\int_{01}{}^{\bullet}\!\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}(\sigma,\tau)\Delta(\sigma,\tau)\Delta(\sigma,\tau)F(z,\sigma,\tau)F(z,\tau,\sigma)=\frac{z}{45(z+1)^{2}}\ ,

(5.B.32)

\displaystyle\int_{01}{}^{\bullet}\!\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}(\sigma,\tau)\Delta(\sigma,\tau)\Delta(\sigma,\sigma)F(z,\sigma,\tau)F(z,\tau,\sigma)=\frac{z}{60(z+1)^{2}}\ ,

(5.B.33)

getting the final result

\left\langle\frac{1}{2}DS_{\rm C1}^{2}+D^{2}S_{\rm C1}S_{\rm C1}\right\rangle=-\frac{T^{3}}{360}\left[\frac{z}{(z+1)^{2}}+\frac{\omega}{(\omega+1)^{2}}\right]\left(2\frac{\mathcal{E}_{2}}{D}-\mathcal{E}_{3}+4\mathcal{E}_{4}\right)\ .

(5.B.34)

(E)

This is produced from the coupling of spin connection to the four-fermions vertex. The list of independent diagrams one needs to evaluate is

\displaystyle\int_{012}{}^{\bullet}\!\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}(\sigma,\tau)\Delta(\sigma,\tau)F(z,\rho,\tau)F(z,\tau,\rho)F(z,\rho,\sigma)F(z,\sigma,\rho)=-\frac{z^{2}}{12(z+1)^{4}}\ ,

(5.B.35)

\displaystyle\int_{012}{}^{\bullet}\!\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}(\sigma,\tau)\Delta(\sigma,\tau)F(z,\rho,\tau)F(z,\tau,\sigma)F(z,\sigma,u)F(z,\rho,\rho)=-\frac{z(z-1)^{2}}{48(z+1)^{4}}\ ,

(5.B.36)

\displaystyle\int_{012}{}^{\bullet}\!\Delta(\sigma,\tau)\Delta^{\hskip-1.42262pt\bullet}(\sigma,\tau)F(z,\sigma,\rho)F(z,\rho,\sigma)F(\omega,\tau,\rho)F(\omega,u,\tau)=-\frac{z\omega}{12(z+1)^{2}(\omega+1)^{2}}\ ,

(5.B.37)

such that the final result reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\langle\frac{1}{2}T^{2}S_{\rm C1}^{2}S_{\rm F}\right\rangle&=T^{3}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{2}}{D}\left[\frac{z\left(z-1\right)^{2}}{12\left(z+1\right)^{4}}+\frac{\omega\left(\omega-1\right)^{2}}{12\left(\omega+1\right)^{4}}\right]\\
&\phantom{=}+T^{3}\mathcal{E}_{3}\left[\frac{z^{2}}{12\left(z+1\right)^{4}}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{12\left(\omega+1\right)^{4}}+\frac{z\omega}{3\left(z+1\right)^{2}\left(\omega+1\right)^{2}}\right]\ .\end{split}

(5.B.38)

(F)

The one arising from the pure fermionic vertex is quite tricky to evaluate at once. To simplify the calculation, it is possible to exploit the subtle double copy structure underlying the
\mathcal{N}=4
spinning particle, rewriting this term as a sum of contributions coming from the two copies of the
\mathcal{N}=2
particles, i.e. for the two values of the internal index
i=1,2
. This allows us to rewrite the
above term as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{6}S_{\rm F}^{3}&=\frac{1}{6}S_{1}^{3}(z)+\frac{1}{6}S_{1}^{3}(\omega)+\frac{1}{6}S_{\rm mix}^{3}+\frac{1}{2}S_{1}(z)S_{2}^{2}(\omega)+\frac{1}{2}S_{1}(z)^{2}S_{2}(\omega)\\
&\phantom{=}+\frac{1}{2}S_{1}^{2}(z)S_{\rm mix}+\frac{1}{2}S_{1}(z)S_{\rm mix}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}S_{2}^{2}(\omega)S_{\rm mix}+\frac{1}{2}S_{2}(\omega)S_{\rm mix}^{2}+S_{1}(z)S_{2}(\omega)S_{\rm mix}\ .\end{split}

(5.B.39)

In the previous expression, we defined for simplicity the actions

\displaystyle S_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\int d\tau\,TR_{abcd}\,\bar{\psi}^{1a}\psi_{1}^{b}\bar{\psi}^{1c}\psi_{1}\,^{d}\ ,

(5.B.40)

\displaystyle S_{\rm mix}

\displaystyle=\int d\tau\left(TR_{abcd}\,\bar{\psi}^{a1}\psi^{b}_{1}\bar{\psi}^{2c}\psi^{d}_{2}+1\leftrightarrow 2\right)\ ,

(5.B.41)

where we explicated the flavor index. Once performed the contractions with Mathematica, based on the
xTensor
package
[
170
]
, we further simplify the result by reducing tensor structures using the Bianchi identity as follows

\displaystyle R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\rho\sigma}R^{\alpha\mu\beta\nu}R_{\rho\mu\sigma\nu}=\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{E}_{3}\ ,

(5.B.42)

\displaystyle R_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}R^{\mu\rho\nu\sigma}R^{\alpha}{}_{\sigma}{}^{\beta}{}_{\rho}=-\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{E}_{3}+\mathcal{E}_{4}\ .

(5.B.43)

Once evaluated the fermionic diagrams our final answer for
\langle\frac{1}{6}T^{3}S_{\rm F}^{3}\rangle
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{=+}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{1}}{D^{2}}\left[-\frac{(-1+z)^{2}z(1+(-8+z)z)}{6(1+z)^{6}}-\frac{(-1+\omega)^{2}\omega(1+(-8+\omega)\omega)}{6(1+\omega)^{6}}\right]\\[3.00003pt]
&\phantom{=}+\frac{\mathcal{E}_{2}}{D}\left[-\frac{(z-1)^{2}z^{2}}{(z+1)^{6}}-\frac{(\omega-1)^{2}\omega^{2}}{(\omega+1)^{6}}-\frac{2(-1+z)^{2}z\omega}{(1+z)^{4}(1+\omega)^{2}}-\frac{2z(-1+\omega)^{2}\omega}{(1+z)^{2}(1+\omega)^{4}}\right]\\[3.00003pt]
&\phantom{=}+\mathcal{E}_{3}\left[\frac{z^{4}-2z^{3}+z^{2}}{6(z+1)^{6}}+\frac{\omega^{4}-2\omega^{3}+\omega^{2}}{6(\omega+1)^{6}}-\frac{2z^{2}\omega\ }{(1+z)^{4}(1+\omega)^{2}}-\frac{2z\omega^{2}}{(1+\ z)^{2}(1+\omega)^{4}}\right]\\[3.00003pt]
&\phantom{=}+\mathcal{E}_{4}\left[-\frac{4\omega^{3}}{3(\omega+1)^{6}}-\frac{4z^{3}}{3(z+1)^{6}}-\frac{4\omega z}{3(\omega+1)^{2}(z+1)^{2}(\omega+1)^{2}}\right]\ .\end{split}

(5.B.44)

(G)

Finally, one has the term coming from the Taylor expansion of the four-fermions interaction, made of two pieces. Just as in the (
D
) case, one can collect them into a single one working on the tensorial structure and using Einstein manifolds simplifications. The resulting diagrams to be evaluated are

\displaystyle\int_{01}\Delta(\tau,\tau)F(z,\sigma,\tau)F(z,\sigma,\tau)F(z,\tau,\sigma)F(z,\tau,\sigma)=-\frac{z^{2}}{6(z+1)^{4}}\ ,

(5.B.45)

\displaystyle\int_{01}\Delta(\tau,\tau)F(z,\sigma,\tau)F(z,\tau,\sigma)F(\omega,\sigma,\tau)F(\omega,\tau,\sigma)=-\frac{\omega z}{6(\omega+1)^{2}(z+1)^{2}}\ ,

(5.B.46)

since the partner diagrams
\Delta(\tau,\tau)\rightarrow\Delta(\tau,\sigma)
can easily be seen to be proportional to the above. The final answer for
\langle\frac{1}{2}T^{2}DS_{\rm F}^{2}+T^{2}D^{2}S_{\rm F}S_{\rm F}\rangle
is then

T^{3}\left[\frac{z^{2}}{12\left(z+1\right)^{4}}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{12\left(\omega+1\right)^{4}}+\frac{z\omega}{3\left(z+1\right)^{2}\left(\omega+1\right)^{2}}\right]\left(2\frac{\mathcal{E}_{2}}{D}-\mathcal{E}_{3}+4\mathcal{E}_{4}\right)\ .

(5.B.47)

5.B.3
Summing up all the pieces

We can collect the result of all the computations above as follows

\alpha_{3}(z,\omega,D)=c_{0}(z,\omega)\,\frac{\mathcal{E}_{1}}{D^{2}}+c_{1}(z,\omega)\,\frac{\mathcal{E}_{2}}{D}+c_{2}(z,\omega)\,\mathcal{E}_{3}+c_{3}(z,\omega)\,\mathcal{E}_{4}\ ,

(5.B.48)

with the coefficients

\displaystyle\begin{split}c_{0}(z,\omega)&=-\frac{1}{2835}-\frac{z\left(z^{2}-8z+1\right)(z-1)^{2}}{6(z+1)^{6}}-\frac{(\omega-1)^{2}\omega\left(\omega^{2}-8\omega+1\right)}{6(\omega+1)^{6}}\ ,\end{split}

(5.B.49)

\displaystyle\begin{split}c_{1}(z,\omega)&=\frac{1}{7560}-\frac{z}{90(z+1)^{2}}-\frac{\omega}{90(\omega+1)^{2}}+\frac{z(z-1)^{2}}{12(z+1)^{4}}+\frac{(\omega-1)^{2}\omega}{12(\omega+1)^{4}}+\frac{z^{2}}{6(z+1)^{4}}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{6(\omega+1)^{4}}\\[3.00003pt]
&\phantom{=}-\frac{z^{2}(z-1)^{2}}{(z+1)^{6}}-\frac{(\omega-1)^{2}\omega^{2}}{(\omega+1)^{6}}-\frac{2\omega z(z-1)^{2}}{(\omega+1)^{2}(z+1)^{4}}-\frac{2(\omega-1)^{2}\omega z}{(\omega+1)^{4}(z+1)^{2}}+\frac{2z\omega}{3\left(z+1\right)^{2}\left(\omega+1\right)^{2}}\ ,\end{split}

(5.B.50)

\displaystyle\begin{split}c_{2}(z,\omega)&=\frac{17}{45360}-\frac{z}{180(z+1)^{2}}-\frac{\omega}{180(\omega+1)^{2}}+\frac{(z-1)^{2}z^{2}}{6(z+1)^{6}}+\frac{(\omega-1)^{2}\omega^{2}}{6(\omega+1)^{6}}\\
&\phantom{=}-\frac{2\omega z^{2}}{(\omega+1)^{2}(z+1)^{4}}-\frac{2\omega^{2}z}{(\omega+1)^{4}(z+1)^{2}}\ ,\end{split}

(5.B.51)

\displaystyle\begin{split}c_{3}(z,\omega)&=\frac{1}{1620}-\frac{z}{90(z+1)^{2}}-\frac{\omega}{90(\omega+1)^{2}}+\frac{z^{2}}{3\left(z+1\right)^{4}}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{3\left(\omega+1\right)^{4}}+-\frac{4\omega^{3}}{3(\omega+1)^{6}}-\frac{4z^{3}}{3(z+1)^{6}}\ .\end{split}

(5.B.52)

At this point, in order to get the full
T^{3}
correction, we first need to recall the connected diagrams coming from the first- and second-order expansion of the path integral, namely

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(z,\omega,D)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{5}{12}+\Omega-\frac{z}{(z+1)^{2}}-\frac{\omega}{(\omega+1)^{2}}\right)R\ ,

(5.B.53)

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}(z,\omega,D)

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{1}{180}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{z(z-1)^{2}}{(z+1)^{4}}+\frac{\omega(\omega-1)^{2}}{(\omega+1)^{4}}\right]\right)\frac{R^{2}}{D}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\left(\frac{1}{180}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{\omega^{2}}{(\omega+1)^{4}}+\frac{z^{2}}{(z+1)^{4}}+\frac{4\omega z}{(\omega+1)^{2}(z+1)^{2}}\right]-\frac{1}{12}\left[\frac{\omega}{(\omega+1)^{2}}+\frac{z}{(z+1)^{2}}\right]\right)R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\beta_{1}(z,\omega)\,\frac{R^{2}}{D}+\beta_{2}(z,\omega)\,R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\ ,

(5.B.54)

which are in complete accordance with the results of Refs.
[
37
,
40
]
and indeed reproduce the
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
coefficients (
5.1.30
)–(
5.1.31
). Now we can move to the exponentiation of all the connected diagrams, including the new results. The expectation value
\big\langle e^{-S_{\rm int}}\big\rangle
in (
5.1.17
) can be compactly written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big\langle e^{-S_{\rm int}}\Big\rangle&=\exp[T\,\alpha_{1}\,R+T^{2}\,\left(\beta_{1}\,\frac{R^{2}}{D}+\beta_{2}\,R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}\right)\\
\hfil&\phantom{=}+T^{3}\,\left(c_{0}\,\frac{R^{3}}{D^{2}}+c_{1}\,\frac{R\,R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}}{D}+c_{2}\,R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}+c_{3}\,R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta}R^{\mu\rho\sigma\nu}R_{\rho}{}^{\alpha\beta}{}_{\sigma}\right)+{\cal O}(T^{4})\Bigg]\ .\end{split}

(5.B.55)

The final step consists of Taylor expanding the exponential to the desired order, namely

\Big\langle e^{-S_{\rm int}}\Big\rangle\Big|_{T^{3}}=T^{3}\left[\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}^{3}}{6}+\frac{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}{D}+\frac{c_{0}}{D^{2}}\right)\,\mathcal{E}_{1}+\left(\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}+\frac{c_{1}}{D}\right)\,\mathcal{E}_{2}+c_{2}(z,\omega)\,\mathcal{E}_{3}+c_{3}(z,\omega)\,\mathcal{E}_{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}(T^{4})\ .

(5.B.56)

We obtained our final answer for the path integral average (
5.1.17
). In the end, one is left only with performing the modular integration, i.e. the double expectation value
\langle\hskip-1.42271pt\langle e^{-S_{\rm int}}\rangle\hskip-1.42271pt\rangle
defined in (
5.1.16
). In doing so, we can choose the appropriate measure
P(z,\omega)
to project only on the ghost, graviton, or total coefficients. This gives our final results of Section
5.1.4
, in particular the newly-computed coefficient

\displaystyle a_{3}(D)

\displaystyle=\frac{35D^{4}-147D^{3}-3670D^{2}-13560D-30240}{90720D^{2}}\;\mathcal{E}_{1}+\frac{7D^{3}-230D^{2}+3357D+12600}{15120D}\;\mathcal{E}_{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{17D^{2}-555D-15120}{90720}\;\mathcal{E}_{3}+\frac{D^{2}-39D-1080}{3240}\;\mathcal{E}_{4}\ .

(5.B.57)

Appendix 5.C
Heat kernel computations

The fourth heat kernel coefficient for the ghost field is computed from the general formula (
5.2.10
), by performing the substitutions (
5.2.22
). It is convenient to write

\alpha_{3}^{\rm gh}(x):=\frac{1}{7!}\bm{A}_{\rm gh}[R,R_{\mu\nu},R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}]+\frac{2}{6!}\bm{B}_{\rm gh}[R,R_{\mu\nu},R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma},\Omega_{\mu\nu},V]\ ,

(5.C.1)

where
\bm{A}_{\rm gh}
and
\bm{B}_{\rm gh}
are two (involved) functions of the metric invariants and of the gauge field strength, as reported in (
5.2.10
). Let us start from
\bm{A}_{\rm gh}
: six of the first eight terms vanish identically since they are all proportional to covariant derivatives of
R
,
R_{\mu\nu}
or
R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
, except from the two proportional to
(\nabla_{\alpha}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma})^{2}
and
R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\nabla^{2}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
. Recalling that
{\textrm{Tr}}{[\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}]}=D
and using (
5.A.14
), we are therefore left with

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\textrm{Tr}}\left[\bm{A}_{\rm gh}\right]&=D\left(3R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\nabla^{2}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}-\frac{208}{9}R_{\mu}{}^{\nu}R_{\nu}{}^{\sigma}R_{\sigma}{}^{\mu}+\frac{64}{3}R_{\mu\nu}R_{\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\rho\nu\sigma}-\frac{16}{3}R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu}{}_{\rho\sigma\tau}R^{\nu\rho\sigma\tau}\right.\\[3.00003pt]
&\phantom{=}\left.+\frac{44}{9}R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma}R_{\rho\sigma}{}^{\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{80}{9}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\alpha\rho\beta}R^{\nu}{}_{\alpha}{}^{\sigma}{}_{\beta}\right)\\[3.00003pt]
&=-\frac{16}{9D}\mathcal{E}_{1}+\frac{2}{3}\mathcal{E}_{2}+\frac{17D}{9}\mathcal{E}_{3}+\frac{28D}{9}\mathcal{E}_{4}\ .\end{split}

(5.C.2)

We now compute
\bm{B}_{\rm gh}
. Since
(\Omega_{\mu\nu})^{\rho}{}_{\sigma}=R_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho}{}_{\sigma}
, the second term of
\bm{B}_{\rm gh}
in (
5.2.10
) vanishes identically; moreover, as
V\propto R
, the same occurs for the last four, leaving us with

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\textrm{Tr}}\left[\bm{B}_{\rm gh}\right]&=4{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\Omega_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{2}\Omega^{\mu\nu}\right]}-12{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\Omega_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Omega_{\nu}{}^{\sigma}\Omega_{\sigma}{}^{\mu}\right]}+6{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Omega^{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\rho\sigma}\right]}-4{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R_{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\mu\sigma}\Omega^{\nu}{}_{\sigma}\right]}\\[3.00003pt]
&=-\frac{4}{D}\mathcal{E}_{2}-2\mathcal{E}_{3}-4\mathcal{E}_{4}\ ,\end{split}

(5.C.3)

where all the traces are computed by explicit substitution, according to (
5.2.22
),

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\Omega_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Omega_{\nu}{}^{\sigma}\Omega_{\sigma}{}^{\mu}\right]}

\displaystyle={\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R^{\alpha\ \ \nu}_{\ \beta\mu}R^{\beta\ \ \sigma}_{\ \gamma\nu}R^{\gamma\ \ \mu}_{\ \delta\sigma}\right]}=R^{\alpha\ \ \nu}_{\ \beta\mu}R^{\beta\ \ \sigma}_{\ \gamma\nu}R^{\gamma\ \ \mu}_{\ \alpha\sigma}=-\mathcal{E}_{4}\ ,

(5.C.4)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Omega^{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\rho\sigma}\right]}

\displaystyle=R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R^{\alpha\ \mu\nu}_{\ \beta}R^{\beta\ \rho\sigma}_{\ \gamma}\right]}=R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\alpha\ \mu\nu}_{\ \beta}R^{\beta\ \rho\sigma}_{\ \alpha}=-\mathcal{E}_{3}\ ,

(5.C.5)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R_{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\mu\sigma}\Omega^{\nu}{}_{\sigma}\right]}

\displaystyle=R_{\mu\nu}{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R^{\alpha\ \mu\sigma}_{\ \beta}R^{\beta\ \nu}_{\ \gamma\ \sigma}\right]}=R_{\mu\nu}R^{\alpha\ \mu\sigma}_{\ \beta}R^{\beta\ \nu}_{\ \alpha\ \sigma}=-\frac{1}{D}\mathcal{E}_{2}\ .

(5.C.6)

Going back to (
5.C.1
) we conclude that

{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\alpha_{3}^{\rm gh}(x)\right]}=-\frac{1}{2835D}\mathcal{E}_{1}+\frac{D-84}{7560D}\mathcal{E}_{2}+\frac{17D-252}{45360}\mathcal{E}_{3}+\frac{D-18}{1620}\mathcal{E}_{4}\ ,

(5.C.7)

which however does not provide the full coefficient for the ghost, since we still have to add the term
\beta_{3}
defined in (
5.2.6
), which turns out to be

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{3}^{\rm gh}&=\frac{1}{6}\left(\alpha_{1}^{\rm gh}\right)^{3}+\alpha_{1}^{gh}\alpha_{2}^{\rm gh}\\
&=\frac{1}{6}\left[\delta^{\tau}_{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{D}\right)R\right]^{3}+\left[\delta^{\tau}_{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{D}\right)R\right]\left[\frac{1}{180}\left(R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}-R_{\mu\nu}^{2}\right)\delta^{\alpha}_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{12}\Omega_{\mu\nu}^{2}\right]\ ,\end{split}

(5.C.8)

and taking the trace

{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\beta_{3}^{\rm gh}(x)\right]}=\frac{(5D^{2}+54D+180)(D+6)}{6480D^{2}}\mathcal{E}_{1}+\frac{(D+6)(D-15)}{1080D}\mathcal{E}_{2}\ .

(5.C.9)

The overall result for the ghost is

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[a_{3}^{\rm gh}(x)\right]}&=\frac{35D^{3}+588D^{2}+3512D+7560}{45360D^{2}}\ \mathcal{E}_{1}\\[5.0pt]
&\phantom{=}+\frac{7D^{2}-62D-714}{7560D}\mathcal{E}_{2}+\frac{17D-252}{45360}\mathcal{E}_{3}+\frac{D-18}{1620}\mathcal{E}_{4}\end{split}

(5.C.10)

and corresponds to (
5.1.42
). To compute the fourth heat kernel coefficient for the graviton, we start again from the general formula (
5.2.10
) and perform the substitutions (
5.2.23
). It is once again more time convenient to split

\alpha_{3}^{\rm gr}(x):=\frac{1}{7!}\bm{A}_{\rm gr}[R,R_{\mu\nu},R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}]+\frac{2}{6!}\bm{B}_{\rm gr}[R,R_{\mu\nu},R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma},\Omega_{\mu\nu},V]

(5.C.11)

as we did in (
5.C.1
) for the ghost. By inspecting more closely the substitution rules (
5.2.22
) and (
5.2.23
), though, it is clear that
\bm{A}_{\rm gr}
and
\bm{A}_{\rm gh}
differ only by the trace of the identity operator
\mathbbm{1}
. Using (
5.C.2
), we find in particular

{\textrm{Tr}}\left[\bm{A}_{\rm gr}\right]=\frac{D+1}{2}\,{\textrm{Tr}}\left[\bm{A}_{\rm gh}\right]=(D+1)\left(-\frac{8}{9D}\mathcal{E}_{1}+\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{E}_{2}+\frac{17D}{18}\mathcal{E}_{3}+\frac{14D}{9}\mathcal{E}_{4}\right)\ ,

(5.C.12)

while for the computation of
\bm{B}_{\rm gr}
we can repeat the previous observations, and consider the only non-vanishing terms

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\textrm{Tr}}\left[\bm{B}_{\rm gr}\right]&=4{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\Omega_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{2}\Omega^{\mu\nu}\right]}-12{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\Omega_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Omega_{\nu}{}^{\sigma}\Omega_{\sigma}{}^{\mu}\right]}\\[3.00003pt]
&\phantom{=}+6{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Omega^{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\rho\sigma}\right]}-4{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R_{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\mu\sigma}\Omega^{\nu}{}_{\sigma}\right]}+30{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[(\nabla_{\mu}V)^{2}\right]}\\[5.0pt]
&=-\frac{4(D+47)}{D}\ \mathcal{E}_{2}-2(D-43)\ \mathcal{E}_{3}-4(D+92)\ \mathcal{E}_{4}\end{split}

(5.C.13)

where we need to compute the traces

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\Omega_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Omega_{\nu}{}^{\sigma}\Omega_{\sigma}{}^{\mu}\right]}

\displaystyle=-\left(D+2\right)\mathcal{E}_{4}\ ,

(5.C.14)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Omega^{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\rho\sigma}\right]}

\displaystyle=-(D+2)\ \mathcal{E}_{3}\ ,

(5.C.15)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[R_{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\mu\sigma}\Omega^{\nu}{}_{\sigma}\right]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{D+2}{D}\ \mathcal{E}_{2}\ ,

(5.C.16)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\Omega_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{2}\Omega^{\mu\nu}\right]}

\displaystyle=-(D+2)\left(\frac{2}{D}\mathcal{E}_{2}-\mathcal{E}_{3}+4\mathcal{E}_{4}\right)\ ,

(5.C.17)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[(\nabla_{\alpha}\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma})^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle=-3\left(\frac{2}{D}\mathcal{E}_{2}-\mathcal{E}_{3}+4\mathcal{E}_{4}\right)\ .

(5.C.18)

Going back to (
5.C.11
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\alpha_{3}^{\rm gr}(x)\right]}&=-\frac{D+1}{5670D}\ \mathcal{E}_{1}+\frac{D^{2}-167D-7896}{15120D}\ \mathcal{E}_{2}\\[5.0pt]
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{17D^{2}-487D+21672}{90720}\ \mathcal{E}_{3}+\frac{D^{2}-35D-3312}{3240}\ \mathcal{E}_{4}\ .\end{split}

(5.C.19)

The last step is to compute the term
\beta_{3}
defined in (
5.2.6
), which is the sum of the two terms:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}\left(\alpha_{1}^{\rm gr}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{1}{216}R^{3}\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{12}R^{2}\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}R\,\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \rho\sigma}\mathcal{V}_{\rho\sigma}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}+\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \rho\sigma}\mathcal{V}_{\rho\sigma}^{\ \ \ \lambda\tau}\mathcal{V}_{\lambda\tau}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\right)\ ,

(5.C.20)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{\rm gr}\alpha_{2}^{\rm gr}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{6}R\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}+\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\right)\left[\frac{1}{180}\left(R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}-\frac{1}{D}R^{2}\right)\delta_{\alpha\beta}^{\ \ \ \lambda\tau}+\frac{1}{12}\left(\Omega_{\rho\sigma}^{2}\right)_{\alpha\beta}^{\ \ \ \lambda\tau}\right]+\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha\beta}^{\ \ \ \lambda\tau}\ ,

where the following traces are to be computed:

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}D(D+1)\ ,

(5.C.21)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\right]}

\displaystyle=-R\ ,

(5.C.22)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \rho\sigma}\mathcal{V}_{\rho\sigma}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\right]}

\displaystyle=3R^{2}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\ ,

(5.C.23)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \rho\sigma}\mathcal{V}_{\rho\sigma}^{\ \ \ \lambda\tau}\mathcal{V}_{\lambda\tau}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\right]}

\displaystyle=-8\mathcal{E}_{4}-\mathcal{E}_{3}\ ,

(5.C.24)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\left(\Omega_{\rho\sigma}^{2}\right)_{\alpha\beta}^{\ \ \ \lambda\tau}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{D}\mathcal{E}_{2}+3\mathcal{E}_{3}\ ,

(5.C.25)

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\mathcal{V}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha\beta}^{\ \ \ \lambda\tau}\right]}

\displaystyle=3\left(\frac{2}{D}\mathcal{E}_{2}-\mathcal{E}_{3}+4\mathcal{E}_{4}\right)\ ,

(5.C.26)

leading to

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[\beta_{3}^{\rm gr}(x)\right]}&=\frac{5D^{3}-D^{2}-186D+72}{12960D}\ \mathcal{E}^{3}_{1}\\[5.0pt]
&\phantom{=}+\frac{D^{3}-29D^{2}+468D+2520}{2160D}\ \mathcal{E}_{2}^{3}-\frac{5}{12}\mathcal{E}_{3}^{3}+\frac{2}{3}\mathcal{E}_{4}^{3}\ .\end{split}

(5.C.27)

In the end,

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[a_{3}^{\rm gr}(x)\right]}&=\frac{35D^{3}-7D^{2}-1318D+488}{90720D}\ \mathcal{E}_{1}+\frac{7D^{3}-202D^{2}+3109D+9744}{15120D}\ \mathcal{E}_{2}\\[5.0pt]
&\phantom{=}+\frac{17D^{2}-487D-16128}{90720}\ \mathcal{E}_{3}+\frac{D^{2}-35D-1152}{3240}\ \mathcal{E}_{4}\ ,\end{split}

(5.C.28)

which corresponds to (
5.1.46
). By summing the ghost and graviton coefficients according to (
5.2.28
), we end up with

\displaystyle{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[a_{3}(x)\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{35D^{4}-147D^{3}-3670D^{2}-13560D-30240}{90720D^{2}}\ \mathcal{E}_{1}+\frac{7D^{3}-230D^{2}+3357D+12600}{15120D}\ \mathcal{E}_{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{17D^{2}-555D-15120}{90720}\ \mathcal{E}_{3}+\frac{D^{2}-39D-1080}{3240}\ \mathcal{E}_{4}\ ,

(5.C.29)

which corresponds to (
5.1.32
).

Appendix 5.D
Topological terms in even dimensions

In order to display the cancellation of the one-loop divergences of pure gravity in four spacetime dimensions, and analogously their resilience in six, we exploit the Chern–Gauss–Bonnet theorem, which allows us to compute the Euler character
\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})
of a manifold
\mathcal{M}
as a volume integral of the 2-form
\mathcal{R}^{\mu\nu}:=R^{\mu\nu}_{\ \ \ \alpha\beta}\,\mathrm{d}{x}^{\alpha}\wedge\mathrm{d}{x}^{\beta}
, namely

\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\dots\mu_{d}\nu_{d}}\mathcal{R}^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathcal{R}^{\mu_{d}\nu_{d}}\ ,

(5.D.1)

where
D:=2d
is the dimension of the manifold, assumed here to be even.
10
10

10

If
D
is odd, the integral (
5.D.1
) vanishes, so that the theorem does not provide a useful way of computing
\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})
.
In local coordinates, (
5.D.1
) becomes

\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})=\frac{1}{2(4\pi)^{d}}\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{D}x}\sqrt{g}\ \frac{D!}{D}\delta^{\alpha_{1}}_{[\mu_{1}}\delta^{\beta_{1}}_{\nu_{1}}\dots\delta^{\alpha_{d}}_{\mu_{d}}\delta^{\beta_{d}}_{\nu_{d}]}R^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}_{\ \ \ \ \ \alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\dots R^{\mu_{d}\nu_{d}}_{\ \ \ \ \ \alpha_{d}\beta_{d}}\ .

(5.D.2)

It is possible to prove that
\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})
defined in this way does not depend on the metric settled upon
\mathcal{M}
, and is fixed only by the global topology of the manifold. For
D=2
(
d=1
), (
5.D.2
) becomes

\left.\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})\right|_{D=2}=\frac{1}{16\pi}\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{2}x}\sqrt{g}\ R\ ,

(5.D.3)

which is proportional to the Einstein–Hilbert action, while at
D=4
(
d=2
) the Euler character reads

\left.\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})\right|_{D=4}=\frac{1}{32\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{4}x}\sqrt{g}\ \left(R^{2}-4R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}+R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\right)\ .

(5.D.4)

On Einstein spaces the first two terms in the integrand of (
5.D.4
) cancel off, and therefore we are left with

\left.\chi_{E}(\mathcal{M})\right|_{D=4}=\frac{1}{32\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{4}x}\sqrt{g}\ R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}:=\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{4}x}\sqrt{g}\ E_{4}\ .

(5.D.5)

Therefore, the third heat kernel coefficient
{\textrm{Tr}}{\left[a_{2}(x)\right]}\propto R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{2}
is proportional to the Euler density
E_{4}
and hence is a total derivative, which can be neglected in the effective action. This result is no more true when
\Lambda\neq 0
, even if we drop the total derivative term corresponding to Euler density.

In dimension
D=6
(
d=3
) the Euler character is
[
182
]

\left.\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})\right|_{D=6}=\frac{1}{384\pi^{3}}\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{6}x}\sqrt{g}\ \left(4\mathcal{K}_{1}-48\mathcal{K}_{2}+64\mathcal{K}_{4}+96\mathcal{K}_{5}+12\mathcal{K}_{3}-96\mathcal{K}_{6}+16\mathcal{K}_{7}-32\mathcal{K}_{8}\right)\ ,

(5.D.6)

which on Einstein spaces reduces to

\left.\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})\right|_{D=6}=\frac{1}{384\pi^{3}}\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{6}x}\sqrt{g}\ \left(\frac{4}{9}\mathcal{E}_{1}-4\mathcal{E}_{2}+16\mathcal{E}_{3}+32\mathcal{E}_{4}\right)\ .

(5.D.7)

The condition
\Lambda=0
, that is
R=0
, forces
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{E}_{2}=0
, so that (
5.D.7
) eventually becomes

\left.\chi_{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})\right|_{D=6}=\frac{1}{384\pi^{3}}\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{6}x}\sqrt{g}\ \left(16\mathcal{E}_{3}+32\mathcal{E}_{4}\right):=\int\mathrm{d}{{}^{6}x}\sqrt{g}\ E_{6}\ .

(5.D.8)

This shows that, up to a total derivative term

2\mathcal{E}_{4}=-\mathcal{E}_{3}\ .

(5.D.9)

Thus, at dimension
D=6
, even with
\Lambda=0
, the perturbative quantum gravity effective action is not free of logarithmic divergences.

Chapter 6
Heat kernel coefficients in massive gravity

In this chapter, we employ the massive model developed in Chapter
4
to carry out a perturbative computation in massive gravity. Specifically, we evaluate the counterterms required for the renormalization of the one-loop effective action of linearized massive gravity. As a preliminary step, we construct the worldline path integral on the one-dimensional torus, while projecting onto the desired degrees of freedom. Once this is achieved, we obtain the correct expression for the one-loop divergences in arbitrary spacetime dimension
D
. The central result of this chapter is the determination of the Seeley–DeWitt coefficient
a_{3}(D)
, which, to the best of our knowledge, had not been previously computed in the literature.

Conventions

Whether we work in Minkowski or Euclidean signature, and in which spacetime dimension, will be specified as needed throughout the text for convenience.

6.1
Extracting degrees of freedom

In this section, the quantization on the circle of the free massive
\mathcal{N}=4
spinning particle with various gaugings of the SO(4) R-symmetry is analyzed. The aim is to determine how the path integral extracts physical degrees of freedom from the Hilbert space. Once this is established, it will be possible to isolate specifically the degrees of freedom associated with the massive graviton.

To set the notation, we summarize here the key features of the model. The graded phase space of the
massless

\mathcal{N}=4
supersymmetric worldline model consists of bosonic
(x^{M},p_{M})
and fermionic
(\Xi^{M}_{I})
coordinates, where
M=0,\dots,D
is a spacetime vector index and
I=1,2,3,4
is a SO(4) internal index. The target space is a
(D+1)-
dimensional Minkowski spacetime
\mathcal{M}_{D+1}
and
t
denotes a parameter that labels positions along the worldline, which is embedded in spacetime by the functions
x^{M}(t)
. The phase space action is given by

S=\int dt\left[p_{M}\dot{x}^{M}+\frac{i}{2}\,\Xi_{M}^{I}\dot{\Xi}^{M}_{I}-\frac{e}{2}\,\mathcal{H}-i\,\mathcal{X}^{I}Q_{I}\right]\ ,

(6.1.1)

with
(e,\mathcal{X})
being a one-dimensional supergravity multiplet enforcing the first-class constraints
(\mathcal{H},Q)
that generate through Poisson brackets the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
superalgebra on the worldline

\{Q_{I},Q_{J}\}_{\mathrm{PB}}=-2i\,\delta_{IJ}\,\mathcal{H}\ ,\quad\{Q_{I},\mathcal{H}\}_{\mathrm{PB}}=0\ .

(6.1.2)

The latter algebra is computed by using the graded Poisson brackets of the phase space coordinates
\{x^{M},p_{N}\}_{\mathrm{PB}}=\delta^{M}_{N}
and
\{\Xi_{M}^{I},\Xi^{N}_{J}\}_{\mathrm{PB}}=-i\delta_{M}^{N}\delta^{I}_{J}
, fixed by the symplectic term of the action
[
27
]
. The constraints
\mathcal{H}:=p^{2}
and
Q_{I}:=\Xi_{I}^{M}p_{M}
must be introduced to ensure the mass-shell condition and to remove unphysical degrees of freedom, eliminating negative norm states. From the latter higher-dimensional theory, the lower-dimensional
massive
model is derived as follows. First, it is convenient to take complex combinations of the original four real Grassmann variables
\Xi^{M}_{I}(t)
and the four gravitinos
\mathcal{X}^{I}(t)
as follows (
i=1,2
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi^{M}_{i}&:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\Xi^{M}_{i}+i\,\Xi^{M}_{i+2})\ ,\quad\bar{\xi}^{Mi}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\Xi^{M}_{i}-i\,\Xi^{M}_{i+2})\ ,\\
\chi_{i}&:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathcal{X}_{i}+i\,\mathcal{X}_{i+2})\ ,\quad\bar{\chi}^{i}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathcal{X}_{i}-i\,\mathcal{X}_{i+2})\ .\end{split}

(6.1.3)

Then, employing the so-called Scherk–Schwarz mechanism
[
222
]
, the model is dimensionally reduced on a flat spacetime of the form
\mathcal{M}_{D}\times S^{1}
. In practice, one gauges the compact direction
x^{D}
, corresponding to
S^{1}
, by imposing the first-class constraint
p_{D}=m
while setting
